# Dr. Jack Kruse — Articles

Dr. Jack Kruse's theories as he explains them, paraphrased from his own forum posts of Apr 11 – Oct 10, 2026; where his account shifted, the latest and fullest version is used.
Each article presents his reasoning in his terms and cites the forum posts it draws on; the app shows these as his views, not as assessed claims.

## Core model

### Mitochondria as light- and field-driven electronics
- **id:** mitochondria-quantum-devices
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse treats each mitochondrion as an antenna, circuit and particle accelerator tuned by sunlight and Earth's magnetic field, not just a chemical furnace that burns food.
- **protocols:** none

Textbooks picture mitochondria, the tiny power units inside our cells, as furnaces that burn food. Kruse argues they are better understood as electronics: antennas and circuits tuned to sunlight and to Earth's magnetic field. In his model, whether this circuit works decides almost everything else, from the state of the cell's water to how it handles oxygen, and so health or disease.

#### The Sun's receiver

Kruse links the mitochondrion to the Sun through what he calls "Optico-Magnetic Engineering." In his view both are low-energy "quantum synchrotrons" whose output depends on tunneling, the quantum trick by which a particle slips through an energy barrier it could not climb over. In the Sun, hydrogen nuclei tunnel through their mutual repulsion to fuse; in the cell, electrons taken from food tunnel between the protein complexes of the respiratory chain. He therefore sees the mitochondrion as a wireless receiver tuned to the Sun–Earth circuit, not a digestion vat.

He calls the fine-structure constant, the number of about 1/137 that Richard Feynman described as physics' great mystery, the guiding star of his thesis. It sets how strongly light grips matter. In Kruse's reading, that grip decides whether a photon of sunlight ends up as a moving electron, which is life, or as waste heat.

He sums up his whole thesis as two established ideas joined together: conservation of angular momentum (spin cannot simply appear or vanish) and Rudolph Marcus's theory of how fast electrons jump between molecules. In his account this means electron flow depends on spin and on the shape of the surrounding field, not only on energy.

The link between the two is CISS, short for chiral-induced spin selectivity. Kruse explains that proteins built with one "handedness" (chirality) act as spin filters, letting through electrons that spin one way and tying each electron's forward motion to its spin. Earth's field keeps this filter aligned, an effect he calls the "Zeeman handshake." Borrowing the radical-pair idea used to explain bird navigation, he says a weak field does not force a reaction; it tips the balance between spin states, and the cell amplifies the nudge.

Kruse faults standard biochemistry for treating molecules as billiard balls when each is also a tiny magnet. In healthy enzymes such as cytochrome c oxidase, he says, electron pairs stay coherent, their spins moving in step, and they cross between two spin states: singlet, which in his usage burns energy as heat, and triplet, which carries signals. That crossing is where, in his view, a cell chooses repair or burning. Because spin rather than heat gates the step, he argues the coherence survives the constant jostling at body temperature. He finds it ironic that doctors diagnose with MRI, a machine built on coherent spin, and then prescribe drugs that ignore it.

He gives one direct example of the field tuning output. Magnesium-25, a form of magnesium with a magnetic nucleus, couples through the radical-pair effect to the electrons that drive ATP production, and he says this can raise output up to about 2.5-fold. He reads reports that ATP is made about 2.5 times faster in Earth's field than in a field-free space as the reason Mars is lifeless and deserts hold less life than rainforests.

#### The parts as circuit components

Kruse describes the inner membrane as so thin that it carries roughly 30 million volts per meter of electric field, which he likens to a lightning bolt. Cardiolipin, a cone-shaped fat in that membrane, is the insulating sleeve that holds the respiratory complexes close together. It keeps them within 14 ångströms (1.4 billionths of a meter), the tunneling limit he credits to Dutton.

By the standards of ordinary materials, he says, that field should spark straight through: air breaks down at about 3 million volts per meter, and the plastics that insulate high-voltage cables fail at around 20 to 30 million. The membrane survives because cytochrome c oxidase, the last enzyme of the electron chain, coats it in a layer of structured water low in deuterium (heavy hydrogen), which insulates far better than ordinary water. A single deuterium nucleus touching that field would, in his account, bridge it and cause an "isotopic flashover"; he gives this as the reason red blood cells carry neither a nucleus nor mitochondria. Melanin near these zones acts as a Faraday cage, an electrical shield that soaks up stray fields.

At the tips of the cristae (the membrane's inward folds), cardiolipin gathers negative charge. In his model this focuses a wave of protons the way the undersea canyon at Nazaré, Portugal, focuses ocean swells into giant waves. He calls it the "Biological Nazaré" and says it drives ATP synthase, the rotary motor that makes the cell's energy currency, toward 9,000 revolutions per minute. He also proposes that the energy packed into these tips allows "biological LENR" (low-energy nuclear reactions), such as turning copper-63 into copper-65, which he says clears isotopic waste at body temperature.

He adds that the junction where each crista meets the rest of the membrane has the curved profile of a brachistochrone, the path along which a falling object arrives fastest. In his view that shape turns the membrane's voltage into maximum proton speed with little loss as heat, and the field then drives protons through ATP synthase fast enough to tunnel. That makes the mitochondrion, for Kruse, less a power plant than a particle accelerator built to shorten each proton's trip.

Kruse builds this electronic picture on earlier scientists. Albert Szent-Györgyi proposed in 1941 that proteins are semiconductors. John Bardeen's transistor and his theory of superconductivity, in Kruse's reading, supply the working mechanisms, and Robert O. Becker built his map of the body's direct-current healing signals on Bardeen's work. In this picture cristae act as transistors switched by neuropsin (a sensor for dawn UV-A light), and the structured water at the membrane acts as a room-temperature superconductor.

He also credits Soviet mathematician Yuri Manin, whose 1980 book *Computable and Noncomputable* proposed quantum computing before Feynman did, and arrived there through biology. Manin noted that building a protein from a gene's message resembles a machine copying tape, and judged classical physics far too slow to explain how efficiently cells do it. Kruse concludes that the ribosome, the cell's protein builder, must work as a quantum device, kept coherent by the clean proton current from the mitochondria.

He extends the semiconductor comparison to chemistry. The chemical tags a cell adds to its proteins are, in his view, nature's controlled "doping," the deliberate impurities engineers use to tune silicon. Foreign chemicals are uncontrolled "rogue dopants" that trap electrons; he names statins, synthetic peptides and lithium.

#### Two kinds of resistance

Kruse says the circuit needs two opposite properties at once. The membrane must insulate well, like the wall of a capacitor holding charge, while the electron chain must conduct easily, like a wire. He maps this onto Martin Picard's "energy resistance" idea: health sits in a just-right zone with enough resistance to turn electron flow into useful work, but not so much that energy is wasted as heat.

Harms come in two matching kinds in his model. Some jam the wire: loss of the magnetic anchor, nnEMF (his term for man-made wireless and electrical fields), light at night, and deuterium, heavy metals, calcium, statins and peptides acting as electron traps. Others short the capacitor: he names spike protein, lipid nanoparticles, and the heat released when the wire jams, which melts the membrane's structured water.

#### One network, ancient roots

Kruse says mitochondria are not isolated units but one wired network. The junctions between them act as a "bus bar," an electrician's term for a shared power rail, so a failure in one tissue spreads body-wide. Tissues also stay in sync wirelessly, he says, through terahertz signals that cross 20–50-nanometer gaps.

He finds a model for what that network can do in a 2026 Nature Communications paper by Watanabe and Takagi, which showed how to draw the most work from many copies of an unknown quantum state. Kruse treats the body's many mitochondria as those copies, with the cristae and their iron–sulfur clusters sorting the signal and melanin reading it. Together, he says, they can turn chaotic radiation from a faltering field into a coherent signal, harvesting the disturbance rather than merely enduring it, with deuterium-poor water and coherent faint light as the useful output.

He traces this design to life's origins. Building on Lynn Margulis's account of mitochondria as once free-living bacteria, he argues they are photosynthesis machinery run in reverse. That, in his view, is why light therapy works by resonance, reminding the machinery of its original job, rather than acting like a drug.

He describes the merger that made complex cells as a "chiral handshake": an ancient motor built on iron–sulfur clusters for stability under harsh UV, set inside a fluid cell body built for agility. The result, in his account, is an inner membrane that behaves as a topological insulator, a material that blocks current through its bulk yet conducts along its surface. When the field shifts, he says, the handshake slips, oxidative stress begins and the alignment of the cristae is the first thing lost.

Kruse treats the iron cores of ferritin (the body's iron-storage protein) and of the cytochromes as "ferrons." These are tiny magnetic compass needles and capacitors that, when a person is unpinned (living without a stable local field, grounding and sun), lose their bearing. They then leak iron and turn stray fields into heat.

Finally, he reads a mitochondrion's shape as a live record of its surroundings: swelling, fragmented cristae and donut shapes are the organelle reshaping itself to survive electrical, magnetic or isotopic stress, not a separate disease.

In practice, Kruse concludes that this is a hardware problem set by the environment. He says the circuit is restored by full-spectrum sunlight, grounding and a stable local magnetic field. He advises against treating a field problem with added chemicals such as statins, synthetic peptides, insulin or GLP-1 drugs, which in his model only add more electron traps.

### The field, the genes and the clock in the cell
- **id:** field-genes-telomeres
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** In his model the environment sets the physics, the physics sets how genes behave, and DNA carries physical "mass" that light and the daily clock must keep low.
- **protocols:** none

Kruse turns the usual view of genes as the body's blueprint upside down. In his model the environment comes first: it sets the physics inside the cell, and that physics decides how genes behave. This matters to him because it means gene tests and "biological age" clocks measure late effects, while the causes lie in light, magnetism and water.

#### Environment first, genes last

Kruse reads Nature's 2024 essay arguing that genes are not the blueprint for life as mainstream biology catching up with him. He adds that it still misses the real cause: deuterium (heavy hydrogen) and the physics of the cell's water. In his view the nuclear genome is a passive library, and the slow drift of chemical marks on DNA (epigenetic drift) simply follows damage done by the field.

He finds a vivid example in Lydia Fairchild, whose blood DNA did not match her children's because she had absorbed a twin in the womb, making her a chimera (one person carrying two genetically distinct cell lines). Kruse calls her a "nuclear chimera but mitochondrial monolith": both cell lines ran on the same maternal mitochondria, so her body stayed coherent. Two mitochondrial populations filtering deuterium at different speeds would, he argues, set off an autoimmune "civil war" or cancer, which is why evolution does not allow it. For him the case shows that the self is defined by the mitochondrial field, with the nucleus a secondary parts library.

He reads a 2025 npj Aging study from Emory and Baylor the same way. Psilocin, the active form of the mushroom compound psilocybin, lengthened the dividing life of human skin and fetal lung cells by roughly half and raised survival in aged mice from 50% to 80%. The authors credited SIRT1 and telomeres; Kruse says the gene changes sit downstream of a recharged inner mitochondrial membrane. He notes that psilocybin shifts gene activity in the brain's visual area much as light does, even in darkness, and treats it as a chemical stand-in for sunlight. His proposed mechanism is deuterium: treated cells, he suggests, deliver deuterium-depleted water to ATP synthase more efficiently, sparing the motor the drag of heavy hydrogen. Because many mushrooms use melanin to harvest ionizing radiation for energy, the molecule itself may come "pre-depleted" of deuterium. He says mouse studies don't impress him but this one deserves human trials, and he offers a test: if he is right, treated cells should show a blue shift (more coherence) in their ultra-weak photon emission and a higher dielectric constant in their water.

He stresses that DNA is paramagnetic (weakly drawn to a magnet) when wet, though not when dry. Gene activity, he says, therefore follows the strength and direction of the surrounding magnetic field, which he calls "electromagnetic terroir." In his telling, this is why ancient peoples sheltered in paramagnetic stone such as basalt and granite when the field weakened. He also points to Russian researcher Pjotr Gariaev's "DNA phantom," a laser-scattering pattern reported to linger for weeks after the DNA sample was removed, as a sign that DNA answers to the field around it.

#### Telomeres as a record of disorder

Telomeres are the protective caps on the ends of chromosomes. Kruse adopts the TRCS (Telomere/rDNA Co-Regulation) model, from a 2026 paper he credits to Huang. It holds that the physical shortening of telomeres and of other repeated DNA stretches drives cell aging by raising p53, the protein that guards the genome. Epigenetic drift is only a passenger, which he says is why attempts to reset those marks give only temporary results.

He links this to a 2026 Shanghai study by Song and colleagues, which found a 71-terahertz vibration in mitochondria that disappears when they are dried or disrupted. Kruse reads it as a timing signal sent from mitochondria to the nucleus that paces telomeres and gene reading while the membrane holds its charge. When the cell's structured water collapses (from his healthy "160" state to ordinary "78" water), electrons slow, the signal goes quiet and the nucleus "goes blind." Telomeres, in his view, therefore record entropy (accumulated disorder), not age.

He finds support in the 2024 sequencing of the South American lungfish, whose genome is about 30 times larger than ours with roughly the same number of genes. Kruse argues genome size reflects how efficient a species' "water battery" is. A slow, low-voltage animal piles up non-coding DNA as ballast that soaks up electromagnetic noise and sheds heat. Humans get by with a lean genome because high-capacitance water and light sensors keep that battery charged. Indoor life under screens, at high latitude and under a weakening field, he says, makes humans run like the lungfish without its buffer, so telomeres shorten, men lose their Y chromosome in blood cells, and cells fall back on fermentation.

#### Time-stamping

The cell's daily clock is a loop in which the CLOCK and BMAL1 genes switch on the Per and Cry genes, whose proteins return about every 24 hours to switch themselves off. Kruse stresses that this loop also decides *when* proteins get their chemical tags (phosphate, methyl, acetyl and ubiquitin groups); he puts the share of gene products tagged on a daily rhythm at 5–10%. He calls this "time-stamping."

He describes the clock's own proteins, Period and Cryptochrome, as semiconductors and "magnetic buffer tanks" built to hold a charge from Earth's field. The whole loop, in his view, is a governor that keeps the spin of ATP synthase (the cell's energy motor) in step with the planet's core field, and it works best near agonic lines, where compass north equals true north.

The loop's built-in delays are, in his account, speed limits set by how thick the cell's water is. In deuterium-heavy "78" water the clock proteins travel more slowly to the nucleus, and the cycle stretches from 24 hours to 25 or 26. He gives this as the physical root of insomnia and the familiar 2 a.m. wake-up.

Light, in his account, also lets cells keep time with one another. He cites experiments by Burlakov, from the Soviet-era literature, who set loach embryos of unequal ages side by side so light could pass between them: the younger eggs sped up and the older ones slowed until they matched, through bursts of ultra-weak photons. A glass barrier abolished the effect, while with quartz between them the eggs could even correct each other's deformities. Kruse takes this as proof that complex cells manage their own "time symmetry" with light.

Indoor living and nnEMF (man-made wireless and electrical fields), he says, erase the day–night swings in temperature and magnetic field that the clock relies on. Proteins are then tagged at the wrong time, flagged as damaged and scrapped, at a heavy cost in ATP and NAD⁺ (the cell's energy currency and its main electron carrier). He calls this costly loss of stored order "Landauer liquidation" and says it pushes cells toward fermentation.

#### Mass on the base chain

Kruse borrows a Bitcoin analogy. DNA is the "base chain," and daily circadian tagging is a "layer two" that adjusts genes without rewriting the code, so the genome never stiffens. Methyl and acetyl tags, and deuterium, add literal mass to that base chain. When deuterium enters the methyl pool used to tag DNA, he says, vibrations slow, enzymes drag, the epigenetic clock stalls and the strand leaks faint light. He faults chronobiology, citing Satchin Panda's work, for treating the clock as chemical software and missing the planetary "hardware" that powers it.

In his account, guarding the base chain is the clock's original job. Daily timing arose in cyanobacteria about three billion years ago, around the time oxygen first built up; it first kept nitrogen fixation apart from photosynthesis, but its deeper task, he says, was managing heavy water so deuterium could not contaminate methyl groups. DNA methylation still runs on a daily rhythm so that at night, when the body's deuterium-to-hydrogen ratio is lowest, the cell can swap out methyl groups that have picked up deuterium; the fully heavy form, CD₃, is the worst. He says people with type 1 diabetes carry heavy loads of these in their insulin-making cells.

The problem can begin before birth. A fetus flooded with deuterium, he says, receives altered methylation that silences genes for fat metabolism and mitochondrial repair, and cradle cap is the first visible sign.

He also describes a protective brake. FOXO3, a gene common in very long-lived people, can pause cell division. Kruse calls this a "clock dampener" that trades copying speed for "structural time permanence." The pause opens a window for light-driven repair, while template-free DNA-writing systems first described in bacteria (DRT3 and DRT7) rebuild telomere caps. Under LED light, nnEMF and a weakening field the opposite happens: rushed copying fractures the repeats and a p53 surge drives senescence (permanent cell retirement) or cancer.

In practice, Kruse tells people not to rely on epigenetic "Horvath" clocks, saliva telomere tests, NAD⁺ precursors or peptides, which in his model read or poke at outputs rather than causes. His rule is to avoid anything that adds "atomic mass" to the base chain; he applies it to mRNA injections and newborn vitamin K shots. Instead he says to reset the physical environment: barefoot grounding under full sun, in a place with a horizontal magnetic field, and dark nights that let the clock do its overnight clean-up.

### Information, erasure and the chromosome 2 "safe mode"
- **id:** landauer-erasure
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse frames disease as lost information rather than low energy: corrupted proteins and falling voltage are "erased" at a cost in heat, and under enough stress a fuse on chromosome 2 drops the body into a primitive safe mode.
- **protocols:** none

Kruse argues that disease is, at root, a loss of information rather than a shortage of energy. Damaged proteins and a falling cell voltage are "erased," and every erasure costs heat. Under extreme stress, he says, a built-in fuse on chromosome 2 trips and drops the body into a primitive "safe mode." In his model this explains why many modern illnesses look like the body deliberately downgrading itself.

#### Information matters more than energy

In Kruse's view, the precision of the cell's spin signals matters more than the amount of energy it has, because energy without direction is only heat. Blue light, nnEMF (man-made wireless and electrical fields) and a weakening magnetic field harm cells mainly by turning their data into noise, not by cutting ATP output. On this point he says mitochondrial researchers Doug Wallace and Nick Lane miss what matters.

He adds that life buys its precision with time rather than energy. Circadian gating, letting each chemical step run only at its set hour of the day, keeps every proton in its place much as a particle accelerator keeps its beam focused. Small, timed breaks in symmetry like this, he says, are what keep DNA far from equilibrium and define life itself.

#### Erasure and its heat

He builds on physicist Rolf Landauer, who showed in 1961 that erasing one bit of information must release a minimum amount of heat. Kruse treats a well-folded protein as a clean channel through which electrons pass with almost no waste. When the cell's structured water degrades and magnetic pinning is lost, the protein's bandgap (the energy step that lets it act as a semiconductor) narrows, electrons stall and the protein misfolds. It has become, in his words, a "corrupt bit."

The cell's answer is ubiquitin tagging. Enzymes spot the protein's lost native field, mark it with ubiquitin (a small "delete" label), and the proteasome, the cell's shredder, unwinds and cuts it. Kruse calls this Landauer erasure in molecular form.

He applies the same logic to voltage. A cell's resting charge is, for him, its information capacity, so the slide from a healthy −70 millivolts toward a fermenting −15 millivolts counts as information being erased. Each erasure dumps heat, the heat dries out structured water, and drier water corrupts more proteins. The cell is trapped in a failure-and-clean-up loop that bankrupts its ATP and NAD⁺ (its main electron carrier). Summed across a tissue, he says, this heat is what people feel as a hot flash.

Kruse uses the same accounting to explain the complications of diabetes. Extra deuterium (heavy hydrogen) is, in his phrase, "mass where it does not belong," and it raises the heat cost of every bit erased and every metabolic decision. When insulin forces glucose into a cell that is already heavy and at its limit, the waste heat, he says, "melts" the retina, the kidney's filtering units and myelin, the insulating sheath around nerves. That is his reason diabetics develop eye, kidney and nerve damage and, over time, neurodegeneration.

#### Repair before erasure

Kruse says the cell first tries a local repair, which he calls the "ubiquitin–G3P pivot." Ubiquitin brings glucose to the misfolded protein, and the glycerol-3-phosphate shuttle there supplies deuterium-poor electrons that let it refold.

He reads a finding from Australia's WEHI institute, that ubiquitin also controls the cell's stored sugar (glycogen), as support for his 28-part ubiquitination series. In his account ubiquitin flags a deuterium-loaded protein, a "stalled semiconductor," for replacement, and the sugar stored nearby supplies a fast burst of light protons, through the pentose phosphate pathway and the G3P shuttle, that washes the deuterium off while the new protein folds into place. He calls this swap a "semiconductive transplant" and ubiquitin the "lattice janitor." Fat is his long-game fuel, because by Kenneth Mellanby's 1942 figures it yields about twice as much metabolic water as sugar; sugar is the emergency "flash-wash." Deuterium-depleted water at 92.5 ppm, he adds, makes the janitor's job far easier by cutting the number of heavy atoms it has to tag.

The unfolded-protein response, the alarm raised when proteins fold badly, is his "error log." Its three sensors, PERK, ATF6 and IRE1, act in his account as antennas that go out of tune as water thickens. PERK halts protein production so less heat is made, and the protein-building compartment expands to give melanin and heme more surface for a magnetic shield. If the deuterium load or field loss is too great, repair gives way to cell death, which he marks as a final red-shifted burst of faint light.

#### The chromosome 2 fuse

Human chromosome 2 formed when two ancestral ape chromosomes fused. A leftover stretch of telomere sequence (chromosome-end cap) remains inside it at the fusion point, and Kruse treats it as a field-sensitive fuse. Under the TRCS model he follows (Telomere/rDNA Co-Regulation), when a voltage crash threatens to erase the genome, this loop "blows." Triggers he names include cosmic rays and stray light during field loss, poor sleep, very low cholesterol and deuterium-heavy food out of season.

A surge of p53, the genome's guardian protein, then forces what he calls an "atavistic retreat": tissues revert to older, simpler designs, like a satellite powering down to safe mode as it crosses the South Atlantic Anomaly. Nerve, myelin and pigment tissue shrink so the body becomes a "worse antenna" for electromagnetic noise. The cell also stops spending energy on rigid collagen, loosening connective tissue on purpose to buy time. The diaphragm and lower esophageal sphincter slip back to a looser template. In his account this is the shared cause of hypermobile Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, reflux, hiatal hernia and sleep apnea. It is also why he says repeated ligament injections fail: the voltage problem is never fixed.

The fusion site, he says, sits among four genes that shaped modern humans, which he calls a "quantum blockchain." POMC makes the hormone that drives melanin. EDAR builds the sweat glands that cool the body. GCG makes glucagon and the gut hormones that run the pancreas's bicarbonate flush, which he calls the gut's isotope exhaust. BCL11A switches blood from fetal to adult hemoglobin, and in the safe-mode surge, he says, blood shifts back toward the fetal form to fuel repair.

#### The same law at planetary scale

Kruse applies Landauer's limit to Earth itself. If the planet's core is erasing its current magnetic pattern on the way to a new one, the lost information must leave as heat and radiation. He reads unusual radio signals detected over Antarctica by the ANITA balloon experiment as that exhaust, the planetary version of the faint light flares from failing mitochondria, and says the core is falling out of step with the mantle above it.

#### Sunlight and the p53 balance

Kruse adds that whole-spectrum sunlight activates p53, which promotes DNA repair. That stops stray chromatin fragments (loose bits of DNA and protein) from piling up outside the nucleus. Left to accumulate, he says, they trip the cGAS-STING alarm and the inflammatory signaling of aged cells, opening the door to chronic disease and frailty. In his view this light-driven switch is the missing explanation for why drugs that clear aged cells have so far disappointed.

In practice, Kruse reads safe-mode symptoms as a message to change the environment that caused them, not to suppress them. To keep DNA damage low and p53 working, he points to whole-spectrum sunlight, avoiding toxins and strong magnetic pinning (living under a stable local field while grounded and sunlit). For older people facing a weakening field, he says higher cholesterol helps hold the membrane's charge and avoid the safe-mode shutdown.

### Water: the "160 vs 78" battery
- **id:** water-160-vs-78
- **pillar:** water
- **summary:** Kruse says healthy mitochondrial water is structured, with a dielectric he numbers at 160, and disease collapses it to ordinary bulk water at 78; the healthy state is set at conception and held up by potassium, light and a strong field.
- **protocols:** sea-salt-ddw

This is the hinge of most of Kruse's teaching. He says the water lining the inner mitochondrial membrane is not ordinary water but a structured layer that stores a great deal of electric charge. He describes its state with a single number, a dielectric constant of 160, and says disease collapses it to ordinary "bulk" water at 78. Nearly every illness in his model passes through this collapse.

#### What the numbers mean

A dielectric constant measures how much electric charge a substance can hold and how strongly it buffers electric fields. Kruse treats the structured water at the membrane as a battery, or capacitor, with a high value of 160, and ordinary water as a much weaker one at 78.

He ties the number to the fine-structure constant, the figure of about 1/137 that sets how likely an electron is to catch a photon. Structured water, he says, tightens that grip and deuterium (heavy hydrogen) loosens it. That is why he calls the dielectric constant, rather than genes, proteins or microtubules, the "transformer of life," and counts this link, which joined his work to Gilbert Ling's, as the most important part of what he teaches.

He identifies the structured state with Gerald Pollack's exclusion-zone (EZ) water, an ordered layer that forms against surfaces and pushes dissolved particles out. In his model dawn UV-A light and near-infrared light build this layer, and a horizontal magnetic field anchors it so it does not discharge. He also credits Mae-Wan Ho with describing water as a quantum-coherent liquid crystal, adding that to stay coherent it must also be free of deuterium, and so free of friction.

Following Ling, Kruse says potassium anchors this "water table," with melanin as its partner; he calls the two "brothers." His mechanism is that potassium holds its own shell of water more loosely than sodium does, so it can sit right against proteins and lock nearby water molecules into stacked, polarized layers, lifting the value from about 78 toward 160. Melanin, he says, amplifies this effect without binding the potassium. Disturbed potassium handling is therefore the first sign of breakdown. He blames potassium-wasting diuretics such as Lasix for collapsing the structure. He describes Ling's contribution as finding the battery's container, and his own as identifying the fuel: light and magnetic fields driving electron flow.

In his account the exclusion zone is Ling's capacitor, and it also does the sorting. A healthy, potassium-held EZ physically pushes deuterium out into the bulk "waste stream." Strip the potassium, as long-term Lasix does, and the EZ collapses, deuterium floods the cell's "inner sea" and the fluid thickens. The same ionic stress, he says, signals through AMPA receptors to switch off MITF, the master switch of melanin-making, so the person loses melanin as well as water. That is why he tells clinicians to read a low potassium level as a clue that internal melanin is missing.

He finds a physical picture for this in "ferrons," waves of electric polarization that physicists recently reported launching with laser pulses in layered ferroelectric crystals, where they give off narrow-band terahertz light. (He uses the same word elsewhere for the iron cores of ferritin.) In a cell, he says, a UV-A photon striking melanin launches the same kind of ripple through potassium-held EZ water, letting millions of reactions coordinate at wave speed; lose the potassium and signaling falls back to slow chemical diffusion. Drawing on the 2016 physics Nobel for topological states of matter (Thouless, Haldane and Kosterlitz), he calls melanin plus potassium-held water a protected surface on which charge moves without resistance, with deuterium as a pothole in the road. His summary: melanin is the hardware, potassium the stabilizer, structured water the medium, deuterium-depleted water smooths the surface, and UV-A with a well-aligned field (declination near zero) supplies the starting torque. Lasix, in this picture, is a "de-gaussing tool" that wipes the cell's hard drive.

Sodium has a separate job in his model. It raises the dielectric of blood and spinal fluid; without enough, he says, these fluids lose their electrical stiffness, thicken like ketchup and can no longer push deuterium out toward the body's edges.

#### Water's two spin forms

Kruse also explains the two states through spin. Ordinary water is a fixed 3-to-1 mix of two forms: ortho-water, whose hydrogen nuclei spin in step, and para-water, whose spins cancel. He calls ortho-water the "grease": magnetic, sticky and viscous, the 78 state where deuterium builds up. Para-water, with no spin noise, packs into the ordered 160 layer that lets protons pass without friction; he calls it the "superconductor."

Carbon dioxide tips the balance in his account. When the body holds on to CO₂ and local tissue pH sits around 6.8–7.1, he says water shifts toward the para form. Hemoglobin senses that change and releases oxygen gently, in its safe form.

#### What the collapse does

When water falls from 160 to 78, Kruse says electron and proton transfer slow about 1.43-fold. That pushes the respiratory chain into "Marcus inversion," his name for the stalled state in which pushing harder moves fewer electrons. He reports his own computer model of the spin filter that guides electrons (CISS): it runs at about 32% efficiency in 160 water but under 9% in 78 water, a loss he calls the point where the cell loses its identity.

Stalled electrons release heat, which melts more structure and thickens spinal fluid. In a full meltdown the membrane's charge swings positive and the cell leaks UPE, the faint light given off by stressed cells. He describes the result as tissue turning into a cloudy lens that scatters light, and says this pushes heteroplasmy (the share of damaged mitochondrial DNA) past its tipping point.

Structured water is also, in Kruse's account, an isotope shield. At 160 it acts as a mass filter that keeps deuterium away from the nucleus and DNA; at 78 heavy isotopes get in and load NAD⁺, the cell's main electron carrier, with deuterium.

The brain feels the collapse directly. Kruse describes microtubules, the fine tubes of the cell's skeleton, as waveguides for signals. In ordinary 78 water, he says, their quantum states would scramble within trillionths of a second; potassium- and melanin-structured 160 water insulates them enough to stay coherent at body temperature. As the dielectric slides toward 78, he says, the brain drops into a cruder operating mode and thinking narrows into survival-driven reaction. He faults depression research for looking only at brain structure and never at how sunlight changes the thickness of spinal fluid.

Other organs show it too. In the gut, he says, the water around the muscle fibers stops acting as a lubricant, segments stall, and stomach paralysis (gastroparesis) follows. He reads a rising blood homocysteine level as a gauge of the same collapse: it climbs, in his account, just as water falls from 160 to 78, often in places of high magnetic declination, so he treats it as a marker of excess tissue deuterium rather than of methylation, and says clearing it takes strong proton flow driven by UV and near-infrared light while grounded in a strong field.

He lists everyday causes of the collapse. Isolated blue light from screens and LEDs lacks the red and infrared needed to rebuild structure. nnEMF (man-made wireless and electrical fields) and loss of magnetic pinning do similar harm. So, he says, do petroleum and silicone lotions applied to skin after a warm shower, which he says halve the skin's charge-holding capacity much as blue light does.

#### Set at conception

Kruse says the egg cell is held at 160 as a protected core. Before fertilization it keeps Complex I, the entry point of the electron chain, switched off, sits in deuterium-poor liquid-crystal water and uses melanin to keep deuterium out. Fertilization switches Complex I on, and from then on the environment begins writing the phenotype, the body's visible traits. If the mother lives under a weakening field such as the South Atlantic Anomaly, or amid heavy nnEMF, her deuterium-sorting fails, deuterium settles in the embryo's brain ventricles and cristae, and its water slides toward 78. He links this to the rise of inherited disease since 1859 in lands bordering the Southern, Indian and Atlantic Oceans.

#### Spirals and the heart

Kruse says spirals order water. His rule of thumb is "three turns sense, seven turns resonate," meaning a seven-turn spiral pushes nearby water toward the 160 state. He says the heart's helical chambers spin blood this way to control its deuterium and thickness, keyed by the vagus nerve.

In his model the heart's spiral works as a centrifuge, flinging deuterium outward so that blood serves as a reservoir at about 150 parts per million while the inside of cells stays light; the brain's nightly cleaning flow during sleep renews the shield. Albumin, the main protein of blood plasma, keeps the water lattice in vessels tight and the blood thin. He calls it the vascular twin of NAD⁺, since both mark a body that sorts deuterium well.

In practice, Kruse's route back to 160 is environmental. He points to morning sun (UV-A plus infrared), grounding and magnetic pinning (living under a stable local field while grounded and sunlit), together with restoring potassium and avoiding drugs that waste it. He sees two other paths to the same result: the bicarbonate loop built from carbon dioxide, and dietary DHA. He also suggests drinking water from deep volcanic fissures ("primary water") and skipping petroleum or silicone lotions after showering. For anyone planning a family, he says the parents' light and field environment before conception matters as much as their own health.

### Oxygen's spin and the paramagnetic gate
- **id:** magnetic-excursion-oxygen
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** In his model Earth's field keeps oxygen in its safe triplet state at the end of the electron chain, and a weakening field flips it to reactive singlet oxygen, leaving cells starved even when blood oxygen looks normal.
- **protocols:** pin-first-order

Oxygen can exist in two spin states, and Kruse makes the difference between them a master switch for health. In his model Earth's magnetic field keeps oxygen in its calm "triplet" form where cells use it. A weakening field, or man-made fields and light, flips it into reactive "singlet" oxygen that burns tissue.

#### Why ordinary oxygen is safe

The oxygen we breathe is triplet oxygen: its two outer electrons spin in the same direction. That makes it paramagnetic (weakly drawn to a magnet). Kruse stresses that the molecules of our tissues have paired, opposite spins, so oxygen cannot react with them directly without breaking the rule that spin is conserved. This "spin barrier," he says, is what makes air-breathing life possible. Oxygen only reacts when a precise catalyst realigns the spins, which is exactly what the cell's electron chain does.

Singlet oxygen is the same molecule with its spins paired. In his account it is diamagnetic (weakly pushed away by a magnet), does no useful work and reacts violently.

#### The paramagnetic gate

At the end of the electron chain sits cytochrome c oxidase, or Complex IV. Kruse calls its copper–iron center a "paramagnetic gate," a checkpoint that matches incoming electrons to oxygen's spin with the help of Earth's field. When the gate works, spin-aligned electrons tunnel in smoothly, oxygen becomes deuterium-depleted water (water low in heavy hydrogen), and the membrane holds about −70 millivolts. He says the center must use the heavy copper isotope copper-65, citing Lahoud and Moynier's work on brain copper isotopes. He adds that paramagnetic oxygen is drawn toward the small magnetic fields of the spinning ATP synthase motors.

He cites a 2025 study by Beutner and colleagues as support for the field's role. In it, isolated heart mitochondria placed in a weak static magnetic field, far weaker than a fridge magnet, raised their oxygen use by about 40% while making fewer damaging oxygen by-products, but only within a narrow sweet spot. He reads this as the field keeping electron spins parallel. Melanin, DHA and cholesterol, he says, help a strong field hold oxygen's spins in place, and grounding supplies free electrons that do the same.

#### The planet's oxygen and its field

Kruse reads a 2025 Science Advances paper by Kuang and colleagues, which tied Earth's oxygen levels to the strength of its magnetic dipole over 540 million years, as proof that the planet's core is the master and oxygen the servant. In his telling, ocean currents coupled to the field after oxygen first built up in the atmosphere. That locked oxygen in its triplet state and made the Cambrian explosion of complex animals possible.

He takes the story further than the paper. As Earth's inner core hardened, he says, it gave the dynamo enough torque to re-anchor its field lines, and the field then sorted deuterium out of the oceans and air, so oxygen rose because of the field and not simply because plants multiplied. Ozone completes his picture as the outer insulator that blocks the harshest ultraviolet (UV-C). He casts the three as parts of one motor: the dynamo is the stator (the fixed part that sets up the field), oxygen the carrier and ozone the insulator. Ozone holes, he says, sit where the field is weakest, which he links to rising ataxia (loss of coordination) and melanoma in Australia.

Mammals, in his account, need this lock most. With a constant body temperature and a very high-voltage inner membrane, they required the tightest coupling to the field, refined over some 200 million years through what he calls the heme–melanin axis.

#### When the gate fails

Because magnetism controls spin, Kruse argues, a weakening core field is now pushing oxygen toward singlet in many zip codes and "liquidating" tissue. nnEMF (man-made wireless and electrical fields), isolated blue light and deuterium do the same locally; the heavier bond deuterium forms with oxygen, he says, resists normal triplet chemistry. Once oxygen flips, it attacks cardiolipin (the membrane's insulating fat), mitochondrial DNA and iron–sulfur clusters. Voltage falls toward −15 millivolts and production of clean water stops.

Citing what he calls a European Space Agency report that the inner core has reversed its rotation, he describes field chaos driving cells toward fermentation by three routes. Electron pairs stay in the wrong spin state too long, flipping oxygen to singlet. Iron "compass" cores in cells lose their north and soak up stray fields. And cell water loses its charge-holding structure.

#### Oxygen everywhere, none in the engine

Kruse says the more nnEMF and blue light surround a person, the more the Bohr effect takes over; this is hemoglobin's habit of releasing oxygen as tissue turns acidic. Without the sun's UV-A and infrared, the structured water at the membrane collapses, protons flood the cell and an "environmental acidosis" sets in. Hemoglobin dumps its oxygen, but with the water's charge-holding capacity lowered the oxygen cannot tunnel into the mitochondria. He sums it up as oxygen everywhere and none in the engine.

For the same reason he calls a normal 99% pulse-oximeter reading a clinical trap. Without nitric oxide and carbon dioxide to loosen it, he says, oxygen stays bound to hemoglobin and cannot fall into cells through thickened, deuterium-heavy water. A person can be suffocating amid plenty, with the brain slipping into local fermentation; he sees this in tired, anxious, foggy children.

He goes as far as calling it a functional oxygen allergy. Red light helps cytochrome c oxidase make water and UV builds melanin, he says, but only in someone who is grounded. Without sunlight and grounding, oxygen at the end of the chain becomes toxic and the brain turns breathing down, and people whose melanin has dried out behave as if allergic to oxygen. He reads sleep apnea in this light, as a brain collecting deuterium it cannot clear.

#### What singlet oxygen destroys

Kruse lists the damage. Collagen needs triplet oxygen for the enzyme that builds hydroxyproline, one of its key amino acids. When oxygen goes singlet, he says, collagen production stalls and tissues loosen, an "environmental" form of Ehlers-Danlos syndrome. Melanin, tooth enamel and fats burn, and heme proteins, including the liver's CYP enzymes and hormone machinery, are lost. He calls singlet oxygen a "thermal hot potato" that cooks retina, skin, hair and mitochondria.

It also corrupts the cell's light. In Kruse's model triplet oxygen releases energy quietly, with very little stray light. Singlet oxygen sets off chain reactions in fats whose decay throws out disordered bursts of UPE (faint light from cells). That noise drowns the coherent UV signals he says guide cell division.

Not every oxygen by-product is bad in his account. Brief superoxide pulses act as healthy signals, like Morse code between the nucleus and mitochondria, and he says diabetics have exhausted them.

In practice, Kruse's answer is the "pin first" order: fix your magnetic and wireless environment first, add morning light second, and change exercise or diet only after that. He also warns that moving to a different magnetic field flips oxygen at once and costs the vagus nerve its ability to clear deuterium, so a move should be deliberate. To stand in for a weakening field, he suggests sleeping on a north-seeking static magnetic pad, taking pure DHA and getting raw morning UV-A sunlight. He does not treat a normal oxygen reading as proof that cells are getting what they need.

- **sources:** [379413](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379413/), [379263](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379263/), [383640](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383640/), [381192](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381192/), [378182](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378182/), [377037](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377037/), [378270](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378270/), [377045](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377045/), [375566](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375566/), [380816](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380816/), [372738](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372738/), [373255](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373255/)

### Marcus inversion and the Warburg shift
- **id:** marcus-theory-inverted-region
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse's core failure state: electron transfer stalls in Marcus's "inverted region," and cells retreat to fermentation as a protective emergency mode.
- **protocols:** none

Kruse's central picture of how cells fail comes from chemistry. Electron transfer, he argues, can stall in a state where pushing harder makes things worse, the "inverted region" described by Rudolph Marcus. Cells caught there retreat to fermentation as an emergency mode. In his model this one sequence underlies cancer, faster aging and neurodegeneration.

#### The inverted region

Marcus's theory, which won him the 1992 Nobel Prize in Chemistry, describes how fast an electron jumps from one molecule to another. Up to a point, a bigger driving force (the energy pushing the electron) makes the jump faster. Past a peak, set by the energy the surroundings must spend rearranging themselves, the rate falls again. That falling side is the inverted region. Kruse likens it to an electronic tunnel diode, where raising the voltage lowers the current. That is why, he says, a stuck chain cannot be fixed by pushing harder.

He places health at the top of this curve, matched with Martin Picard's "just-right" zone of energy resistance. Distance matters too: in his model, pushing the respiratory complexes just one ångström (a ten-billionth of a meter) farther apart cuts tunneling about tenfold.

#### What pushes cells there

Kruse names field loss, nnEMF (man-made wireless and electrical fields), blue light and calcium overload as the main pushes. Deuterium (heavy hydrogen) adds its own: with twice hydrogen's mass, he says, its spin precesses (wobbles) out of step with the chain's spin filter, causing backscatter and friction.

A weaker magnetic field also widens the circle an electron traces as it moves (its Larmor radius). Once that path exceeds the 14-ångström limit he credits to Dutton, tunneling collapses and the whole chain stops. He calls this a "cyanide-like" lock that is physical rather than chemical. He now treats the full three-dimensional local field, including patches of reversed polarity, as the variable that matters, with compass offset (declination) only a rough guide. Living or sleeping near smart meters, Wi-Fi routers or 5G arrays with an already damaged system, he says, can act like a small field collapse inside the tissue.

#### What happens next

Stalled electrons spill their energy as singlet oxygen (the reactive form of oxygen) and heat. In Kruse's account the heat melts the structured water at the membrane from its healthy "160" state to ordinary "78" water, which slows electrons further. The membrane's charge then swings toward positive, faint UPE light leaks out, and the 71-terahertz timing signal to the nucleus goes quiet. He applies the same picture to the brain: in sleep-deprived, ungrounded, screen-lit people, he says, navigation cells "smear" positional signals like a telegraph on an overloaded wire.

#### A circuit breaker, not just a fault

Kruse increasingly frames the inverted region as protection. On a planet whose field varies, he says, nature uses it as a built-in circuit breaker, his "topological insurance." When the field sags or technology warps the cristae, entering the inverted region clamps electron flow before a runaway thermal meltdown.

He describes a milder breaker that adults use under deuterium stress, modeled on the egg cell, which keeps Complex I, the chain's main entry point, switched off entirely. A stressed adult copies it partway through the glycerol-3-phosphate shunt (and a related proline route), which feeds electrons in further down the chain and bypasses a Complex I jammed by deuterium. This keeps the cell's water from collapsing toward 78 but costs membrane voltage, which he says shows up as mental haze and essential tremor. Biology, in his phrase, would rather be "slow and foggy" than "fast and dead," and he urges people to lean on this route during the present field decline rather than slide into lattice lock, his term for water fully stiffened by deuterium.

A place or a season can hold someone there, which he calls a "Marcus inversion well," and body fat and seasonal timing can soften its walls. He even suggests obesity and cancer may be safer low-energy modes that stave off organ failure, reversible if the magnetic interference is removed. For the same reason, he says, no diet can fix the problem. Food supplies fuel, not the magnetic reference that shapes the cristae. Extra fuel jams an inverted chain further, and a prolonged "keto flu" signals a light-and-field problem, not a macronutrient one.

#### The Warburg shift and the biological SCRAM

Otto Warburg observed that cancer cells ferment sugar even when oxygen is present. Kruse treats this as an ancient survival mode. As the field falls, cells deliberately lower their voltage and retreat to glycolysis (sugar fermentation) to shield their DNA. In doing so they also make deuterium-depleted water in the cell fluid and cool themselves. Building on Warburg, he says cancer cells fall from about −70 millivolts to −15 to −30 millivolts. A gate in the inner membrane then lets deuterium-rich water flood the core of the mitochondrion and slow the ATP synthase motor. So, for Kruse, the Warburg question is about spin, not chemistry.

Speed, not volume, explains the turn to sugar in his account. Glucose is burned quickly but yields less metabolic water than burning fat, so a cell damaged by deuterium, having lost the fat-burning machinery that normally anchors it, uses glucose for a fast "flash-wash" out of trouble.

Normally, he says, light runs two clean-up programs: UV drives the suicide of damaged cells and near-infrared drives their self-recycling. When both fail, a damaged cell can neither repair nor die, and fermentation is what remains.

Field loss also triggers what he calls a "biological SCRAM," the nuclear-reactor term for an emergency shutdown, which he traces to Enrico Fermi's first reactor in 1942. Mitochondrial DNA leaks into the cell and fires the cGAS-STING alarm and Robert Naviaux's "cell danger response." Kruse links this to rising early-onset cancers, faster aging and neurodegeneration. He also says lipid nanoparticles and spike protein can drag the membrane past the same wall, with a person's level of damaged mitochondrial DNA at the time setting the risk.

In practice, Kruse says the way out is to remove what pushed the cell in rather than to force more fuel through: restore sunlight, grounding and a stable local field, and keep wireless transmitters away from where you sleep. He does not expect keto, carnivore or any other diet alone to fix it. The same logic applies to oxygen: forcing extra oxygen into a fermenting engine, he says, "cooks" it, so he treats hyperbaric oxygen and scuba diving as equal risks for someone in this state and is wary of CPAP machines for the same group.

- **sources:** [385637](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385637/), [384183](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384183/), [383819](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383819/), [384604](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384604/), [379984](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379984/), [383342](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383342/), [384270](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384270/), [384969](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384969/), [374074](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374074/), [378207](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378207/), [372391](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372391/), [372505](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372505/)

### Pin first: magnetism, gravity and "spaceflight sickness without the rocket"
- **id:** pin-first
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse ranks magnetism ahead of light and water and says chronic illness behaves like spaceflight, so magnetic pinning must come before diet, exercise or drugs.
- **protocols:** pin-first-order

"Pinned" is Kruse's word for living under a stable, mostly horizontal local magnetic field, ideally over volcanic rock such as basalt, while grounded and sunlit. He now ranks magnetism first, then light, then water, and says all three are needed together. Because he sees chronic illness as a form of spaceflight sickness, his rule is to pin first and only then change diet, exercise or medication.

#### Why magnetism comes first

In Kruse's model magnetism is the "father" of the body's direct current, because it controls the spin of electrons and so the state of oxygen. Sunlight, he says, does little for a person without Earth's magnetic flux around them. Once oxygen has flipped to its reactive singlet form and NAD⁺ (the cell's main electron carrier) is loaded with deuterium, a state he calls "NADD+," metabolism goes blind to light.

That creates a catch-22, he says. Without pinning, body water becomes deuterated and the cell can no longer use the very sunlight that would rescue it. He calls this "near-infrared deficiency": an unpinned person can be short of usable red and infrared light even while lying naked in the sun. That magnetism should outrank light is, in his view, rare in human history.

He sums up the roles this way: the field tells life *why* to move, morning light tells it *when*, and barefoot grounding near an agonic line (where compass north equals true north) tells it *where*. When people tell him something simply feels wrong in their body, his short answer is missing magnetic flux.

#### Gravity and the other forces

Kruse adds gravity to the picture. He cites a Nature Communications paper, "Gravitational and mechanical forces shape mitochondrial translation," which found that leaving Earth's gravity lowers mitochondrial gene activity with no chemical change. In his reading, gravity is a physical press that keeps the cristae folded and the iron–sulfur clusters close enough for electrons to tunnel between them. In microgravity the press is released, the gaps widen and electrons back up.

He treats gravity, electromagnetism and the weak force as separate anchors that cannot stand in for one another. Gravity is the press, electromagnetism the governor of spin and tunneling, and the weak force the source of life's one-handed (chiral) molecules. A strong magnet in space, he says, cannot stop the matrix from expanding, and a centrifuge without a magnetosphere cannot govern spin. Drawing on papers that treat gravity as a product of entropy, he calls ordered "160" water the "diamond" and ordinary "78" water the "coal."

#### Spaceflight sickness without the rocket

He reads NASA's Twins Study and long space-station missions as showing that spaceflight leaves chemical locks on DNA. These silence the genes for the entry and exit gates of the electron chain (NDUFS1, SDHA and COX4I1) for more than 14 weeks after return, which he calls a "14-week epigenetic lockout."

Kruse argues that modern life recreates orbit on the ground. Insulation from the earth, high-rise living, frequent flying and dense wireless exposure (wireless "soup," which he calls a "hyper-local magnetic excursion") strip away the planet's anchors. Chronic illness is then "spaceflight sickness without the rocket." He names type 2 diabetes, fibromyalgia and neurodegeneration in heavily wired homes. People in this state carry the same gene locks, he says, so pushing them into Zone 2 or high-intensity training drives electrons into a blocked chain, making singlet oxygen and damaging the membrane. Supplement stacks, peptides, Cialis or GLP-1 drugs given to such a cell "feed the fire."

In his model the brain shows the damage first. It is about 2% of body mass yet takes 15–20% of the heart's output and about a fifth of the body's resting oxygen, and its spinal fluid is pushed mainly by the heart's pulse, with cilia (tiny beating hairs) steering the flow. When the planet's anchor weakens, he says, the heart's spiral flow loses coherence and the brain develops, here on Earth, the picture astronauts get in orbit, called SANS (spaceflight-associated neuro-ocular syndrome): enlarged fluid spaces, swollen sheaths around the optic nerves and flattened eyeballs. He reads balance problems reported in the Artemis 2 crew as the same gravity-loss effect.

#### Two scales of pinning

Pinning works at two scales in Kruse's model: the global field from Earth's core (the geodynamo) and the local field of the ground beneath you. While the core field was steady, local rock hardly mattered. As it weakens, local conditions decide who stays well. Everyone's pinning is individual, he says, so the first step is to pin where you already live and to consider moving only if that proves impossible.

Local rock can also work against the global field. Where volcanic rock froze with reversed magnetism, as he describes at Kilauea in Hawaii, the two fields cancel and the field felt by the body approaches zero. The restoring pull on the body's protons then vanishes, and their relaxation time (how long they take to settle after being disturbed) stretches far past what he calls a "7.2-second quiet buffer." In his words the magnetic pin is pulled, which is why living beside a volcano is no guarantee of being pinned.

He offers the Hunza people of the Karakoram as a model of place over plate. Going lightly clothed in high-altitude, full-spectrum sun builds their structured water, cold orders it further, and their quartz-rich ground and mineral-heavy glacial meltwater keep their blood thin. Focusing on their food, he says, misses the point.

He calls accepting that food and drugs can't fix a field problem "Personal Quantum Solvency," and taking charge of your own magnetic environment "magnetic sovereignty." He puts about three-quarters of today's disease burden on the field's decline and only about 5% on diet.

In practice, Kruse's "pin first" order runs in three steps. First, leave the wireless soup, ground strictly (skin to earth, or a low-resistance indoor grounding system) and sleep in a bedroom free of man-made fields. Second, rebuild structured water with morning solar infrared light. Only third, change exercise or diet, which he says restores a healthy membrane charge of about −70 millivolts. He also advises flying less, and checking the local rock as well as the global field before assuming a place is pinned.

- **sources:** [379642](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379642/), [379524](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379524/), [382019](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382019/), [382911](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382911/), [381022](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381022/), [375888](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375888/), [379435](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379435/), [376217](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/376217/), [374140](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374140/), [382846](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382846/), [371742](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371742/), [372859](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372859/)

### Lattice lock
- **id:** lattice-lock
- **pillar:** water
- **summary:** His name for the end state of water collapse: body water stiffens and loads with deuterium, the energy motor jams, and the cell can no longer make its own light water or use sunlight; it can build slowly or strike suddenly.
- **protocols:** carbonated-water, volcano-protocol, hypnosis-defrag, hrv-tracking, spinal-lattice-lock, sun-allergy, iv-fluids-acute-collapse

"Lattice lock" is Kruse's term for the end point of the water collapse at the heart of his model. Body water stiffens and fills with deuterium (heavy hydrogen), ATP synthase (the cell's energy motor) jams, and the cell can no longer make its own deuterium-poor water or use sunlight. He uses it to connect conditions as different as spinal scarring, sun allergy, stiff heart rhythms and premature aging.

#### What locks

Deuterium is hydrogen with an extra neutron, about twice as heavy. Kruse says a single deuterium atom stiffens roughly 96 surrounding water molecules. Heavy deuterons (deuterium nuclei) also deform the proton channels of ATP synthase, slowing the motor from its normal 9,000 revolutions per minute toward a stop. ATP output and voltage fall, singlet oxygen (the reactive form) rises, and the cell turns pseudohypoxic: it behaves as if starved of oxygen even though oxygen is present.

At root, he defines the lock through spin. Earth's field normally flips electron spins inside radical pairs, short-lived pairs of unpaired electrons formed during reactions, so oxygen can reach its working triplet form. If that flip fails, the system freezes in place and oxygen stays singlet, a "hot potato" of heat rather than a signal. Deuterium is the main scrambler: its nucleus has a spin of 1 rather than hydrogen's ½, so a single atom in the lattice confuses the cell's internal compass and traps it in the singlet, fermenting state. He goes so far as to call every disease a form of lattice lock.

In his account the membrane's own charge is what normally keeps deuterium out of the mitochondrion. Once NAD⁺ (the main electron carrier) becomes deuterium-loaded "NADD+" and singlet replaces triplet oxygen, that charge falls, less clean water is made, and the lock tightens. He likens the result to a "bad metal" turning fully glassy, its orderly conducting channels lost. Muscle serves as a backup deuterium sink, so losing muscle shifts the load onto the pancreas, heart and vagus nerve.

#### How it develops

Kruse describes the usual path in an unpinned, indoor, wireless-soaked person. Without solar near-infrared, which he calls a "photonic purge," nitric oxide stays stuck on cytochrome c oxidase, the last enzyme of the electron chain. Electrons back up and oxygen flips to singlet. Singlet oxygen oxidizes the membranes, the water loses its liquid-crystal order, and NAD⁺ becomes NADD+. The spin-filtering protein tracks that let the cell decode light (CISS) are destroyed, so even full sun can't be read, and the cell leaks chaotic light he calls "radiant smoke."

He explains it with a mechanic's picture. The bicarbonate loop and the vagus nerve's drainage are the body's exhaust pipe. nnEMF (man-made wireless and electrical fields) and missing UV and infrared light weld that pipe shut, and grounding in a place without a magnetic pull lets back-pressure build. However good the fuel, meaning food, the engine eventually fails. The "carbon buildup" is deuterium silt, around 150 parts per million, collecting in the brain as Alzheimer's, in the inner ear as tinnitus and in the gut (bacterial overgrowth), while the drag heavy isotopes put on reactions slows p53, the genome's guardian, until the genome falters.

Diet and location add to the load in his view. He names deuterium from high-fructose corn syrup, tropical fruit eaten in a northern winter, and drinking water from deuterium-rich river basins. A person can be grounded and tanned on the outside, he warns, and still be locked inside if high-deuterium water keeps entering through the gut.

#### Acute lock

Lattice lock usually builds over years, but Kruse says it can strike at once in someone already loaded by technology and by deuterium inherited through the mother, when something suddenly raises metabolic demand. Exercise asks the membrane to move electrons and protons faster than a stiffened lattice can, and infection does the same through the immune system's surge. He reads the on-field collapse of 17-year-old Ohio athlete Maddox Graser, and a New York runner who goes into anaphylaxis whenever she exercises, as this kind of breakdown: demand from the heart and brain outruns the weakened field's ability to hold protons in place, and the system arcs like an overloaded insulator. He says he saw the same pattern in intensive-care patients during COVID, and that an acute lock "subtracts" consciousness.

He places brain fog and anesthesia on one scale. A mild thickening of the water slows electron transfer and brings haze; a rapid, body-wide loss of membrane charge is anesthesia. Halogenated anesthetic gases, he says, slip into the membrane and instantly thicken its water, and the fog that lingers after surgery is deuterium left clogging the brain's microtubules (the fine tubes of the cell's skeleton).

#### A race against the clock

Building on a forum member's "quantum triage," Kruse also frames the lock as a timing problem. He borrows T2 from MRI physics: the dephasing time, meaning how long the body's internal compass holds its phase before noise scrambles it. He sets it against how often a deuterium atom strikes, which he calls a "speed bump." On ordinary water at about 155 parts per million (ppm) he puts a strike every 4.3 seconds against a T2 of about 6 seconds. The next hit lands before the last one is cleared, a permanent "phase deficit" in which the cell's coherent signal can never lock in and leaks away as heat and faint light.

A weakening field makes it worse. With less magnetic torque on each proton, T2 stretches past 7 seconds in the South Atlantic Anomaly, leaving a repair buffer of minus 2.7 seconds; this is why he expects lattice lock to become common as the decline goes on. At 92.5 ppm, strikes come only every 7.2 seconds, leaving a 0.2-second "survival gap," and that is why he calls 92.5 ppm his minimum effective dose. Hypertonic 3% salt and grounding add torque and electrical stiffness and pull T2 back to 5–6 seconds. Win back a buffer of one to two seconds, he says, and the signal re-pins, the brainstem drain clears, and anxiety, PTSD and ADHD, which he calls "MKULTRA programming," lift along with what he calls "global brain fog." In his phrase, buying low-deuterium water is buying "coherence time."

#### Where it shows

**Brainstem.** Kruse says the body normally filters deuterium at the floor of the fourth ventricle (a fluid space in the brainstem). When a weak field or heavy wireless exposure makes that filter fail, heavy water backs up in his "sphenoid Venturi," the drain point behind the nose, and blocks the flow of spinal fluid and the vagus nerve's drainage. In ALS, he says, the deuterium instead flushes down past the obex (the lower tip of the fourth ventricle) into the spinal cord's central canal, locking the fluid there so motor neurons burn out.

**Spine.** He treats tethered cord and adhesive arachnoiditis (scarring of the spinal nerve roots) as one disease at different times of life. Tethered cord is a "fetal matrix freeze," caused by deuterium in the parents' eggs and sperm. Arachnoiditis is an "adult matrix freeze," developing as spinal fluid deuterates over decades. Drawing on Michael P. Hughes's work on phase separation (proteins dropping out of solution), he says proteins fall out of the fluid into scar webs. The scar, in his view, is a containment wall that shields bare nerves from singlet-oxygen damage, not a malfunction. Epidural steroids, spine surgery, nnEMF, viruses and mouth breathing speed it.

**Connective tissue.** Fascia that looks "dry," Kruse says, has not lost its water; its water has changed state, from a 160 liquid crystal to a stiff, deuterium-heavy 78 gel. Deuterium's stiffer bonds lock hyaluronic acid, the slippery gel between tissue layers, so layers grind instead of glide; he links this to fibromyalgia, chronic tightness and repeated ligament tears in athletes. In joints, deuterium-heavy fluid works like sand in the gears, making arthritis a matter of friction. Deuterium-laden fats in membranes also damp the burst of faint light he says collagen needs to assemble its fibers, leaving disorganized collagen, micro-tears and oxalate problems.

**Skin.** Sun allergy (polymorphous light eruption, a spring sun rash) is, in Kruse's account, winter's dammed-up deuterium flushed out by the first strong spring sun. With the vagal drain clogged, deuterium collects in covered skin. It cannot shed UV energy quickly, so the energy builds into heat, itching and burning. The rash is the immune system cleaning up, he says, and summer infrared then rebuilds structured water and clears the backlog.

**Focal locks.** He also describes local locks after alcohol, after GLP-1 drug damage to muscle, in the stomach as gastroparesis (a gut that falls electrically quiet), after long SSRI use as chronic pain and fatigue, and in a new sensitivity to blue light. He reads a mouse study by Singh and colleagues (2018), in which wrinkles and hair loss caused by switching off mitochondrial DNA reversed when it was switched back on, as a reversible focal lock. Aging, he concludes, is lost synchrony between mitochondria and the environment rather than simple wear. He even tracks sudden "lattice-locked accidents" by zip code.

#### Reading the lock

Kruse uses heart-rate variability (HRV, the beat-to-beat variation in heart timing) as the readout. Rigid, flat HRV signals complete lock; flexible HRV, especially in deep sleep, is his gauge of progress. His order of warning signs runs from chronic nasal congestion, to fatigue and memory loss, to low HRV. A lost sun-sneeze reflex, or trouble tolerating carbonated water, shows how deep the lock goes.

In practice, Kruse pairs lattice lock with release routines. The Volcano Protocol combines carbonated water with deuterium-depleted water to build a CO₂–bicarbonate loop that draws deuterium out. It adds a strong water jet and a 40 Hz tuning fork on the back to shake deuterium loose from the spine, and grounding at sunrise in a magnetically pinned place. He adds hypnosis as a "de-frag" session and breathing in shoreline wave spray at sunrise. For spinal locks he layers his tethered cord and arachnoiditis notes on top of the Volcano Protocol. For sun allergy he says to build tolerance through the season rather than hide from the sun. Because sudden demand can tip a loaded system into acute lock, he puts clearing the exhaust ahead of hard training. In a weakening field he treats 92.5 ppm deuterium-depleted water as the floor, paired with 3% salt and grounding to shorten the repair window. Throughout, he tracks HRV in deep sleep, and he says no one can honestly promise a reversal timeline.

## Light

### Sunrise: dawn UV-A and the master clock
- **id:** sunrise-why
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse says dawn light — UV-A near 380 nm plus red and near-infrared, taken in through bare eyes and skin — is the master signal that sets the body's clocks and readies its energy machinery for the day.
- **protocols:** sunrise-horizon

Kruse argues that the most important light of the day is the first light at sunrise. In his model its UV-A (the gentle ultraviolet just beyond visible violet, which he pins near 380 nanometres), together with the red and near-infrared that dominate the low sun, is the master signal that tells every clock in the body what time it is. Because he treats mitochondria, the cell's power plants, as light-driven devices, missing sunrise means starting the day with the body's wiring out of step.

#### How light reaches the clock

Kruse says most of light's effect on the body has nothing to do with seeing. Citing the ophthalmologist Fritz Hollwich's 1948 work on the "energetic portion" of the visual pathway, along with Hufeland, Paracelsus and Franz Halberg's early circadian research, he describes an "optico-magnetic" route: light entering the eyes and skin steers the hypothalamus and pituitary, the brain's hormone control centre. The receivers are not the cells used for vision but other light-sensitive materials — neuropsin, melanopsin, melanin, cholesterol, water, heme and collagen.

Neuropsin (also called OPN5) is the one he puts first. It is a UV-A sensor in the eye and skin, and in his account 380 nm light striking it sets the daily rhythm of NAD⁺, the molecule cells use to carry electrons and to run repair enzymes such as SIRT1. That keeps Complex I, the entry point of the mitochondrial energy chain, working cleanly. He calls 380 nm the "mTOR switch" because it tells mTOR, the cell's build-or-clean-up regulator, which mode to be in. He also treats NAD⁺ itself as a light-storing material that relays light to Complex I.

UV-A has a second target in the skin. Acting on tryptophan, an amino acid, it makes a compound called FICZ that switches on the aryl hydrocarbon receptor (AhR), which Kruse treats as another light antenna. Blue light and wireless radiation, he says, disorder the water around these molecules and blur that signal.

Kruse describes neuropsin as a "dual-axis transistor": it reads sunlight on one axis and Earth's magnetic field on the other. When the two agree — his "Zeeman handshake" — oxygen stays in its stable, safe form (triplet oxygen) and metabolism flows toward repair. When the field is disturbed or the light is artificial, he says the handshake fails and the same chemistry tips toward histamine, inflammation and singlet oxygen, oxygen's reactive and damaging form.

#### What sunrise renews

In his model, sunrise UV-A "renovates" heme (the iron-holding core of hemoglobin and of the energy chain's cytochromes) and melanin. The mild stress of UV-A switches on POMC, a hormone precursor the body cuts into alpha-MSH, the signal that makes melanin; mitochondria make melatonin at the same time to buffer that stress. Because the first step of heme production happens inside mitochondria and depends on UV, he links many anemias to missing full-spectrum sun. The same light, he adds, gives hair its colour.

Kruse calls hormones "light ferry boats": carriers of magnetic and photonic information, not just chemical messengers. Watching the sunrise while grounded, he says, switches on the MITF–AMPAR pathway (the circuit that keeps a person pigmented) and builds an "internal tan" of biophotons, the faint light cells give off. That light helps cut POMC into ACTH, the pituitary hormone that drives the adrenal glands, and alpha-MSH. ACTH then carries the signal to the adrenal cortex. With enough sun, he argues, the message arrives so cleanly that the adrenals need less bulk cortisol, which is how he explains a sun-living Addison's patient whose medication needs fell.

Kruse also says the body reads sunlight as a sentence, in order. The blue pre-dawn hour is the preface that anchors the clock, the amber sunrise is the opening line, and dusk is the closing chapter that cues night-time melatonin and repair. A lone orange LED switched on at a random hour carries none of that meaning. Each sunrise, in his telling, reboots the whole system and reminds it that the sun is the one reference with no alternative. He points to Egypt and the Maya at Tikal as cultures that organised their days around this sequence.

The first horizon light, he says, also triggers the cortisol awakening response (the natural morning rise in cortisol) and the glucagon gene network. Together these start the pancreas's roughly two-litre daily bicarbonate flush, which in his model sweeps deuterium (heavy hydrogen) out through the gut. Morning sun also lets the longevity gene FOXO3 apply its brakes on cell division, which he says protects the genome.

#### In practice

His sunrise-horizon routine is to be outside as the sun clears the horizon, using the first 60–90 minutes and staying 30–45 minutes. Nothing should sit between your eyes and the sun: no sunglasses, contacts, glasses or window glass. Uncover as much skin as you reasonably can and stand barefoot on surf, wet sand, dewy grass or basalt. He takes a well-formed stool (Bristol type 4) as the sign that the morning bicarbonate flush is working.
