### Midday UV-B and vitamin D
- **id:** solar-noon-vitamin-d
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** In Kruse's model, UV-B and the vitamin D it makes in skin are part of a coded light-and-magnetic-field system with a built-in safety cutoff that pills bypass, and the local field, latitude and season limit how much vitamin D sunlight can make.
- **protocols:** sun-exposure, pills-he-avoids

Kruse rejects the idea that vitamin D is just a nutrient to top up. In his view UV-B, the shorter and stronger ultraviolet of the midday sun, and the vitamin D it makes in skin form a self-regulating light system tied to the body clock and to the local magnetic field. That is why he wants vitamin D made in the skin, not swallowed.

#### UV-B as a safety brake

Kruse treats UV-B and UV-A as a pair of brakes on the mitochondrial energy chain. Vitamin D's receptor, which he places on the inner mitochondrial membrane, slows electron flow between Complexes III and IV, while UV-A releases nitric oxide, which briefly slows Complex IV. Together, he says, they keep the chain out of "Marcus inversion" — his term for electron flow that stalls when it is pushed too hard — whenever Earth's field is unstable.

That is why he calls UV-B "term life insurance" for anyone who is not "pinned" (living grounded and sunlit under a stable local magnetic field), and why he says it should not be feared even by people with cancer. Citing Slominski, Holick and Reiter (2026), he treats vitamin D and the circadian clock as one coupled loop. He adds that mitochondria make melatonin in daytime using UV, pointing to melatonin's absorption peaks near 224 and 290 nm, so vitamin D and melatonin are twin products of the sun rather than day-and-night opposites.

#### The built-in cutoff

UV-B turns a cholesterol precursor in the skin into vitamin D, but Kruse notes that only about a quarter of the yield becomes vitamin D3; the rest becomes lumisterol and tachysterol. He calls this a self-limiting "thermostat" that makes it impossible to overdose on sun-made vitamin D. In his account the UV-B hit creates a radical pair — two fragments each holding one unpaired electron. If their spins point opposite ways they recombine into D3; if they point the same way they break down into the cutoff products. The local magnetic field tips that balance.

Because the field chooses the path, he says the magnetism where you live caps how much vitamin D you can make — his explanation for why levels may not rise in California despite plenty of sun. He reads a reader's stalled vitamin D level in Florida the same way: Florida sits close to the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), a large weak patch in Earth's field, and there, he says, tissue cannot complete the light-driven repair that lets levels climb. The form of skin cholesterol matters too. In summer, sulfur-rich cholesterol carried in HDL is the "antenna" tuned to UV-B. Years of blue light and nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring) can freeze skin cholesterol in its electron-poor LDL form, his "Mismatch Freeze," so even naked equatorial sun finds nothing to work with. A cell loaded with deuterium (heavy hydrogen), he adds, lacks the energy to make or activate vitamin D at all.

Latitude and season set limits as well. Above about 37° — the Pacific Northwest or northern Japan, for example — Kruse says skin makes little vitamin D for two to eight months of the year, and in winter the low sun stops it whatever a person's ancestry, so blood levels fall. Darker skin needs more UV to make the same amount, which he says is why the stronger UV of altitude helps melanin-rich people. In a mid-latitude summer, regular time outdoors supplies enough UV-B.

#### More sun is not always better

Strong UV without grounding and infrared can itself stall the energy chain, so Kruse reads a sunburn as proof the person was not pinned enough, and calls burning on purpose foolish. Citing Fosbury's maps of US deaths, he notes that deaths from heart disease, cancer, stroke, diabetes, Alzheimer's and flu all peak one to three weeks after the winter solstice and bottom out after the summer solstice; he blames low light combined with an unstable field.

#### Sunlight as a coded signal

Kruse describes the sun as a pulsed, filtered, coded power supply rather than raw energy. When oxygen rose in Earth's early atmosphere, the ozone layer that formed began carving a pattern into sunlight. The dark Fraunhofer lines in the solar spectrum (narrow gaps where elements in the sun absorb light) act, in his telling, like a barcode, and ozone's broad Chappuis absorption band casts a "shadow" across the yellow-orange region around 589 nm. He calls this pattern a "photonic grammar," and says it is what lets the vitamin D receptor work as a metabolic brake. Melanin, in his model, is the software that reads the code.

#### Why pills fall short

An oral pill delivers pure D3 with none of the cutoff products and none of the light-run steps — in his phrase, it gives the body "the ink without the book." Kruse cites an observational study of more than 5,000 older adults, reported in the Washington Post, that linked routine supplementation in people with normal levels to slightly faster cognitive decline. In his reading the extra vitamin D acts as an "atomic impurity" in an ungrounded brain under artificial light, and he says supplemental vitamin D carries deuterium into the head. He treats a low blood level as a sign of melanin breakdown, not a reason to supplement.

#### In practice

His sun-exposure routine: near solar noon, when your shadow is shorter than you, expose your torso and back, with no sunscreen, sunglasses, contacts or glasses. Build up gradually and move to shade at the first warmth on the skin. Give UV-B priority if you are not pinned and around the winter solstice, and stay grounded while you do it. Oral vitamin D3, especially when levels are already normal, is on his list of pills to avoid.

### The four-phase sun day and the "solar callus"
- **id:** solar-callus
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse's account of how a person, especially one from a northern maternal lineage, builds tolerance to strong sun by stepping through the day's wavelengths from sunrise to sunset, and why place and altitude change the pace.
- **protocols:** four-phase-sun-day, sunset-viewing

Kruse uses "solar callus" for skin and tissue that have adapted to strong sun, so they tan rather than burn. He argues the callus is built gradually, not inherited, and that how it is built matters most for someone whose ancestors evolved far from the equator and who moves to the tropics, as he did when he moved to El Salvador.

#### Lineage and pace

He explains this through haplogroups, the maternal lineages traced through mitochondrial DNA. His own, H, is northern European, and he describes its mitochondria as naturally "leaky": built to shed heat rather than to couple tightly to energy production. Plunged suddenly into tropical UV, he says, such mitochondria lose their coherence. An H-lineage person who flies straight from northern Europe to San Salvador is, in his view, in a very different position from one who arrives slowly.

Mitochondrial DNA does not change, and a northern lineage does not become a tropical one. But Kruse says epigenetic flexibility plus local grounding — bare feet on basalt or wet black volcanic sand, which he says carry a strong horizontal magnetic field — lets the person "re-couple." The mitochondria become better insulated, leak less, and keep the blood and spinal-fluid flows that clear deuterium (heavy hydrogen) working. He calls this a re-coupled relocation. At high latitudes, by contrast, he notes that no one can hold a tan all twelve months, so a year-round natural callus is out of reach there.

#### Place and altitude

Where you stand also changes how fast the callus forms. A reader in Australia noticed he tanned far faster at sea level than at home, about 700 metres up. Kruse's explanation is that at altitude the magnetic field is weaker and noisier, closer to the radiation belts, so MITF, the master switch of pigment cells, stays in a repair-and-survive mode, spending its effort quenching damage instead of laying down pigment. At sea level the thicker atmosphere and stronger field give the melanin-making pathway the steady conditions it needs to switch into building mode, and the tan comes quickly. A tan, he adds, reports only on the skin, not on the state inside the body.

#### Four phases of light

He built his own callus by treating each day as a training program through the solar spectrum, in order. Sunrise red and near-infrared expand structured water in tissue (water ordered into a charge-holding gel layer) and prepare cells to handle the UV that comes later. Mid-morning UV-A on bare eyes signals the brain, through the POMC hormone pathway, to lay down melanin, which he treats as the skin's spin filter. A brief midday UV-B dose makes vitamin D and sulfur-rich cholesterol and, together with the new melanin, completes the callus. The last daylight hour's red and infrared then repair the day's oxidative damage and prepare the body to make melatonin. In his model the infrared at both ends prepares and repairs the tissue around the UV-heavy middle.

#### Redness and filters

Redness, Kruse says, means the membrane's electrical containment has broken down, so his rule is never to burn. Sunscreen blocks the UV-B and UV-A needed for vitamin D and melanin. Sunglasses make the brain read cloud or darkness, so the melanin signal never starts and the person burns quickly at midday; contacts interfere in the same way. He names DHA from wild seafood as the fat that turns sunlight into current in tissue, and says saffron speeds the callus: its carotenoids crocin and crocetin, its aroma compound safranal and the flavonol kaempferol.

#### Skin colour as a readout

In Kruse's view pale skin is not a sign of health but an atrophied organ, made pale by sunscreen, sunglasses, indoor life or drugs. Near-infrared sun draws blood to the skin and boosts red-cell production, and strong UV keeps blood oxygen in its stable form, turning blood deep red. Sun-flushed skin is, for him, a visible readout of redox health that other species also use to choose mates (he cites gelada baboons and birds). He retells accounts, which he credits to Lavoisier, of ship surgeons near the equator mistaking bright-scarlet venous blood for arterial blood.

#### In practice

His four-phase sun day: at sunrise, 30–45 minutes of red and near-infrared on bare eyes and as much skin as possible, feet on basalt or wet black sand; mid-morning (about 8:30–11:00), 15–25 minutes of UV-A on eyes and skin while moving, with no eyewear; at midday, when your shadow is shorter than you, 3–10 minutes of UV-B on torso and back while grounded, starting at 3 minutes and adding a minute every few days as you tan, and moving to shade at the first warmth. In the last daylight hour, spend 20–30 minutes outside with bare skin and no sunglasses to wind down (his sunset-viewing practice). Keep artificial night light and out-of-season food out of the picture, and if you are relocating to the tropics, arrive gradually.

### Red and near-infrared: the sun first
- **id:** red-nir-light
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse values red and near-infrared light highly — it rebuilds cell water, restarts the energy chain and lowers blood sugar — but says it cannot fix an unpinned body on its own, and that the sun is its only complete source.
- **protocols:** red-nir-light

Red and near-infrared light (NIR — invisible light just past red, roughly 600–1000 nm) hold a central place in Kruse's model. About 43% of sunlight falls in this band, he says, and it reaches deep into the body to power mitochondria. But he insists it is only one part of what sunlight provides, and that it only works fully in a body that is "pinned": grounded and sunlit under a stable local magnetic field.

#### What red and infrared do

Building on Peter Mitchell's account of how mitochondria make energy, Kruse calls red light the sun's "command channel" for light hydrogen (ordinary hydrogen, as opposed to heavy deuterium). Red light, he says, penetrates the abdomen to the liver's mitochondria and spins ATP synthase — the rotary motor that makes ATP, the cell's energy currency — at full efficiency on light hydrogen. The liver then needs less food fuel and keeps deuterium out; he calls this the "43% red light engine." He puts the energy barrier for smooth electron and proton flow down the energy chain at about 0.66 electron-volts, which he says matches near-infrared photons. He also describes Complex III's recycling loop (the Q-cycle) as a "deli slicer" that shaves light frequencies off the electrons food supplies.

That 0.66 eV energy, he says, literally accelerates the hand-off of electrons from NADH, the loaded form of the carrier NAD⁺, into the energy chain, turning it back into NAD⁺. When the chain stalls, NAD⁺ falls. He reads the NMN and NR supplements promoted by David Sinclair as a chemical attempt to get around that stall rather than fix it.

NIR's best-known job in his model is at Complex IV, the last step of the energy chain, where oxygen is turned into water. Nitric oxide can sit on Complex IV's copper–iron centre and block it. Morning NIR breaks that bond — his "photonic purge" — so oxygen can enter. Without it, he says, electrons pile up, oxygen flips into its reactive singlet form, and the cell can no longer make its own deuterium-depleted water. Infrared also lowers the oxidative stress that uses up vitamin C, and until Complex IV is running and the cell's water has changed, he says, no supplement works.

Order matters. Kruse says red and near-infrared first rebuild exclusion-zone water — the ordered, charge-holding layer water forms beside surfaces — and only once that layer is restored can UV drive the orderly spin changes the energy chain depends on. That is why he puts red and infrared at both ends of the sun day.

Red light also acts on blood sugar. Citing work with 670 nm light, he says blood glucose fell by 27% with no insulin, while blue light pushes glucose up as the system stalls to avoid overheating. Lowering blood sugar, in his view, means fixing the light and clearing deuterium; drugs that force glucose down are a chemical bypass.

#### Near-Infrared Deficiency Syndrome

Kruse coined "Near-Infrared Deficiency Syndrome" (NIRDS) for the modern lack of 600–1000 nm light. An ancestor sitting by a wood fire, in the sun and grounded to a strong local field, was bathed in it. Modern people live under LEDs that emit almost none, and the phase-out of incandescent bulbs removed the last indoor source.

#### Why NIR alone is not enough

Yet Kruse says NIR "lubricates rather than drives." It thins the water around ATP synthase and speeds Complex IV, but it does nothing to align electron spins, the job his model gives to the magnetic field. If Complexes I–III upstream are misaligned by a weak field or nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring), speeding up Complex IV only deepens the traffic jam and risks a heat dump; he likens it to flooring the accelerator with a crushed transmission. He calls NIR an excellent tool but says relying on it alone, as some near-infrared teachers do (he names Fosbury), breaks nature's rules: evolution added UV-A and UV-B sensing as a safety margin for a planet whose field varies.

He goes further for people who are unpinned. When oxygen is in its singlet state and NAD⁺ is loaded with deuterium, he says metabolism becomes blind to light, so magnetism now matters more than light — a catch-22 he calls rare in human history. Such a person can have NIRDS while standing naked in the sun. The precondition for using light is what he calls a "paramagnetic lock": membranes, cholesterol rafts and structured water acting together as an ordered antenna that keeps oxygen in its stable form.

Kruse also warns against a popular combination. Methylene blue shuttles electrons past Complexes I and III, and 670 or 830 nm light excites Complex IV; in an ungrounded person living under wireless and blue light, he likens the pairing to a turbocharger on a short-circuiting transformer and links it to testicular and ovarian cancer.

#### In practice

His red-nir-light guidance puts the sun first: get red and infrared from sunrise and daylight, and if you have hours of sun, a panel is redundant. In the tropics he skips light devices altogether. Don't rely on single-wavelength devices, which he says can harm; where he does use panels, they are 660 and 850 nm. For problems such as eye swelling he recommends grounding plus solar UV-A and near-infrared rather than panels, and he says not to combine methylene blue with red light unless grounded and away from wireless and blue light.

### Melanin: the body's solar semiconductor
- **id:** melanin-semiconductor
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse treats melanin as a light-driven semiconductor, spin filter and metal and deuterium trap that daily UV-A and a stable magnetic field must renew, and sees its breakdown as a root of many diseases.
- **protocols:** sun-exposure

For Kruse, melanin is far more than skin pigment. Found in skin, eyes, brain, the nerves around the heart, fat and fascia, it is in his model a "biological quantum semiconductor" — a material that turns light into electrical current — and a vault for metals. Keeping it renewed with daily sunlight is central to his approach, and he sees its breakdown beneath a long list of diseases.

#### What melanin does

Kruse compares melanin to chlorophyll. It absorbs a broad range of light, especially UV and visible, and uses it to split water into hydrogen, oxygen and free electrons. That continual splitting feeds a "wireless" current into cells that does not depend on food. Working with structured water (water ordered into a charge-holding layer), he says, melanin also sorts light hydrogen from deuterium, its heavy form.

That sorting has a cost. Splitting deuterium-heavy water, Kruse says, takes eight to ten times more energy, so an overloaded melanin stalls, bleaches or browns — his "Maillard-orange" shift. He calls melanin a sacrificial sink: it soaks up deuterium so the heavy hydrogen never reaches the mitochondrial motor, and it wears out doing so.

Its chaotic, one-handed (chiral) structure is, in his view, a strength. Through CISS — the spin filtering that chiral molecules perform — it passes on only electrons with the right spin, and its disordered make-up lets it soak up almost any frequency of electromagnetic noise before that noise reaches mitochondria. In the embryo, he adds, pigment-making cells travel with the neural crest along the elastic fibres of vessel walls, which is how melanin comes to sit in arteries and heart valves as part of their electrical insulation.

Melanin is also a trap and a buffer. Its chemical groups grab free iron and copper, which in his account stops the Fenton reaction (iron-driven chemistry that makes destructive radicals) and singlet-oxygen formation at the source. It buffers protons, magnesium and calcium like a capacitor (a store that takes up and releases charge), steadying the conditions mitochondria need to handle calcium. Because it is built from L-DOPA and dopamine, it soaks up excess dopamine before it can oxidise. During light famines in Earth's history, he says, dense melanin also locked away manganese and rare-earth lanthanides. Today he reads high heavy-metal levels as a sign that a person's internal melanin is failing, which makes them a sun and grounding problem: melanin, selenium and light-driven repair do the detox work.

In the brain, Kruse calls neuromelanin bound to iron in the substantia nigra a solid-state battery that holds local magnetic fields steady so incoming oxygen keeps its stable spin; nnEMF, blue light or dehydration deform it. A skin tan, he says, shows only the outer shield, and this "interior tan" matters more. A deep skin tan can hide a "browned" interior; his example is tanned but foggy shoppers on Rodeo Drive.

#### Renewal, and how melanin "rusts"

Melanin must be renewed. Without 380 nm UV-A reaching neuropsin (a UV-A sensor in eye and skin) and without strong "pinning" (living grounded and sunlit under a stable local field), the POMC pathway — the hormone precursor the body cuts into alpha-MSH, the signal that makes melanin — shuts down. The ageing polymer then soaks up amine toxins, chemicals and deuterium, its iron "rusts" to the oxidised ferric form, and it releases free iron and oxidised dopamine. The debris settles as lipofuscin, his "rusted melanin," which he says clogs the brainstem's sleep centres. Degraded melanin also feeds a breakdown route toward quinolinic acid that silences BDNF, the brain's growth signal.

Kruse describes the pigment pathway as a road that can run either way. With 380 nm UV-A reaching neuropsin and well-oxygenated blood, it runs forward: the amino acid tyrosine becomes DOPA, then dopamine and adrenaline, then melanin, with vitamin C keeping the enzymes' copper and iron ready. Without UV-A, or with poor oxygen, it slides back toward tyrosine and phenylalanine, because MITF, the master switch of pigment cells, can no longer keep the person pigmented.

That backlog, Kruse says, is the real toxicity. Excess phenylalanine and tyrosine crowd LAT1, the transporter that carries amino acids into the brain, starving it of other nutrients. They also undergo glycation (sugars bonding to them without an enzyme), which he ties to deuterium, and form a tissue "sludge" that people mistake for oxalate crystals. In his reading, oxalate is a symptom of the low-oxygen state, not the cause of the amino-acid pile-up.

When that happens, he says, the body breaks its stored melanin back down into dopamine, noradrenaline and adrenaline (the catecholamines), using them as emergency "dopants" to keep a baseline current flowing. He reads vitiligo's white patches, which he says begin as thickened skin, as this kind of liquidation rather than as autoimmunity.

Kruse reads a low vitamin D level as a stand-in for this melanin breakdown, not as a reason to supplement. Greying hair, he says, is a visible readout of the same MITF circuit, and once follicles repigment they again turn light into structured water. Rising homocysteine is another readout: he calls B12, folate and B6 light-absorbing molecules that need near-infrared's small energy kick to work, so in light-starved people — very fair skin, indoor lives — homocysteine climbs as the "check engine light" for a failing interior tan.

#### What blocks the signal

Blocking daylight, in his model, cuts the eye-to-skin signal that builds melanin. Sunglasses make the brain read darkness, so pigment is never ordered and the person burns at midday; contacts, sunscreen and daytime blue blockers do the same. Moderate tanning ability, he adds, signals melanin potential that sunlight and grounding can put to work.

#### Melanin and the magnetic field

Melanin, like oxygen, is paramagnetic — drawn into a magnetic field — so in Kruse's model making it inside the body depends on Earth's field. Over 540 million years, he says, melanin levels have tracked oxygen, and when the field weakens the internal melanin fails first, even while skin pigment holds for a while. That is why he tells people they must live where they can make melanin, and names places with an erratic compass, such as Hawaii and New Zealand, as hard on it. Depression, he adds, tracks low sun and poor grounding through this paramagnetic brain melanin.

He applies the same logic to melanoma. Australia's world-leading rates come, in his view, not from sunlight but from melanin overworked and "bleached" by deuterium in a weakening field and falling ocean oxygen. In the Southern Hemisphere he sees raising melanin as the only way to stay well.

#### Skin water and lotions

Kruse treats dry skin as a light-clock failure, not a shortage of drinking water. Light at night disrupts the POMC signal, and the eccrine sweat glands — which he describes as water-sorting organs wired to the melanin loop — stop building a film of structured, deuterium-depleted water on the skin and leak ordinary water instead. Ancestral skin, he says, made its own lipid-and-water film that turned UV into energy, so no lotions were needed; skin is hydrated from the inside, by mitochondria making water.

Lotions, in his view, make matters worse. Any layer on the skin changes the light that reaches it. Petroleum and silicone bases collapse skin water from its structured state (his "160") to ordinary water (his "78"); hidden UV filters breed singlet oxygen under LEDs; and, citing 2025 research from the Max Planck Institute for Chemistry, any synthetic coat smothers the "oxidation field" of hydroxyl radicals the skin makes at its surface. A single natural fat such as tallow or coconut oil is his alternative.

#### Melanin in deep time

Kruse also gives melanin a role in surviving planetary crises. Melanin loaded with lanthanides, he says, turns radiation into a direct current, letting melanised life "carry its own sun" through long darkness. That is his explanation for why bird and mammal ancestors survived the ash-darkened world after the K–T impact while dinosaurs, dependent on outside sunlight, died. He points to radiotrophic black fungi, like those found at Chernobyl, that turn ionising radiation into metabolic energy, and looks ahead to basalt soils worked by melanised fungi as the food-growing base of a weak-field era.

#### In practice

His sun-exposure routine is the main lever: regular unfiltered sun, with sunrise UV-A for renewal, and no sunscreen, sunglasses, contacts or blue blockers in daylight. For dry skin he uses morning sun, cold and grounding rather than lotion; after a shower he allows at most tallow or coconut oil, and never petroleum, silicone or UV-filter products.

### Blue light, LEDs and screens
- **id:** blue-light-hygiene
- **pillar:** light
- **summary:** Kruse blames blue-dominant indoor light and screens, stripped of red and infrared, for damage to the body clock, eyes and metabolism, and calls screen life "reality subtraction."
- **protocols:** blue-light-hygiene, screen-light-hygiene

Kruse argues that modern indoor light is one of the biggest drivers of chronic disease — in his view a bigger one than food. LEDs and screens deliver a spike of blue light with almost none of the red and infrared that come with natural light, and people take it in indoors, away from both the sun and the ground.

#### How lighting changed

He dates a turning point to efficient blue LEDs, whose inventors (Akasaki, Amano and Nakamura) won the 2014 physics Nobel. Built to emit only visible light, LEDs strip out the red and infrared that sunlight, firelight and incandescent bulbs supply — the longer wavelengths that, in Kruse's model, penetrate the body and power mitochondria. Phasing out incandescent bulbs removed the last indoor source of infrared.

#### What it does to the body

Indoor spaces, Kruse says, are both blue-dominant and "devoid of magnetic effects." That combination forces a spin change in the mitochondrial energy chain (intersystem crossing) that drives insulin spikes and insulin resistance; he holds that light and missing magnetic "pinning" raise blood sugar and insulin more than any food. A burst of red light while grounded, he adds, can blunt a glucose and insulin spike.

Wrong-way light also makes singlet oxygen, an energised form of oxygen that bypasses the body's normal safety controls. Kruse calls it a "molecular buzzsaw": it burns the fatty membrane rafts that anchor PER2, a core clock protein, which he names as the root of every circadian mismatch. In his model the body clock is physical hardware being damaged from inside, not an abstract genetic timer.

The eye takes the first hit. He says nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring), blue light and a weak local field damage melanopsin (the eye's blue-sensing clock receptor), mitochondrial DNA and heme proteins, dry out the retina's melanin and let deuterium (heavy hydrogen) into the eye's water. Contact lenses worn around the clock, he adds, trap blue light and EMF right at the eye.

Isolated indoor blue, roughly 400–465 nm, keeps the energy chain stuck on the singlet-oxygen path and browns the brain's neuromelanin, the pigment he treats as its light processor. Kruse sees the myopia epidemic, which began in Asia, as a whole population's eyes losing full-spectrum input.

#### When to block blue — and when not to

After sunset, blue light should be gone. But Kruse is equally firm that blue blockers of any tint, red or yellow included, should never be worn in daylight. There, he says, they act on the clock "like a drug": they change the body's own feedback loops in the same way a supplement or medication does, and they block the daylight signals that build melanin.

#### Screens and "reality subtraction"

Kruse calls social media and AI feeds "reality subtraction": algorithms that replace direct perception of the real world with a profit-driven version, exploiting dopamine while users sit ungrounded under blue light. The weaker the magnetic field a person senses, he says, the more marketing and manipulation get through.

A phone's blue light is, in his phrase, a "counterfeit timestamp": it tells the body clock it is always noon, so the brain's night-time waste flush never gets going. Fast scrolling adds strobing spikes of blue light down the optic nerve. In healthy brain fluid they would pass cleanly, he says, but in deuterium-heavy spinal fluid they scatter into noise that the brain's vomiting centre reads as a toxin, bringing on nausea and migraine.

Screens also spoil exercise. In his account blue light from TV leaves the clock genes and DNA weighed down, and training on top of that only multiplies the strain, so going straight from the television to a workout makes exercise partly toxic; he points to the hip replacements of ancestral-health author Mark Sisson as an example.

#### In practice

His blue-light-hygiene rules: swap LED and fluorescent bulbs for incandescent; after sunset, use red incandescent bulbs or glasses that block 100% of blue; never wear blue blockers in daylight; and by day, open a window rather than buy sun-imitating lamps. His screen-light-hygiene rules: cut screen and wireless time, don't go straight from TV or screens into a workout, and keep devices out of the bedroom.

- **sources:** [381130](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381130/), [383640](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383640/), [377239](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377239/), [382679](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382679/), [382553](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382553/), [384572](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384572/), [380518](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380518/), [379828](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379828/), [372047](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372047/), [370772](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370772/), [373261](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373261/)

## Water

### Deuterium and what to drink
- **id:** deuterium-and-water
- **pillar:** water
- **summary:** Kruse says healthy mitochondria make their own deuterium-depleted water, that deuterium can only be excreted and should be controlled rather than erased, and that it matters most once magnetic or electrical interference has already damaged the cell's engine.
- **protocols:** drinking-water, ddw-targets, sea-salt-ddw

Deuterium is hydrogen carrying an extra neutron, which makes it twice as heavy as ordinary hydrogen. Kruse calls clearing it "the real game" for human health, but he argues the body is built to do this itself. The main goal, in his model, is restoring that ability; drinking water with less deuterium is a support, not the fix.

#### How the body handles deuterium

In Kruse's model, Complex IV — the last step of the mitochondrial energy chain, where oxygen is turned into water — acts as an "isotope mass gate" that makes deuterium-depleted water (DDW). Healthy mitochondria keep their internal water near 90 parts per million (ppm) of deuterium, so only light hydrogen enters ATP synthase, the cell's energy-making motor. Deuterium cannot be converted into anything else; it can only be excreted. Sunlight carries no deuterium, he notes, which is why life built its energy system on light hydrogen.

Kruse stresses that deuterium becomes a problem only after the cell's "engine" has been deformed by magnetic or electrical interference: a weak or unstable local field, nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring), or artificial light. When the field holding the mitochondrial membrane together collapses, deuterium floods in and jams the motor. A strong, stable horizontal field, by contrast, sorts light hydrogen from deuterium, because their nuclei respond differently to magnetism. That, he says, is why coconuts and bananas carry no penalty in El Salvador that they would in Boston. Cutting deuterium intake is always wise, in his view, but sorting it well matters more.

Sorting has limits, though. Someone who grounds but keeps drinking ordinary water of about 155 ppm in a poor-field area, he says, will tan on the outside yet still lock up the inside, because deuterium keeps arriving through the gut.

#### Water in nature

Light water evaporates first, so heavy water rains out early as weather moves toward the poles, and high-latitude water and food are naturally low in deuterium. Kruse accepts this as the explanation for long-lived Siberians, though he doubts the benefit will last if the Atlantic's great overturning current (the AMOC) collapses. Yet he says northerners who live indoors under artificial light load up anyway, because a weaker shield and indoor life stop them sorting it. Warm, stagnant seas and desert surface water grow richer in deuterium as light water evaporates.

Big river floodplains gather heavy water too. The Mississippi's draws runoff from vast basins, and he says rain over regions where the compass points far from true north is heavier still, so local water and food carry more deuterium; he links this to the obesity he sees peaking around Memphis and Little Rock. Each source, he adds, has to be judged by its own hydrology: he rejected linking one bottled spring water to the South Atlantic weak-field zone because its source lies nowhere near it.

Deep "primary water," which the geologist Stephan Riess held forms new deep in the earth, has never evaporated and is naturally low in deuterium; Kruse credits the Nazca with tapping deep runoff for that reason.

#### What to drink

Kruse's choice is reverse-osmosis water with a UV stage. He warns against hard, metal-laden well water, which, together with heat and farm chemicals, he blames for the kidney epidemic among farmworkers in El Salvador. He ranks fluoride worse than iodine in an unstable field: it mimics deuterium, collapses structured water (water ordered into a charge-holding layer) and blinds the body's light sensors. Clean local spring water drunk in the sun is his source of minerals, and deep primary water from volcanic fissures or ancient artesian wells beats surface water.

For DDW he gives a range of 25–105 ppm, against about 150 ppm for tap water, and says how much a person needs is individual — as is whether to drink it daily or in cycles. It works quickly, he says, only when the body's own clearance routes are working. Feeling worse at first is expected in a deuterium-loaded body. He makes his own DDW by running a small current through zinc and copper electrodes in a decanter, which he says copies how mitochondria do it. He warns that commercial hydrogen water, made by splitting ordinary water, carries that water's deuterium with it.

He does not aim for zero. A vortex, in his model, needs a little heavy mass as ballast to catch and hold its spin, and water of pure light hydrogen would let ATP synthase spin out and lose its rhythm. He pictures the cell as a spinning top that needs some weight at the tip but topples if it carries too much: the goal is to control deuterium, not erase it.

#### Pipes and structure

Plumbing matters too. Copper pipes, he says, give water free electrons and expand its structured layer, while plastic strips its charge — his "plastic static trap" — and weakens the gut microbes' ability to sort deuterium. He extends this to copper drinking vessels: water breaks apart faster on a copper surface, light hydrogen reacts first, and deuterium is held back at the metal's oxide layer, so he says letting water stand in copper for 4–16 hours shifts it toward light hydrogen.

Older cities built vortices into their water systems; at the centre of a water vortex, he says, the lighter, thinner water gathers while heavier water is flung outward. Water cascading down a stepped wall restructures it through the Lenard effect (the charge separation produced when water splashes and breaks). He says Gerald Pollack's lab has now shown that DDW builds a larger exclusion zone — the ordered, charge-holding layer water forms beside surfaces — than ordinary water.

#### In practice

His drinking-water steps: filter by reverse osmosis followed by UV; don't rehydrate on hard well water; avoid fluoride; get minerals from clean spring water in the sun; prefer copper plumbing to plastic. His ddw-targets: 25–105 ppm in general, made at home or bought, paired with carbonated water, and checked by sending a sample to a lab. He treats deuterium as secondary — location and light come first.

- **sources:** [377200](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377200/), [377858](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377858/), [384740](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384740/), [382179](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382179/), [383948](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383948/), [381072](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381072/), [382942](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382942/), [378274](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378274/), [377577](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377577/), [376259](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/376259/), [372850](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372850/), [373387](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373387/)

### How deuterium jams the motor
- **id:** deuterium-atp-synthase
- **pillar:** water
- **summary:** Kruse's account of how deuterium harms the cell: it scrambles electron spin, pays a heavy energy "tax" to move, chokes Complex I, makes the ATP synthase motor stutter and stiffens the water around it.
- **protocols:** ddw-targets

Kruse argues that deuterium (hydrogen with an extra neutron) harms cells not simply by being heavier, but by disrupting both the quantum and the mechanical steps of energy production. Its main target is ATP synthase, the tiny rotary motor in mitochondria that, in his figure, spins about 9,000 times a minute to make ATP, the cell's energy currency.

#### Why the extra neutron matters

Deuterium is not rare. Kruse puts its level in blood serum at 12–14 millimoles per litre, roughly 150 parts per million, which is more than the calcium, magnesium or potassium doctors routinely measure. That is normal in blood; the trouble starts when it reaches the mitochondria's inner chemistry, where he likens it to a bowling ball hitting a desk fan. He points to two 2023 brain-imaging studies, by Bednarik and colleagues and by Niess and colleagues, that trace glucose using deuterium labels: the doubled mass that makes deuterium visible to the scanner is, in his view, the same property that disrupts metabolism once it builds up.

Deuterium has the same charge as ordinary hydrogen but double the mass, and its nucleus has a different spin. Kruse says that spin drifts out of step with the chiral (one-handed) proteins that sort electrons by spin, a process called CISS. He calls deuterium a spin-phase "wrecking ball": it scatters spin-sorted electrons and shifts the outcome of the magnetically sensitive reactions that depend on them.

Every chemical bond vibrates at a minimum energy, its zero-point energy. In Kruse's account deuterium's sits lower, so its bonds need more energy to break and it tunnels (slips through energy barriers by a quantum effect) far less easily. Light hydrogen tunnels almost "tax-free"; deuterium pays a heavy "tax." He says a single deuterium atom locks about 96 surrounding water molecules into a stiffer lattice and drags cell water from its structured, charge-holding state (his "160") toward ordinary water (his "78"). Morning infrared, he says, builds exclusion-zone water — the ordered layer water forms beside surfaces — whose walls push deuterium to the edges; nnEMF, blue light or weak spots in Earth's field collapse those walls and let it in.

The slowdown has a name, the kinetic isotope effect: heavier isotopes react more slowly. Kruse says it slows the enzymes of the energy chain and of fat burning two- to sevenfold, weakening the proton gradient that drives the motor and leaking reactive oxygen. Healthy mitochondria normally scrub deuterium by pumping light hydrogen in preference, but a rising load from food overwhelms them.

#### Complex I takes the first hit

Kruse names Complex I, the entry point of the energy chain, as its most deuterium-sensitive step, and links falling NAD⁺ levels to it. A heavy glucose load forces electrons through this bottleneck. Wireless exposure feeds the jam: citing Nora Volkow's 2011 finding that cell-phone fields raise the brain's glucose use, he says nnEMF disorders the water around proteins, the body answers with more blood sugar, and glucose carries deuterium straight to Complex I.

Under that load the cell switches to a side route, the glycerol-3-phosphate (G3P) shuttle, which drops electrons further down the chain through FADH₂, another electron carrier, without pumping protons at that step, so heavy hydrogen is left behind. He calls it "a proton thing, not a food thing." A healthy adult, in his ranking, runs full Complex I, which clear thinking needs; a stressed adult falls back on the shunt. Clearing deuterium and restoring grounding lets the person leave the shunt, and he says the mental haze lifts.

#### The stuttering motor

On ATP synthase's rotor, a deuteron (a deuterium nucleus) takes a proton's place, binds too tightly and lets go too slowly. The motor stutters, its parts warp, and in his phrase deuterium "strips the gears." ATP output and membrane voltage fall, and the cell drops back to fermentation, the Warburg shift. Kruse applies this to diabetes: pancreatic beta cells release insulin only when ATP rises, so a jammed motor leaves them full of insulin they cannot release — what medicine calls beta-cell fatigue, he calls an isotope jam. In the brain, he says, the same shortage of electrons is misread by reward centres as famine and drives cravings.

Drawing on Albert Szent-Györgyi, Gilbert Ling, Ilya Prigogine and Robert Becker, he describes ATP synthase as a motor that must stay far from balance. Deuterium pulls it back toward a settled, symmetric state, which for a living system that runs by constantly dissipating energy means death — his "internal rigor mortis."

The damage spreads. Deuterium-loaded NAD⁺, which he writes as "NADD+," produces pseudohypoxia: oxygen is present but the cell cannot use it. Its extra mass also disrupts DNA's protective structure, which he offers as the explanation for rising sperm DNA fragmentation. Bonds between deuterium and singlet oxygen, he adds, set like "molecular concrete" that jams fast enzymes, and he links nervous-system deuterium load to mental illness.

#### How the body fights back

In Kruse's model the body has several ways to keep deuterium out. The ten steps of glycolysis and nine of the Krebs cycle act as an "isotope centrifuge," harvesting light hydrogen and holding back deuterium before it reaches the motor. Drawing on Stavros Kassinos's work on vortex structure, he says spinning blood and spinal fluid fling deuterium-heavy water outward, where melanin in vessel walls catches it. Joints spin deuterium out by moving. When the inner mitochondrial membrane holds its full voltage, he says, deuterium cannot get in at all. He even says red blood cells gave up their mitochondria because of deuterium.

He borrows the power-plant maxim "the solution to pollution is dilution" for this. In any swirling flow the lightest isotope moves fastest, so light hydrogen races ahead while deuterium is flung aside and diluted. The spinning ATP synthase does the same at the smallest scale, throwing heavy isotopes away from the high-voltage membrane.

mTOR, the cell's growth switch, is in his account its deuterium sensor: a liquid-crystal "logic gate" whose shape is set by the surrounding water. A deuterium-heavy cell is locked out of both self-recycling (autophagy) and orderly cell death (apoptosis). He reads parasites such as Chagas as markers of a deuterium-loaded body.

#### In practice

The linked ddw-targets protocol calls for deuterium-depleted water in his 25–105 ppm range, paired with carbonated water. He expects people to feel worse at first in a deuterium-loaded body, and treats deuterium as secondary to fixing light and location, since those determine whether the body can sort it. His waking readout: warm in the core but cool on the skin means the membrane is back at full voltage, while waking sweaty or cold and clammy means deuterium is jamming the motors and their output is scattering as heat.

### Carbon dioxide: breath, sauna and carbonated water
- **id:** co2-brain-clearance
- **pillar:** water
- **summary:** Kruse treats carbon dioxide as a brain protector and deuterium flush, raised by gentle under-breathing, rhythmic CO₂ pulses, sauna and naturally carbonated water.
- **protocols:** carbonated-water, sinus-hum-reset, co2-pulse-breathing, buteyko-breathing, volcano-protocol

Most people think of carbon dioxide as waste. Kruse sees it as protective: in his model CO₂ shields the brain from magnetic degradation, releases oxygen into tissues and helps clear deuterium (heavy hydrogen). Holding on to more of it, through breathing, sauna and carbonated water, is therapeutic.

#### Copying the planet

Kruse takes Earth's behaviour as his model. During a decline in the magnetic field, he says, volcanoes release CO₂ that dissolves into seawater as a "bicarbonate blanket," changing the water's electrical properties and keeping iodine locked in the sea. He reads rising atmospheric CO₂ (he cites 370 to 426 ppm) and the roughly 5% greening of the planet as Earth's "dielectric booster" — a boost to its charge-holding capacity — as its magnetic shield weakens. His Volcano Protocol copies this response at body scale.

#### How CO₂ delivers oxygen

CO₂, not oxygen, sets the rhythm of breathing. Through the Bohr effect — CO₂ making blood slightly more acidic so hemoglobin lets go of its oxygen — CO₂ is what hands oxygen to tissues. Without enough CO₂ and the nitric oxide made in the sinuses, Kruse says, oxygen stays stuck on hemoglobin: a pulse oximeter reads normal while tissue starves. He calls this the "Deuterium Ghost" and says it shows as dark circles under the eyes and as fatigue, anxiety and brain fog. The less sun a person gets, the better their breathing must be, and he calls mouth-breathing in a high-tech office a "death sentence."

#### Waking the brain's waste clearance

Kruse cites Dr. Sephira Ryman's team at the University of New Mexico, who alternated room air with CO₂-raised air every 35 seconds for 30 minutes. This woke the glymphatic system — the brain's waste-clearance network that normally runs in deep sleep — while people were awake: vessels opened and closed rhythmically, as in slow-wave sleep, pumping spinal fluid through tissue and flushing out tau and amyloid and, he adds, deuterium. He stresses that the rhythm does the work, and says clearing that debris lets the brain's water restructure.

He reads Kochunov's findings of reduced fluid flow in the brain as a viscosity map. Blood and spinal fluid are thixotropic — they thin as they move — and deuterium's stronger hydrogen bonds thicken them. Where Earth's field is weak, he says, melanin cannot sort deuterium out, so flow slows.

He also points to Russia, which uses CO₂ in its space program and once sold carbonated water ("gazirovka") from street machines. In his reading those machines recharged a population living in high-latitude, low-UV "magnetic darkness": a Soviet preference for physiology over pharmacology that used CO₂ as a street-corner stand-in for missing sun and field. He says he spent close to $200,000 translating Russian biophysics papers two decades ago, and that this is why he drinks carbonated water.

#### Breathing less: Buteyko, Tummo and sleep

Buteyko breathing (deliberately breathing less) raises arterial CO₂ to about 40–45 mmHg without lowering oxygen saturation, unlike the oxygen dips of sleep apnea. In Kruse's account this dilates small brain vessels and, through the Bohr shift, sends more oxygen to the brainstem. In Tummo breathing it is the breath hold, not the active phase, that raises CO₂. He says self-hypnosis lets a person raise their own CO₂ to calm atrial fibrillation triggered by the vagus nerve, and that a Buteyko belt slowing breathing during sleep raises CO₂ and, he says, magnetic flux in the diaphragm, toning the vagus. A traditional sauna's real benefit, in his view, is its CO₂, not its heat.

#### Carbonated water and baking soda

Carbonated water with deuterium-depleted water (DDW) is his "gaseous isotope flush": it builds a CO₂–bicarbonate loop that draws deuterium out of spinal fluid and gut. Carbonation also dissolves minerals, making sulfate and magnesium more available; he names San Pellegrino for sulfate and Muszynianka for magnesium. The source rock matters: San Pellegrino flows through dolomite and gypsum and has its CO₂ added at bottling, while Muszynianka is carbonated naturally in volcanic Carpathian rock, and natural CO₂, he says, dissolves more of the rare-earth trace elements he values. Drinking carbonated water in the cold, he adds, strengthens the Bohr effect.

Trouble tolerating carbonated water, he says, signals deeper "lattice lock" (stiff, deuterium-loaded body water). Oral baking soda, his "bicarb hack," copies the roughly two litres of bicarbonate the pancreas releases each day, which in his model flushes deuterium. Citing a 2018 study, he says it signals the spleen through acetylcholine to calm inflammation, and he uses it for autoimmunity and for the sluggish gut that follows GLP-1 drugs.

#### In practice

The linked protocols: drink carbonated water with DDW, favouring naturally carbonated volcanic sources (carbonated-water); breathe less with Buteyko — a small breath in and out, a 3–5 second nose-pinch hold, then 10 seconds of easy breathing, for 5 minutes — and use a traditional sauna and, if desired, a sleep belt (buteyko-breathing); alternate room air and CO₂-raised air every 35 seconds for 30 minutes (co2-pulse-breathing); for a blocked sinus, hum, position the head, do CO₂ breathing and ground in morning sun (sinus-hum-reset). His Volcano Protocol combines a high-pressure water jet with a 40 Hz tuning fork on the spine, carbonated water, DDW and sauna, and barefoot sunrise on volcanic ground in a pinned Pacific Rim location.

- **sources:** [381076](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381076/), [375978](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375978/), [381025](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381025/), [379564](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379564/), [379565](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379565/), [381107](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381107/), [378191](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378191/), [375631](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375631/), [384898](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384898/), [371222](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371222/), [371751](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371751/), [373683](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373683/)

### Seafood, DHA and careful iodine
- **id:** seafood-meal
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse favors wild seafood for DHA and minerals, prefers cold, well-mixed waters such as the Pacific Northwest, accepts antioxidants only within whole marine food, and says iodine is safe only for someone who is pinned.
- **protocols:** seafood-meal

Wild seafood is Kruse's go-to food. In his model it supplies DHA and magnesium in a form the body can use, along with marine antioxidants bound into whole food. But he attaches conditions — above all to iodine and to where the seafood comes from — that depend on a person's light and magnetic environment.

#### DHA as a light conductor

DHA is the long omega-3 fat concentrated in the retina and brain. Kruse rejects the textbook view of it as a generic fat for membrane flexibility. Its chain of six double bonds forms a continuous cloud of shared electrons, which in his account makes it a semiconductor that turns sunlight into current. Packed into the retina, the brain's master clock and its links to the thalamus, it pushes surrounding water into a structured, charge-holding state, so these pathways carry sunlight's signal like fibre optics. He counts it among the "magnetic fats" lining vessels and brain, and says how much it helps depends on how damaged a person's mitochondrial membrane voltage already is.

DHA is also the raw material for healing signals. Citing Bazan's work, he notes that photoreceptors and neurons lengthen DHA into elovanoids and neuroprotectin D1, messengers that tell inflamed tissue to resolve. When the fat carries deuterium (heavy hydrogen), its bonds vibrate at a different frequency, so these "heal" signals arrive mistuned and weaken while the inflammatory signals made from arachidonic acid carry on — his "signal-jamming."

#### Where the seafood comes from

That makes the water a fish grew in matter. Kruse says southern-hemisphere seafood has become a concentrated source of deuterium: the water cycle leaves heavier water at those latitudes, and Australian aquaculture, industrial processing and seed-oil contamination of marine food chains add to it.

In the Atlantic, he points to the AMOC, the great overturning current that carries warm, evaporation-enriched surface water north, where it sinks and takes deuterium into the deep. As it slows — he cites estimates of a 15–50% slowdown since 1950 — deuterium builds up in US East Coast surface water, and he expects smaller, more deuterium-heavy fish in oxygen-poorer seas.

His regional ranking follows. The US East Coast is suboptimal. The Gulf is mixed: evaporation adds deuterium, but the Loop Current keeps the water moving. The Pacific Northwest is his preference, because he sees the Pacific's field as steadier and its cold, deep upwelling as lower in deuterium. Oysters show the trade-off: one filters up to 50 gallons of water a day, concentrating whatever is around it, so for people already deuterium-loaded an East Coast oyster's heavy hydrogen may now outweigh its DHA. He says he has switched to Gulf and Pacific oysters.

#### Antioxidants only as food

Astaxanthin, the red carotenoid in salmon and krill, Kruse accepts only as food — wild sockeye or krill — ideally after time barefoot in the surf. Food-form astaxanthin arrives bound to DHA and lines up across the membrane like a wire; isolated or synthetic forms, he says, insert crookedly and scramble the membrane's structure. Neutralising reactive oxygen also releases heat, which a healthy membrane can use but which melts a failing, deuterium-loaded one; he likens it to a hair dryer on an ice sculpture.

Isolated antioxidants also silence a useful alarm. Citing Martin Picard's work, Kruse calls brief bursts of singlet oxygen "mitoception" — the cell sensing its own distress — which tell the nucleus and the brain's clock that the local field is compromised and prompt mitochondria to make their own melatonin. Scavenger pills cut that wire. Reactive oxygen, in his view, is a readout of a broken spin filter, not a chemical mess to mop up.

Vitamin C, in his account, is the main guard of brain DHA: when it runs low, DHA oxidises and the brain shifts into an emergency low-oxygen mode. Whole-food vitamin C, together with fat-soluble partners such as vitamin E, protects DHA at the membrane.

He also explains why extra vitamin C pills often fail to raise levels inside cells. Glutathione, the cell's main internal antioxidant, normally hands an electron back to spent vitamin C (dehydroascorbate) so it can work again. If sulfur metabolism is impaired, as with a variant in the CBS or CSE enzymes of the transsulfuration pathway, homocysteine is not turned into cysteine, the scarce building block of glutathione. Vitamin C is then used once, breaks down into oxalic acid and leaves the cell, so more synthetic ascorbic acid changes nothing. He puts light first: infrared that frees cytochrome c oxidase (the energy chain's last enzyme) lowers the oxidative load eating up the vitamin, and he says nothing else works until the cell's water has changed state.

#### Magnesium

Magnesium pills help briefly, Kruse says, but a low-voltage cell cannot hold the mineral. Under nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring) and LEDs the cell's charge flips, it loses its grip on the ion, and the magnesium leaks out, causing diarrhea or a false calm. He compares it to delivering parts to a factory with no power. His source is wild marine food or local spring water drunk in the sun.

#### Iodine: powerful but conditional

Citing Küpper and colleagues (2008), Kruse notes that iodide neutralises reactive oxygen up to 500 times faster than vitamin C, and that kelp moves iodide to its surface under stress. He sees iodine as a heavy atom that steadies electron spins in the thyroid, the stomach lining and the brain's fluid. But in people who are indoors, unpinned and out of rhythm, singlet oxygen turns incoming iodide into radicals that damage cardiolipin (the membrane fat that holds the energy chain together) and drop the cell's voltage. Isolated or high-dose iodine, he says, sets off an "uncoupled halide storm." Inside the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), a large weak patch in Earth's field over South America and the South Atlantic, he calls iodine use right now "a death sentence."

Mercury, by contrast, does not worry him: the selenium in seafood is, he says, nature's built-in antidote.

#### In practice

His seafood-meal guidance: eat wild-caught seafood for DHA and magnesium, favouring cold Pacific waters over the US East Coast and the southern oceans; take astaxanthin only as food, such as wild sockeye or krill, ideally after barefoot time in the surf; get vitamin C from whole food; limit iodine if you are not pinned (living grounded and sunlit under a stable local field), avoid isolated or high-dose iodine, and take no iodine supplements while living in the SAA.

- **sources:** [382654](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382654/), [382483](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382483/), [381069](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381069/), [381070](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381070/), [381076](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381076/), [375606](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375606/), [382093](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382093/), [373427](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373427/), [372307](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372307/), [373025](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373025/), [373212](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373212/)

### Meal timing and the heme clock
- **id:** early-dinner
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse says when, and under what light, you eat drives diabetes more than what you eat, through a heme-based clock that should switch off the liver's night-time sugar output.
- **protocols:** early-dinner

Kruse argues that the timing and lighting of meals matter more for metabolic disease than the food on the plate. A steak eaten at 10 p.m. under LEDs, he says, drives night-time glucose output, fatty liver and type 2 diabetes even at zero carbohydrate, while the same ribeye eaten at solar noon in sunlight does not. His rule is that light outranks food.

#### The heme clock

In Kruse's account, the liver's night-time brake on sugar production runs on heme, the iron-holding molecule also found in hemoglobin. Heme production inside mitochondria follows the light day: the enzyme that starts it, ALAS1, peaks in the late afternoon, heme itself peaks around dusk, and later in the night the enzyme heme oxygenase-1 turns heme into carbon monoxide. Heme is the key that lets the clock protein Rev-Erbα switch off the liver's glucose-making enzymes overnight, and the carbon monoxide quiets the daytime clock genes.

Because Rev-Erbα must bind heme to work, Kruse calls it a sensor of both mitochondrial health and the local magnetic field. With morning 380 nm light and strong "pinning" (living grounded and sunlit under a stable local field), it rhythmically holds down inflammatory signals such as IL-6 and IL-1β. When its heme "rusts" (oxidises), it fails, inflammation runs unchecked and, he says, the body walls off the damage with adhesions.

Dietary iron, in his view, is only raw material: it sets how strong the heme rhythm is, never its timing. So, against Nick Norwitz and Harvard researchers, he argues that iron from red meat does not cause diabetes.

#### What a late meal does

Eating at night under blue-heavy LED light, Kruse says, disrupts melanopsin (the eye's clock receptor) and the retina–clock–liver connection, and flips oxygen into its reactive singlet form. The liver's energy chain stalls and the heat melts its structured water. Without the heme brake, the liver makes sugar and fat through the night and deuterium (heavy hydrogen) pours into the portal vein, his "Fatty Liver Sludge Shunt." Eating late also disturbs the spinal-fluid vortex through the gut's vagal nerves and blocks hormone release after dark. He adds that blue-light treatment of newborn jaundice disrupts this same heme clock for life.

#### Why breakfast matters

Kruse wants a first meal in the morning, at the peak of the cortisol awakening response. In his model that morning surge raises blood pressure enough to restart the brain's blood and spinal-fluid vortices, and it primes the pancreas to release its roughly two-litre bicarbonate flush, which sweeps deuterium out of the stomach and upper gut. Skipping breakfast stalls the flush, and deuterium pools in the upper gut, which he links to reflux and SIBO (bacterial overgrowth in the small intestine).

#### Never on a plane

Eating on a plane is the worst case. Inside a metal fuselage at altitude, he says, the steadying pull of Earth's field drops while cosmic radiation and nnEMF from wiring and Wi-Fi rise, so both food and gut tissue lose their magnetic footing. He calls it one of the fastest routes to pseudohypoxia — oxygen present but unusable — in the gut and brainstem.

#### In practice

His early-dinner protocol: eat the main meal near solar noon, ideally outdoors; eat nothing after dark; never eat on a plane; and eat a first meal in the morning near the cortisol peak so the pancreas's bicarbonate flush keeps running.

- **sources:** [383821](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383821/), [383820](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383820/), [380976](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380976/), [381099](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381099/), [374284](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374284/), [379445](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379445/)

### Food: substrate, isotopes and local information
- **id:** food-substrate-isotopes
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse says food supplies fuel and isotopes but not the magnetic field the cell needs; he favors fat over glucose, low-deuterium plant parts and fresh local food, and rejects any diet as a fix for a location problem.
- **protocols:** saturated-fat-fuel, zip-code-food

For Kruse, nutrition is secondary to light and location. Food supplies substrate — electrons and hydrogen to burn — but not the external magnetic reference that, in his model, folds and orients the mitochondria's inner membranes, and it cannot reach the light-run clock that governs fat synthesis. So no diet can fix what he calls a geometric problem caused by place, light and nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring). Cells, he says, count electrons, not calories.

#### Food imports spin and isotopes

Kruse reduces diet to one idea: food brings in electron spin states and hydrogen isotopes that either support or disrupt the cell's dynamo. What decides the outcome is how much deuterium (heavy hydrogen) the food carries, the spin of its electrons, whether NAD⁺ ends up loaded with deuterium (his "NADD+"), and whether oxygen stays in its stable triplet form. All of these depend on the field you are standing in, so without a stable local field even the cleanest organic diet ends, in his words, in a singlet-oxygen crash.

That is why he treats more fuel as no answer for a stalled network. A keto flu that never lifts is, in his model, a "Marcus crash." Keto shifts electron input from Complex I to Complex II, which depends on very precise spacing; under magnetic interference the fat-derived electrons pile up and the chain stalls (Marcus inversion — electron flow stalling when pushed too hard). By contrast, if switching to fat brings a felt rise in CO₂ and easier, clearer breathing, he reads that as fat doing its job.

#### Ranking the fuels

Kruse ranks fuels by their isotopes. Glucose is a "deuterium bomb," because plants pack deuterium into their storage sugars. Burning fat yields deuterium-depleted water and plenty of CO₂, and he calls animal fat the most deuterium-depleted fuel — keto, for him, is a hack of the body's water, not of its macronutrients. Protein is a fuel of desperation. Carbohydrates suit life near the equator under full sun. Fructose drains ATP and halts the cell's clean-water production; he says it evolved as a winter fat-storage switch. Because UV-B slows electron flow, heavy fat and protein under tropical sun fit poorly, and he calls keto unwise for cancer.

The same logic works inside a single plant. As fruit and tubers ripen, plants park deuterium in their sugars and starches while the leaves stay light, so Kruse calls it an oversight to chase potassium with potatoes, sweet potatoes or coconut water while trying to cut deuterium. His lighter sources are leafy greens, animal fat, avocado, salted nuts and wild marine foods such as squid, octopus, halibut, salmon and clams. Wild ocean fish beat freshwater fish, which he says carry more deuterium from still inland water.

Season changes the engine too. Summer, in his model, leaves metabolism loose and open to deuterium from tropical carbohydrates, while winter cold and fat switch on brown fat and a fat-friendly side route in the energy chain, so cells need less insulin. In real cold, he says, even honey at night behaves more like lard.

Saturated fats such as ghee, coconut oil, lard and clarified butter template structured water: citing Abha Sharma and Gerald Pollack, he says they expand the ordered, charge-holding layer of water, with cold ghee producing especially large ones. Seed oils, in his view, harm mainly an ungrounded engine. Egg-yolk choline helps the liver clear deuterium and improves sleep. And salt cannot rescue a deuterium-loaded system.

#### Carbon spin and corn syrup

Isotopes of carbon carry spin too. Carbon-12 has no nuclear spin; carbon-13 does. C3 plants — wheat, rice, trees and most temperate vegetables — prefer carbon-12, giving what Kruse calls a "low-noise" fuel. C4 plants such as maize, sugar cane and sorghum load carbon-13, whose spin adds noise to the electrons food delivers. Because commercial glucose and many supplement bases come from maize, he says they clash with a body tuned to local C3 food.

Corn syrup, in his account, is worse than sugar on three counts. Corn hoards deuterium because it loses little water through its leaves; enzymatic processing in ordinary water enriches it further, since each step uses up light hydrogen first; and the syrup is about a quarter unstructured, deuterium-heavy water with its sugars floating free. Crystalline beet sugar, from a C3 plant, is the lesser harm.

#### Soil, farming and glyphosate

Glyphosate acts, in his model, as a "dopant" — an impurity in a semiconductor — that breaks spin filtering. He also says it pulls magnetic iron out of soil: it props up crops in weak-field land but leaves them deuterium-heavy, and he calls it a melanin inhibitor. He holds up Mariangela Hungria's 2025 World Food Prize for microbial nitrogen fixation, now used on more than 40 million hectares of Brazilian soy, as a sign that farming inside the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), a large weak patch in Earth's field, has had to turn to microbes. The gut's microbes, he adds, have the same main job: depleting deuterium.

#### Fresh, local and in season

Food also carries information from where it grew. A freshly picked garden tomato, Kruse says, holds solar infrared, structured water and faint coherent light that the gut reads to set its clock; shipping and display under blue LEDs destroy all three. Hence his long-standing advice to eat within your zip code and in season — a tropical banana in Boston in December is, for him, a quantum error. Weston Price, he argues, mapped local magnetic dynamos, not a food formula.

He adds that deuterium-heavy rain from stalled ocean circulation can turn local food toxic. He redraws John Wesley Powell's 100th meridian, the old line between the humid US East and the arid West, as a boundary of good-quality water that has since moved east; as a sign he cites 2025, the first year most confirmed US tornadoes struck east of the Mississippi. Mississippi-valley water and food, he warns, are more deuterium-heavy than ever, and he says deuterium cuts crop growth by 40%.

#### In practice

His saturated-fat-fuel protocol: favour ghee, coconut oil, lard and clarified butter; eat egg yolks for choline; keep carbohydrates for equatorial life under full sun, avoid year-round fructose and never use corn syrup; don't make protein a main fuel (no daily protein powder, no carnivore in the tropics); and treat a keto flu that won't lift as a light and magnetic signal, starting with deep cold. His zip-code-food protocol: grow or buy food from where you live, eat in season, get nutrients — potassium included — from local whole food eaten in sunlight rather than pills, avoid maize-derived glucose and supplement bases, and avoid food and water from weak-shield zones such as the SAA.

- **sources:** [383819](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383819/), [378713](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378713/), [383640](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383640/), [381025](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381025/), [382681](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382681/), [383944](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383944/), [377752](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377752/), [375787](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375787/), [378705](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378705/), [384740](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384740/), [373386](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373386/), [372136](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372136/)

### Fat as a capacitor: obesity, fasting and the buffer
- **id:** adipose-capacitor
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse treats body fat as an electrical capacitor and deuterium store that protects heart and brain, so stripping it, or dry fasting without enough of it, backfires; obesity, in his view, is an efficiency failure rather than a calorie surplus.
- **protocols:** saturated-fat-fuel, coherent-dry-fast, glp1-damage-offset, anorexia-recovery

Kruse turns the usual view of body fat around. In his model fat is an "adipose capacitor" — a store that soaks up excess electrical charge and heavy hydrogen to protect the heart and brain — and obesity is a protective response to a failing energy system rather than the cause of disease.

#### Why the body stores fat

Drawing on Enzo Nisoli and Martin Picard's model of fat as a capacitor-like organ, and on Cameron Borg's idea of "energetic resistance," Kruse says fat builds up when mitochondria are stuck in Marcus inversion (electron flow stalling when it is pushed too hard). Under nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless and wiring), blue light and lost grounding, cells cannot burn the fuel passing through them. To avoid a blowout, the body routes the unburned fuel into white fat, which acts as a pressure-relief valve. Nothing raises that resistance, he adds, like deuterium (heavy hydrogen).

Fat also stores deuterium. Visceral fat and fatty liver hold heavy hydrogen away from core organs, and later the fat can be burned for water: citing Kenneth Mellanby's 1942 note in Nature, Kruse says 100 grams of fat yields about 110 grams of metabolic water, twice the 55 grams from the same weight of carbohydrate, and that this water is deuterium-depleted. Babies, he notes, are born fat to build the brain. He extends the shield idea further: a uterine fibroid, he says, protects the ovary from cancer, and fat cells protect the brain, by holding deuterium. Obesity, like cancer, may be a "safe mode" that buys time while the environment is put right.

Desert animals show the strategy. Camels, jerboas and desert ants store saturated fat and burn it for their own deuterium-depleted water, rather than relying on surface water that evaporation has made heavy. Their melanin-rich coats, he says, turn sunlight into the electron current that builds that fat. An animal that runs out of fat falls into what he calls an "atavistic shunt" and starts consuming its own organs. In a weakening magnetic field, he says, "we are all desert mammals," and should shift our baseline fuel toward fat to make the most of this internal light water.

#### Fats that burn on the spot

Not all fats behave alike. Medium-chain triglycerides (MCTs), especially C8 (caprylic acid), go straight from the gut to the liver and must be burned at once, so they deliver deuterium-depleted water and ketones on demand; long-chain fats can instead be parked in white fat.

Burning fat also routes more electrons through FADH₂, a carrier that enters the energy chain after its deuterium-sensitive first step. That, Kruse says, means fewer protons forced through a deuterium-burdened motor, less leak, heat and reactive oxygen, and intact folds in the inner mitochondrial membrane. It is why he calls saturated fat and cold "quantum engineering tools" for a time when Earth's field can no longer steady mitochondria.

#### The BMI paradox

The danger, in Kruse's model, is low fat mass. Heavier patients in intensive care survive infection and trauma because their fat buffers the surges, while frail, thin older people have no buffer left. Lean athletes lack the capacitor and can die suddenly under stress — he names spike-protein and EMF exposure — while heavier people survive the first hit. He calls this the "BMI paradox" and puts the danger zone below roughly BMI 20–21.

Forcing fat off with calorie restriction or GLP-1 drugs while the light and nnEMF environment stays the same strips the buffer and leaves the cell's voltage "redlined." Fix the light signals, clear heavy hydrogen and restore the energy chain, he says, and the body dissolves the capacitor on its own.

Calories, for Kruse, do not explain the epidemic. He points to US national survey data (NHANES) showing calorie intake roughly flat since about 1999 while obesity rose about 30%, and reads that as a failure of efficiency — rising deuterium in the food chain plus a disrupted light and magnetic environment — not a surplus of energy. Poverty makes the same point. If weight depended on being able to buy calories, the poor would be thin; instead the poorest groups are often the most obese. He reads this as field deprivation: cheap LED and fluorescent light, constant screens, dense urban nnEMF, and no time or space for sunrise or bare ground.

#### Dry fasting needs fat

A dry fast (no food or water) makes the body "drink" from its own fat, so Kruse says it only works if there is fat to draw on: not below about 20% body fat in strong sun (elsewhere about BMI 24), and not for thin people with MS. His "coherent" dry fast is done barefoot and grounded in morning sun, ideally with cold — which, he says, draws membrane layers closer so electrons pass more cleanly — and is broken with saturated fat. An indoor fast under LEDs and wireless backfires: no clean metabolic water can be made, and extra fat or ketones only speed the damage.

In anorexia, he adds, the fat that holds the body's light signal is missing, so leptin — the fat hormone he treats as the brain's report on stored energy — falls toward zero and the brain reads endless starvation.

#### In practice

The linked protocols: run mainly on saturated fat such as ghee, coconut oil, lard and clarified butter (saturated-fat-fuel); dry fast only with enough body fat, grounded in morning sun, ideally with cold, broken with saturated fat, and never indoors (coherent-dry-fast). For people harmed by GLP-1 drugs he gives morning sun in a clean field away from wireless, deuterium-depleted water at 90–105 ppm with frequent cold immersion, unshielded early infrared and UV-A, timed (2S)-hesperidin, and the oral baking-soda "bicarb hack" for gut damage (glp1-damage-offset). For anorexia he uses the same base with gentle 80–85 °F water dips instead of deep cold, plus liquid ionic magnesium and trace copper-histidine timed with morning sun (anorexia-recovery).
