## Magnetism

### Field direction, local geology and what global maps miss
- **id:** earth-field-vs-geology
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says cells respond to the direction of the local magnetic field and how fast it is changing, not its average strength, and that global field models and ground readings smooth those local changes away.
- **protocols:** magnetic-sleep-pad

Kruse argues that what your cells respond to is not how strong Earth's magnetic field is where you live, but which way it points and how quickly that direction is changing. Official maps average those local shifts away, so in his model a place can look normal on paper while its field is tilting in ways that undermine mitochondria, the energy-producing parts of every cell.

#### Direction matters more than strength

At any spot the field has a horizontal part that runs parallel to the ground (he writes it "Bh") and a vertical part that dives into it ("Bz"). In Kruse's model the horizontal part works like a shock absorber for the cell's "spin filter": his name for the way one-handed (chiral) proteins let through only electrons spinning in one direction, an effect physicists call CISS. When a local field tips toward vertical, he says, that filter fails even if total strength is unchanged, and a vertical-dominated zone has only a "razor-thin" margin when the compass direction shifts.

Declination, the angle between compass north and true north, is in his view a measure of mismatch. The body runs on two references at once: true north, fixed by the planet's spin, keeps time for body clocks and the rotation of body fluids, while magnetic north lines up electron spins. He calls the spinning planet the "stator," after the fixed part of an electric motor, and treats declination as the "error rate" between it and the wandering dynamo inside. As the gap widens, the two gears stop meshing, and the body's internal whirlpools (blood flow through the heart, the fluid around the brain and the mitochondrial motor) lose their timing, so heavy hydrogen is no longer cleared.

He applies this to real places. Citing the World Magnetic Model (the official field map used for navigation) and ESA's Swarm satellites, he says the US East Coast is losing its horizontal component quickly. The geometry he prefers is the one found nearer the magnetic equator: flat field lines, a strong total field and a compass that points close to true north.

The horizontal part also explains, for Kruse, why the Southern Hemisphere has always been the harder half. Field lines gather toward the north, which he calls the "input," and spread apart over the south, the "exhaust." Southern lands stayed livable only where a strong horizontal component acted as a "magnetic dam" holding protons in place, as in the belt from Perth to Sydney and across northern Australia, and he says that component is now collapsing.

#### Speed of change

Kruse puts even more weight on how fast the field changes. Geophysicists call the speeding-up of that change "secular acceleration," and sudden lurches "geomagnetic jerks." In the past, he argues, shifts were slow enough for each generation's body clock to recalibrate; today's pace is new. A chart showing a steady 50,000 nT (nanotesla, the unit of field strength) over a decade can therefore hide a jerk that tilts a horizontal field toward vertical within a year. A fixed offset between compass and true north may be survivable, he says; erratic wandering is the warning sign, because it means the outer core itself is unstable.

#### What global models miss

The standard models, the World Magnetic Model and the IGRF, are smooth mathematical averages built for navigation, and Kruse says they deliberately iron out regional accelerations. Mitochondria, in his view, register the local ripple as it happens, so the models produce a "linearization mirage" of stability. He sees the real driver as the "non-dipole" field: patches at the surface of Earth's core with reversed polarity, which he says are deep and long-lived (citing de Oliveira, 2024) and speed up local decay. Declination stays useful as a shortcut; he calls it a "geographic multiplier" rather than the root cause.

Timing makes this worse. The World Magnetic Model is refreshed only every five years, while non-dipole features such as the South Atlantic Anomaly move and pulse faster, so jerks like those of 2025–2026 appear only after the models are corrected. Ground readings have their own blind spot: local iron deposits, basalt and wireless fields form what he calls a "magnetic fog" over the core's signal, and the three-axis sensors in phones struggle to separate the horizontal from the vertical part. Only satellites above the crust, such as Swarm, can tell a global trend from local interference. Even they record strength rather than the field's twist and coherence, which he likens to measuring a tornado with a thermometer.

#### The brain senses direction

For proof that people register field direction, Kruse cites the 2019 Caltech study by Joseph Kirschvink and Connie Wang: alpha brain waves dropped by about 25% when the direction of an Earth-strength field was rotated, with its strength unchanged. He takes this to mean that the thalamus, the brain's relay center, works as a magnetic sensor. Because subjects responded to rotation in one direction only, he reads the human magnetic sense as magnetite-based and one-handed, with a blind spot that pole drift is now pushing many populations into.

Heavy hydrogen, he argues, dulls that sense further. He cites an *eLife* paper showing that a radical pair (two molecules sharing linked unpaired electrons, the basis of many proposed biological compasses) grows less sensitive to weak fields as "hyperfine" interactions with nearby atomic nuclei multiply. Deuterium's nucleus adds exactly that kind of noise, so in his model it blinds the sensor; he calls this the "hyperfine noise trap" and links it to birds dying as southern declination rises.

#### The rock under your feet

Local geology, Kruse says, modifies all of this. Volcanic or iron-rich rock carries magnetism frozen in when it formed and can hold a flat "micro-field" at the surface even while the regional field tilts; he names El Salvador's volcanic front and the iron-rich bedrock of New York's Adirondacks. Telluric currents, the natural electric currents in Earth's crust, travel along faults and fractures, and saltwater-soaked limestone can carry electrical noise for miles. For these reasons he judges places lot by lot rather than from a national map.

He sums this up as the "local stator" rule: global averages can no longer be trusted anywhere, and the rock underfoot decides. Because cooling lava records the field of its day, old rock can work against the present field. On Hawaii's Kīlauea, he says, compasses spin or point south because massive older basalt below set during the reversed field before the last full pole swap, the Brunhes–Matuyama reversal about 780,000 years ago, and it overpowers today's weaker field. Hawaii and New Zealand's North Island thus sit on a patchwork that he likens to standing on a reversed battery, with null zones and wildly varying strength: they have the mineral fuel (copper and iron for melanin enzymes) but a noisy ignition. He prefers thick crust of consistent normal polarity near zero declination, noting that Central America was laid down about 80 million years ago.

In practice, he tells people to look beneath the smoothed maps: compare Swarm data and secular-acceleration charts for their own zip code over time, and move to stable ground if the area shows a jerk or turns toward vertical. Where the local field is poor, the linked magnetic sleep pad protocol uses a static magnet pad at night as a personal stand-in, though he warns that a strong vertical field at night and a very different field by day can confuse the brain's sense of direction.

### Grounding, and why the ground matters
- **id:** barefoot-earth
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says barefoot contact with conductive earth feeds the body electrons that keep oxygen in its safe state, but how much it helps depends on the ground, your posture and the local magnetic field.
- **protocols:** barefoot-earth

Grounding, or "earthing," means skin-to-earth contact: bare feet on soil, sand or rock, or in the sea. Kruse argues it gives mitochondria a steady electrical reference that indoor life removes, and that this matters more as Earth's magnetic field weakens. In his model, though, grounding is only as good as the ground you stand on, and it cannot replace a good magnetic field.

#### What grounding does

Kruse describes the planet as a battery: the churning iron core (the geodynamo) keeps a large reservoir of free electrons at the surface, and the body is conductive enough to draw on it. Those electrons, he says, keep oxygen in its "triplet" state, its normal and stable spin arrangement, instead of letting it flip into reactive "singlet" oxygen. He adds that grounding restores Zeeman splitting (the way a magnetic field separates electron energy levels) in heme, the iron-containing core of blood and respiratory proteins, which he calls the body's magnetic antenna.

The result, in his account, is a steady electrical baseline for the inner mitochondrial membrane, the surface where energy is made, that bypasses swings in the local field. That is why he treats grounding as a stabilizer where the field is declining or shifting, and why he says sleeping grounded lets the body's night-time repair circuit run.

He links this to Robert Becker's "current of injury," the small natural current that steers tissue repair, and to a surgeon's chance finding that a weak current can reshape cartilage. The Hunza, he says, get such currents free from their mountains' steady natural field and the sun, which keeps their collagen and elastin supple. He pictures the grounded body as a pyramid, the brain at its apex catching light and the feet as its base on the earth; cut off the ground and, in his phrase, you become a "Maillard oven," slowly browning from inside the way food browns when cooked.

He extends the antenna idea to hair and skin, which he describes as a liquid-crystal "phased-array radar" tuned to the planet's field. Drawing on a clairvoyant's account of needle-thin blue rays (about 435–465 nm) standing at right angles from each hair even when the hair lies flat, he calls these rays a "topological surface state" and the lead wires for Becker's current. Deuterium, he says, "neutralizes" hair and thickens the skin until it carries this information poorly. Acupuncture, in his reading, works by inserting a better antenna: the metal needle acts as a "lightning rod" that re-pins the protons in local tissue water and bypasses the deuterium-frosted skin. He calls shaving a man's beard "magnetic castration," because it removes the anchor for those rays and, in his account, leaves the vagal exhaust unable to set his field right.

#### Only half the solution

Grounding fixes only half the problem, Kruse argues. Melatonin, which cells make at night as an antioxidant shield, needs both electric and magnetic containment. Grounding restores the electric side, but magnetic disorder from a weak field or wireless devices is harder to block. So in a magnetic "dead zone," or where declination changes steeply, standing on grass is not enough on its own.

#### Not all ground is equal

The type of ground matters. Kruse's concern here is not Wi-Fi, which water absorbs quickly, but low-frequency electrical noise such as power-grid leakage and stray currents. Limestone riddled with water channels and soaked with saltwater (karst) conducts that hum sideways for miles, so grounding on it near resorts or heavy infrastructure is, he says, like grounding to a live wire. He calls it a "voltage siphon" that drains the body's charge. Quiet rural karst is less of a problem, but he still rates Florida and Mexico's Yucatán as poor places to ground during the decline. Dense basalt, by contrast, insulates against that sideways noise and connects you to the steady local charge of the rock.

#### Posture at dawn

How you ground matters too. At the beach he prefers only the feet and ankles in wet sand or shallow surf, with as much skin as possible in open air. Water absorbs the near-infrared light of sunrise and, he says, acts like a shield, so neck-deep immersion blocks the dawn signal the skin is trying to catch.

His fullest version he calls "mammalian sphinxing": facing east at sunrise on all fours, so that hands as well as feet, with their sweat glands, touch the earth. The extra electrons, he says, let the body capture more of the dawn light, provided its water is in the structured state he scores at about 160 on the dielectric scale (a measure of how well water stores charge) rather than the 78 of ordinary water; sunlight itself pushes that number up. In his account this lowers the body's need to pull electrons from food and quiets mTOR, the cell's main growth signal, at no cost.

Even occasional contact counts in his model. People who sometimes ground or catch morning sun show a different disease pattern from those who never do, which he calls the "Earth–Sun circuit breaker": the earth supplies electrons that restore the charge of blood-vessel linings, while sunrise infrared frees cytochrome c oxidase, the mitochondrial enzyme that hands electrons to oxygen. He says people with collagen disorders typically get too little of both.

In practice, the barefoot grounding protocol asks for 20–60 minutes a day barefoot in surf, wet sand or dewy grass or on basalt, ideally at dawn, with basalt or wet volcanic black sand preferred. Avoid salt-soaked limestone near resorts or big power lines, check a spot with a meter if stray current worries you, never join dissimilar metals in a grounding connection, and use a low-resistance indoor grounding system as the fallback.

### Volcanic ground, basalt and passive anchors
- **id:** volcanic-basalt-pinning
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says magnetic volcanic rock locks in a flat local field and holds water and clouds in place, so grounding on basalt beats any device, and passive stone or copper anchors beat powered ones.
- **protocols:** barefoot-earth, el-salvador-guide, copper-ring-basalt, volcano-protocol

"Volcano Rx" is Kruse's name for living on, and grounding barefoot on, magnetic volcanic rock. In his model basalt holds a stable, flat magnetic field at the surface even when the planet's field weakens or tilts overhead, which makes volcanic regions refuges during the current decline. He ranks this above any device and offers passive stone and copper tools for people who cannot yet live there.

#### How basalt holds a flat field

Kruse starts from the rock itself. Lava from the mantle is rich in iron, and as it cools below about 550 °C, its Curie temperature (the point below which a mineral can hold magnetism), tiny crystals of magnetite and titanomagnetite form and line up like compass needles with the field of that moment. Frozen into basalt, they keep that direction permanently. More than 90% of volcanic rock is basalt, so large volcanoes and lava tubes create strong local magnetic anomalies.

Drawing on Yannick van Doorne's work on basalt, he adds that the stone's iron and magnesium make it paramagnetic, so it gathers field lines and concentrates them. The effect depends on the stone's tie to the regional field, though: spreading basalt sand through a house with no connection to the larger field, he says, "makes zero sense."

Where the regional field is tipping toward vertical, Kruse says, this frozen magnetism bends the field lines flat and creates a horizontal micro-field at ground level, a "lithospheric straightener" that holds even if the agonic line moves or the core weakens. Grounding barefoot on basalt then supplies, in his account, a dense stream of earth-current electrons that charge the mitochondrial membrane, help ATP synthase (the rotary enzyme that makes the cell's energy currency) repel deuterium, or heavy hydrogen, and support melanin. Beside this he calls PEMF (pulsed electromagnetic field) devices the "loser option."

Not every volcanic place works. Hawaii's rock, he argues, froze during the reversed field that came before the Brunhes–Matuyama pole swap about 780,000 years ago, so it can cancel today's field instead of adding to it; El Salvador's younger volcanic rock carries no memory of that flip. Active volcanoes pin best, because every magnetic field needs moving charge, and he likens them to superconductors that lock field lines in place. His own homes sit over water-filled lava tubes that he likens to a dynamo.

#### Cloud caps and black sand

Kruse reads the sky over a volcano as a gauge. The rock, he says, gathers the field around the cone, and water molecules, being tiny magnets themselves, are drawn in. A cloud that sits on the summit for long stretches, such as the "capilla" clouds over Nicaragua's Ometepe or El Salvador's volcanoes, is in his words water "pinned to the stator," and it shields the people below from cosmic rays. He says the Maya chose sites near volcanoes and cenotes (natural sinkhole wells) where clouds linger, because that water is lighter in deuterium, the idea behind the "living water" of Viktor Schauberger and Rudolf Steiner.

The coast adds a second battery. El Salvador's black-sand beaches are grains of magnetite and ilmenite that keep the field strength of the eruptions that made them. When salty waves, which conduct electricity, wash over that sand, their motion induces a small field of its own, so in his view the shoreline is a high-flux zone that resists the South Atlantic Anomaly and resets the protons of anyone standing on it.

#### Why El Salvador

Kruse calls El Salvador a "geological ark." It lies near the magnetic equator (about 13°N), where the field already runs flat; its fault zone and volcanic chain run east to west, matching those flat lines; and deep below the surface lavas sit slowly cooled magma bodies full of very stable magnetite that act as a hidden anchor. Together, he says, these work as a "spatial shear deflector" that keeps a weakening field from tilting vertical. He adds that the country has sat near an agonic line (zero declination) for about 11,500 years, since the end of the Younger Dryas cold period, and that all of it qualifies because it is volcanic throughout. Elsewhere, iron-rich bedrock such as New York's Adirondacks offers a partial refuge.

He also points to heat from below. More than 20 volcanoes have been active within the last 10,000 years, and their young lava and hot-water vents create local highs and lows in the field that, he says, filter out noise and steady the local signal; geothermal plants such as Berlín and Ahuachapán supply over a fifth of the country's energy. He drills his own wells into deuterium-depleted water from the volcano. Even the earthquakes help in his account: the regular shaking works as a "mechanical dither," a jiggle that clears background noise so the body's clock genes can pick up Earth's rhythm.

#### Ancient builders

Kruse believes earlier cultures understood this. He reads the six tons of perforated magnetite and ilmenite blocks found at the Olmec site of San Lorenzo as a deliberate grid to restore a stable reference field when the dynamo sagged. Megaliths, dolmens and long barrows of granite, basalt and quartz-rich stone were, in his view, "paramagnetic sponges" (paramagnetic meaning weakly drawn into a magnetic field) that concentrate a weakening field inside the chamber like a lens. Built on earth-current lines and faults, their quartz was squeezed by tidal stress into making small currents, and people sheltered or were buried in such stone during past excursions.

Pyramids, in his reading, were "reverse volcanoes." The Maya, he says, began in volcanic El Salvador and, as they moved north away from the volcanoes, built pyramids to copy their effect, with observatories beside them. He cites the Rhind Mathematical Papyrus (about 1650 BCE), the Great Pyramid's precise true-north alignment, a 2018 *Journal of Applied Physics* model in which a pyramid concentrates radio energy in its chambers and beneath its base, and Alexander Golod's large fiberglass pyramids. Built over cenotes and aquifers, such structures in his model spun deuterium out of the water table, leaving lighter water for drinking and crops. As the field kept falling after Egypt's Old Kingdom, Sudan's Kushites built 255 smaller, steeper pyramids (about 70°) to strengthen the effect; when the magnetic anomaly over the Yucatán grew too large for the Maya pyramids to correct, that civilization dispersed. He floats El Salvador's planned Bitcoin City, at the foot of the Conchagua volcano, as a possible modern "magnetic pyramid."

#### Passive anchors: stone and copper

For people not yet on volcanic ground, Kruse prefers passive tools to powered stimulators that push "harsh, non-native" current into tissue. While grounded, rest a smooth basalt stone or volcanic clay behind the ear or on the temple, which he calls a "zero-frequency amplifier," or keep a paramagnetic stone in the pillow. Another option is an open copper ring about 30 cm (12 inches) across worn around the head or neck. He traces it to Georges Lakhovsky, who in 1925 at the Salpêtrière Hospital in Paris placed a single open copper ring around a geranium infected with tumor-causing bacteria; in Kruse's account that plant recovered while the untreated plants died. He also credits Slim Spurling's copper coils.

In his model such rings are passive antennas for the Schumann resonance (the natural 7.83 Hz electromagnetic hum between the ground and the upper atmosphere) and for geomagnetic waves. They raise cell voltage, help cells shed heavy isotopes, and let copper stand in for melanin where it is thin. The neck is his preferred target, his "vagal vortex," where the carotid arteries, the vagus nerve and structured water run together.

He explains the open ring as an inductance–capacitance circuit, the simplest kind of tuned antenna, which gathers frequencies from the dynamo and the sun and concentrates them in one spot. Lakhovsky described sick tissue oscillating at lower frequencies, falling from 72 to 42 MHz; Kruse attributes that drop to deuterium, which in his view weighs down the natural vibrations of DNA, proteins and collagen. Lakhovsky's later Multi-Wave Oscillator, with its broad spread of frequencies, let every part find its resonance and, in Kruse's reading, shake the deuterium loose.

In practice, the barefoot grounding protocol says to ground on basalt or wet volcanic black sand. The El Salvador guide lists his preferred spots near active volcanoes such as Ilopango and says to test any lot with mosquitoes and candles before buying. The basalt-and-copper-ring protocol covers the stone, ring and pillow options, which he frames as a bridge while you fix your location. His Volcano Protocol copies a volcano's answer at body scale, pairing life in a pinned, low-latitude place and sunrise grounding on volcanic ground with steps to shake deuterium from the spine and build up carbon dioxide.

### Choosing a place to live
- **id:** choosing-a-location
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse calls location the final arbiter of mitochondrial health and gives three field numbers, a way to read the declination map, geology to avoid and on-the-ground tests for choosing it.
- **protocols:** relocation-checklist, el-salvador-guide

For Kruse, where you live finally decides how well your mitochondria can run: no supplement, diet or exercise plan can outrun land that has lost its magnetic footing. He gives three numbers to look up, a way to read the declination map and tests for a specific lot, and he says your location sets how hard you must work every day to stay well.

#### The three numbers

His checklist uses the World Magnetic Model, the official field map. Total intensity, the field's strength in nanotesla (nT), should be as high as possible: above about 45,000 nT, ideally up to 60,000. Inclination, the angle at which field lines dip into the ground, should be 0–45°; flat lines, he says, anchor the body's internal axes, while lines plunging steeply toward the poles destabilize them. Declination, the angle between compass north and true north, should be close to 0°. A line of zero declination is called an agonic line, and on it there is no twist between the planet's spin axis and its field. He also says to avoid the South Atlantic Anomaly, a huge weak patch where intensity falls below about 22,000 nT and which, he says, now reaches the Caribbean.

The best places, in his model, are where an agonic line crosses a volcanic chain at a latitude with strong sun: his "fountain of youth coordinate." He notes that the North American agonic line runs down through the Midwest toward the Gulf and lines up with the Pacific's volcanoes, but the Midwest itself lacks volcanic ground. Agonic lines move with the poles, so he says to follow them rather than latitude, and that the best places will never be fixed; there are "no islands or oases."

Even so, he warns against chasing an agonic line by itself. The goal is a place where the body can keep making melanin and sorting heavy from light hydrogen, which needs all three legs of his stool: light, water and magnetism. Picking a spot only because the agonic line crosses stable ground leads into what he calls a "Mayan trap."

#### Reading the maps

In his reading of the declination map, green (the agonic line) is best, red (east declination) acceptable and bold dark blue (strong west declination) very bad. Where the lines bunch together the field changes over short distances, which in his view shifts the alignment of mitochondrial membranes as you move around; that alone is a reason to leave. He compares his town's ESA Swarm satellite map on the same date across years to see which way it is trending.

The standard UV index, he says, is useless for this, because it ignores declination and the local deuterium load. Sydney and Perth can share a UV reading, yet in his view Sydney, under steadier field conditions, works like a "quantum processor," while Perth, on the approach of the South Atlantic Anomaly, is a "Maillard oven" that browns tissue. What people need, he argues, is a "Dynamo-Isotopic Calculator" that weighs sun, field and deuterium together.

Deserts give strong light but a weak magnetic background, and dry sand gives no ground, so he advises against raising children there. Some still-green land, he adds, already carries a desert-grade weak field; he names North America's breadbasket.

#### Faults and plate boundaries

The geology beneath a region can rule it out. Kruse singles out the New Madrid Seismic Zone between Memphis and St. Louis, which sits on a failed rift (the Reelfoot Rift) over hot, wet, weak mantle. Unlike the frozen magnetism of old lava, he calls it an active "induction zone": citing radio direction-finding research, he says the fault gives off broad-band electromagnetic signals under crustal stress, so the local field turns spiky and the body's repair window unreliable. He spent 18 months in nearby Cape Girardeau watching the zone affect people, which confirmed his 2013 move out of middle Tennessee, made before he had good magnetic maps. He is similarly wary of the Azores, where the North American, Eurasian and African plates meet, a junction he says is being loaded by the East African Rift shoving the African plate west.

#### Testing a lot

Before buying land, Kruse reads what lives there. Mosquitoes active by day as well as night, a 24-hour beeswax candle that burns out early and a faltering local water cycle all warn of a weak spot; he used mosquitoes and candles to choose his own property. A "smart" house full of wireless devices, he says, cancels even a good location.

#### Where he says no, and where he says yes

He rules out California, which he says has a "double deficit" of high declination (about +11–12°) and too little steady UV-B sunlight, and calls the West Coast and Texas "cooked," with the East Coast next. He adds Florida, the Louisiana coast, Tennessee, Kansas, Colorado (a mile up, closer to the radiation belts), Hawaii, Minnesota (a decent but falling field and only about three months of useful sun), the UK, Germany, Mexico's Yucatán and Australia. Florida's Atlantic coast is fed by the weakening Atlantic overturning current (AMOC), which for him is a deal-breaker, and as that current fails he expects people east of the Mississippi to load up on deuterium even when doing much right. Further afield he names South America, which he calls the most dangerous place to be because the anomaly is swallowing it, continental Europe, Japan (which shares Hawaii's Pacific-basin rock signal) and New Zealand's South Island, which he says worsened within a year of his visit. In the US he names only the Pacific Northwest. He rates the Hong Kong area, volcanically active at the start of the Ring of Fire, as good apart from its crowding. His own choice is El Salvador's volcanic coast, though not every spot there is equal; he avoids crowded, wireless-heavy beaches such as El Zonte.

#### After the move

Kruse warns that people can feel worse after moving somewhere better. Borrowing Robert Becker's idea of "adaptive healing," he says tissue has to rebuild for the new environment, much as running modern current through 1920s wiring can burn a house down. People of northern descent, he adds, should adapt to tropical sun gradually.

In practice, the relocation checklist gathers his criteria: the three World Magnetic Model numbers, avoiding the South Atlantic Anomaly, reading declination and Swarm maps, steering clear of stressed faults such as New Madrid, grounding on basalt or wet black sand, testing lots with mosquitoes, candles and the water cycle, no smart house, and ground-floor living. The El Salvador guide adds his local notes: buy an empty lot and build, favor areas near active volcanoes, and use the Costa del Sol beach for sunrise grounding.

### Electroculture: growing food in a weakening field
- **id:** electroculture-food
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** food
- **summary:** Kruse says a weakening magnetic field and deuterium-heavy water are cutting crop yields, and that electroculture, basalt and low-deuterium water can keep food growing where the field still holds, while melanized fungi keep the rain coming.
- **protocols:** zip-code-food

Kruse argues that plants suffer the same physics he describes in human mitochondria. As Earth's field weakens, soil water carries more deuterium (heavy hydrogen), photosynthesis slows and harvests shrink. He says lowering the deuterium in a plant's water raises yield by about 40%, and that raising it costs about the same. Electroculture, the practice of using buried wires, magnets, antennas and stone to shape the electrical and magnetic environment of soil, is in his view a practical, decentralized way to keep food growing through the decline.

#### Deuterium and the harvest

Deuterium in a plant's water, Kruse says, causes the same "kinetic isotope drag" it causes in people: enzymes slow, proteins and their water shells stiffen, protons tunnel less easily and the chloroplasts (the plant's light-harvesting compartments) lose efficiency. Lighter water reverses this. The plant makes metabolic water faster, its water holds charge better, it takes up nutrients more readily and it photosynthesizes more efficiently, which he gives as the basis of his 40% figure.

He expects photosynthesis itself to come under attack. Over the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), the large weak patch in Earth's field, more particle radiation gets through, loading air and water with heavier hydrogen and raising reactive oxygen in chloroplasts. A weak field also disturbs the plant's own magnetic sensors, the cryptochromes, and the iron–sulfur proteins of its electron chain. Plants, in his account, pay the same entropy price that mammals pay.

#### How electroculture works in his model

He points to Yannick Van Doorne's magnetic antenna: a galvanized steel wire laid north–south 20–30 cm underground and connected to a beeswax-coated magnet with its north pole facing geographic north. Van Doorne reports yield gains of 30% or more in the first year, building over three to five years. Kruse's explanation is that such antennas set up tiny vortices, telluric currents (natural ground currents) and magnetic gradients in the soil water that push the heavier deuterium aside, a plant version of the burst of deuterium-depleted metabolic water that ketones give a person.

He traces the roots to Rudolf Steiner's biodynamic farming and Viktor Schauberger's work on vortexing water and avoiding steel tools that disturb its structure. Neither used the word deuterium, he says, but both were chasing the same coherent, low-deuterium water. He adds that placing basalt, or minerals such as jade, in particular geometries raises the dielectric constant (charge-holding capacity) of water in soil or in the body. Steiner knew it, he says, and growers will have to relearn it to feed people in a magnetic decline. He says he raised these ideas with President Bukele at the 2021 Mizata event and later pitched them to the Bukele administration as a "big idea." The pyramids of the ancient world, built over aquifers and cenotes, are in his reading the monumental version: devices that concentrated the field into the water table to yield lighter water for crops.

#### Plants as field sensors

Kruse treats plants as living sensors of what he calls the "Universal Stator," his term for the field that organizes living systems. Building on a member's note that the ash of C4 plants is far more magnetic than that of C3 plants, he says C4 species such as maize, sorghum and tropical grasses carry two to four times more iron oxide and raise the soil's magnetic susceptibility (how strongly it responds to a magnet) by 30–40% after burning. He calls them "magnetic athletes," built for a strong dynamo. C3 crops such as wheat and rice are the plant kingdom's "safe mode," and their dominance on the Great Plains is, to him, the signal of the field's "Siberian Jerk" and the SAA's spread, compounded by the oxygen-poor, isotopically heavy water of a failing Atlantic overturning current (AMOC). Glyphosate, he says, chelates (binds and strips) the soil's magnetic iron; it keeps the crops alive but leaves them high in deuterium and low in what he calls magnetic torque.

He reads the map the same way. John Wesley Powell marked the 100th meridian as the line between the humid East and the arid West, a warning Kruse dates to 1860, a year after the Carrington Event. Powell took the line as fixed; Kruse says it is tied to the dynamo and is now moving east; he notes that in 2025, for the first time, most confirmed US tornadoes struck east of the Mississippi. The Mississippi valley's water, he says, is more deuterium-heavy than ever, so the food it grows will be too.

#### Fungi as the backup rainmakers

When the field fails, Kruse says, the biosphere reroutes through older, radiation-hardened life. He cites a new Science Advances paper, summarized in Anton Petrov's April 24, 2026 video and backed by work in PNAS, reporting that Mortierellaceae fungi release soluble ice-nucleating proteins into the soil. The fungi acquired the gene for these proteins from bacteria (a relative of the bacterial InaZ gene) by horizontal gene transfer. The proteins form complexes of more than 100 units, drift into the air and seed ice, and so rain, at warmer temperatures than volcanic mineral dust, and could one day replace silver iodide in cloud seeding.

His addition is the "missing layer": melanin-armored fungi survived the weak-field intervals of the Neoproterozoic, about a billion years ago, and keep the water cycle running when the dynamo falters. Fungi weather basalt aggressively, so he expects basalt soils and melanized fungi to become nature's geoengineering stack in a decline, with measurably more soil fungi inside the anomaly. Mammals, by contrast, pay what he calls a "POMC/SCN time-over-energy tax," which he sees in rising autism, Alzheimer's, heart disease, cancer, mental illness and obesity.

#### Where it works

Electroculture is not a fix everywhere. Asked whether biodynamic vortexed preparations, spiral horns or field-broadcasting "towers of power" could help farms, Kruse says they become superfluous where the field has already gone; the wise watch what happens to living things in the ground and adjust. He sends growers to basalt-rich regions with magnetic promise, such as El Salvador or Iceland, where he says C4-type life still thrives without chemical help. He says two growers he lectured, Billy and Logan, tested his claims in the US, one of them in North Carolina, in what he calls "magnetic excursion land," found them borne out, and now plan to bring their methods to El Salvador, his "ARK," which he predicts will become North America's new Midwest.

#### In practice

Kruse's zip-code-food approach applies here: eat food grown where the field and water are good, and avoid crops from weak-field zones. He says he would not eat from the Canadian prairie or the US breadbasket now and that North Americans should be eating from the Pacific. For growers in a still-good field, he points to electroculture antennas, basalt in the soil, low-deuterium water and vortexed water, with setups mapped against SAA contours and basalt soils. Where the field has already failed, he says the better move is to relocate rather than spend on vortex preparations and towers. The same deuterium-depletion tools he uses for people, ketones from fat and deuterium-depleted water, are in his view the human side of the same fix.

### Haplogroup and the "magnetic zipcode"
- **id:** haplogroup-zipcode
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says your maternal mitochondrial lineage sets the gearing of your energy engine and so decides which local field and sun suit you, and a mismatch speeds aging.
- **protocols:** four-phase-sun-day, relocation-checklist

A haplogroup is a maternal lineage traced through mitochondrial DNA, which passes from mother to child. Kruse calls it the blueprint and your "magnetic zipcode," the field where you live, the dial. When the two don't match, he argues, aging speeds up, so the same place can suit one person and harm another.

He frames it as a chain: the haplogroup is the blueprint, Marcus theory (the physics of how fast electrons jump between molecules) is the law, the magnetic zipcode is the dial, and disease is the failure that follows a mismatch. Lineages arose, he says, as answers to past changes in Earth's field. As people left Africa for colder climates, the M and N lineages picked up changes that let some of the mitochondria's energy leak away as heat instead of being stored, a trait called "uncoupling" that helps in the cold.

#### Isotopic gearboxes

Kruse calls lineages "isotopic gearboxes." Each one, in his model, set the gear ratio of ATP synthase, the cell's rotary energy motor, to suit local conditions. Tropical L lineages run it flat out, at a speed he puts at 9,000 RPM, which needs strong light and free-flowing, light water; northern lineages such as H and J are deliberately "leaky," dropping to about 6,000 RPM and turning the difference into heat. He goes as far as sketching a "human Lagrangian," an equation in which field strength, melanin and solar dose set the ratio of light to heavy hydrogen a cell should hold at a given spot on Earth, and he thinks lineages were shaped by it.

#### Different tolerances

Kruse ranks lineages by how they cope as the field weakens. African L lineages, especially L1, evolved near the equator, where the field is flat and naturally weak, so in his model they function with little magnetic help. Declination is a separate matter: for an L lineage, he says, 2–4° of offset between compass and true north can be enough for a stage 4 cancer.

The European H lineage, his own, is "leaky": when magnetic pinning slips it can drop its voltage and shed the surplus as heat, which he says clears deuterium and resets the cell. East and Southeast Asian D5b1 and F lineages are the most vulnerable. They stayed where strong sun and the dense magnetic pinning of the Pacific "Ring of Fire" volcanoes were constant, so they have neither L1's low-field tolerance nor H's heat release. Lineages that crossed the Bering region (D1–D4, A, B and C) were, by contrast, hardened by intense high-latitude conditions.

Mismatch runs both ways. Move an L-lineage person to Canada, with its high declination and weak UV, and the fast engine stalls; he points to Somali communities in Detroit and Toronto. Northern lineages (H, J, T, U and K) placed under strong UV or heavy radiation, such as the anomaly's fringe in Australia or Brazil, overheat instead into what he calls a "singlet flashover," which he links to melanoma and rapid aging; for this reason he warned one H-lineage member not to move to Hawaii. As the field shifts, he adds, places that once supported a full-speed engine now support only the slower one, which he blames for the broad rise in chronic disease, from obesity to anxiety and depression.

For D5b1 and F in a weak field he says cold therapy backfires. They should avoid deep cold (water around 55–65 °F) until they are magnetically pinned, first maximizing morning UV and infrared light, grounding fully and shielding strictly from wireless fields.

#### Running the numbers

Kruse turns this into a personal sum with three inputs: latitude and altitude (how much sun you get and how close you sit to the radiation belts), local field strength and haplogroup. Below roughly 30,000 nT, as under the South Atlantic Anomaly, he says cells need a longer quiet spell to complete repair. If a place demands about seven seconds of that quiet time and your body can hold only three, you must cut your deuterium load, and he says to redo the sum every month. A strong field alone is not enough: for a member at 48°N in Europe, probably of a Mediterranean lineage, he called the 48,810 nT field "elite" and its declination steady, yet the sun is too weak for most of the year to keep melanin production running.

#### Northern bodies and the field

Kruse says northern peoples shed skin melanin, the pigment he treats as a spin filter, and leaned on strong polar field lines instead. He calls this "Zeeman outsourcing," after the Zeeman effect by which a magnetic field splits electron energy levels. A strong polar field plus cold, he argues, let them grow larger, using the body as a bigger solar panel. Coming out of the Laschamp excursion about 41,000 years ago, he says, humans had smaller, wetter, more melanin-rich brains. He also reads Weston A. Price's famous survey of traditional diets as a map of local magnetic conditions rather than a list of recipes.

#### Moving to the tropics

A northern lineage moving into strong tropical sun must, in his view, adapt gradually. A sudden plunge into equatorial UV can overwhelm a leaky H mitochondrion, while stepping through the day's light and grounding on basalt or wet black sand builds a "solar callus," skin and tissue adapted to strong sun, without changing DNA. Redness, he says, means you pushed too far.

In practice, the four-phase sun day protocol lays out that schedule: sunrise red and infrared light with feet on basalt or wet black sand, mid-morning UV-A, a few minutes of midday UV-B that grows slowly as you tan, and late-day sun, never to the point of redness. The relocation checklist reminds you that your maternal haplogroup sets how much declination you can tolerate and that latitude, field strength and lineage have to be weighed together.

### The South Atlantic Anomaly, muons and failing regions
- **id:** magnetosphere-saa-deuterium
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says Earth's weakening field, worst under the expanding South Atlantic Anomaly and across the Southern Hemisphere, lets in particles that load the body with deuterium, and names the regions he sees failing.
- **protocols:** track-your-field

Kruse argues that Earth's magnetic shield is weakening and that the damage is worst under the South Atlantic Anomaly, a huge and growing patch where the field is weakest. In his model the thinner shield lets in particles that load bodies, water and food with heavy hydrogen, and he names the regions he expects to fail first.

#### Failing shields

Kruse says three "cosmic shields" are failing together: a filter of interstellar hydrogen around the solar system, the heliosphere (the Sun's protective bubble) and the geodynamo (the field made by Earth's core). He puts the loss so far at about 10% of field strength, falling roughly 5% a decade, and ties rising epidemics to the years since 1859, when the magnetic pole began moving fast, with a sharp rise after 1940. Another 10–30% drop would, he says, be an evolutionary bottleneck, and his main worry is a 30–50% loss within 50 years.

#### The anomaly

The South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA) is where the shield is thinnest, below about 22,000 nT against the 45,000 nT or more he looks for. Citing ESA's Swarm satellites, he says it has split into two lobes: the main low drifting west over South America, deepest near Asunción in Paraguay and Foz do Iguaçu in Brazil, and a second stretching toward southwest Africa and Cape Town. ESA reports that over 11 years of Swarm data it has expanded by close to half of continental Europe's area, and he says it now covers all of Argentina. Its northern edge reaches the Lesser Antilles, including Dominica, which is why he treats the Caribbean as off-limits. Under it, he says, the radiation belts reach down to about 200 km, so residents live in low-orbit conditions without knowing it: "astronauts walking on Earth."

#### Muons and deuterium

His key study is a paper by Augusto and colleagues. Under the anomaly the "cutoff rigidity," the minimum energy a cosmic particle needs to get through the field, collapses, and muons (heavy cousins of electrons made when cosmic rays strike the air) rise up to tenfold at ground level. He reads this as the Van Allen radiation belts sagging closer to the surface. Muons pass through walls and tissue, and in his model act as positive "dopants" that cancel the negative charge of cell membranes and knock light hydrogen out of its channels, so deuterium (heavy hydrogen) builds up in the basal ganglia, heart and liver.

He describes a healthy field as a "janitor" that sweeps deuterium to the outer edge of the body's whirlpools, such as blood flow and spinal fluid, where it can be cleared. Under the anomaly the janitor is "on strike": deuterium settles in nerves and in the brain's fluid spaces, and the body's water slides from its structured state (160 on his scale) toward ordinary water (78).

The lower belts also, he says, change how moisture is ionized in the air, raising deuterium in rain and so in grass and groundwater, and water's deuterium content will swing more widely as the dynamo fails. The Atlantic's great overturning current (AMOC) runs straight through the anomaly, which he calls a "ground fault" that makes it harder for the current to sink near the poles; a stagnant ocean means stagnant air and deuterium-heavy rain. Cosmic neutrons striking nitrogen make tritium, the radioactive form of hydrogen, which rains onto South American farms and rivers. The body builds it into DNA as if it were ordinary hydrogen; when it decays into helium, which cannot bond, the chemical link snaps from the inside. For this reason he avoids food from the SAA, and he says seeds germinate poorly in its weak field. Plants pay as well: heavier water slows the enzymes of photosynthesis and lowers chlorophyll, he says, citing work on algae grown in heavy water, while extra cosmic radiation damages chloroplasts; fungi, which do not photosynthesize, sidestep the problem.

#### The Southern Hemisphere

Kruse treats the whole Southern Hemisphere as exposed. Field lines gather toward the north, his "input," and spread out over the south, the "exhaust," so as the main field weakens the anomaly behaves like a low-pressure hole drawing deuterium and tritium down from the upper atmosphere, which he calls "isotopic insufflation." With the radiation belts lower there since 1859, he says, the southern food chain has been loading with deuterium for about 150 years, and he now reports sharp rises in Australia, South America and West Africa. In his reading the Indian Ocean and Australia's west coast are already hit, with Sydney's side next. He no longer considers anywhere in the Southern Hemisphere habitable for the long term, and he says the north magnetic pole's rush toward Siberia, his "Siberian jerk," adds strain to the southern weak spot like one side of a tug-of-war.

#### Who fails first

Zip codes near the expanding anomaly, Kruse predicts, will fail first: early-onset Alzheimer's and Parkinson's, cardiomyopathy and arrhythmia, grade 4 cancers and shorter lives. Parkinson's in particular, he says, strikes where magnetic flux is weakest. He calls the West Coast and Texas "cooked" and the East Coast next; people there, he says, sense something has changed without being able to name it. California has a "double deficit" of high declination and too little steady UV-B sun. He adds Florida, which he says the change "swallowed" in about 14 years, and the Louisiana coast, says Australia is losing field strength locally, and names the Pacific Northwest as the only US option.

The fittest, he says, go first: high-demand athletes collapsing without explanation are the canaries, and he predicts more sudden collapses from the Midwest to the East Coast. He reports cardiac deaths soaring in Brazil, with new arrhythmia diagnoses up 600%, cites a 2026 study by Behrens finding that the anomaly reversed the usual link between space weather and violence, and says wound healing is becoming poor enough that surgeons should avoid elective operations. He explains the hantavirus deaths aboard the cruise ship MV Hondius as four stressors stacked together: rising CO₂ worldwide, the anomaly's weak field regionally, the ship's radar and Wi-Fi locally, and a virus strain able to exploit the resulting breakdown in the blood-vessel lining.

In practice, the track-your-field protocol says to watch declination drift, secular-acceleration ("jerk") charts and Swarm data for your own zip code, comparing the same date across years, along with unusual blocked weather and your own heart-rate variability. If your area shows a jerk or turns toward a vertical field, move before official charts catch up. He also keeps a watch-list for a bigger shift: field loss reaching 30–50% of the Laschamp collapse, sudden changes in the SAA and faster pole drift.

### Solar storms, the Schumann signal and the "thalamic GPS"
- **id:** solar-flares-space-weather
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says the thalamus works as a position sensor locked to Earth's field and resonance, which solar storms and high declination knock out of alignment, with effects on sleep, mood, hearing and even earthquakes.
- **protocols:** space-weather-days, brain-eye-ear-conditions

Kruse argues that the brain keeps track of where the body is by locking onto Earth's magnetic field and its natural electromagnetic rhythm. Solar storms, and more slowly a skewed local field, knock that internal position sense out of alignment, which in his model explains storm headaches, a cluster of heart, ear and immune symptoms, and agoraphobia.

#### The Sun and the thalamic GPS

Kruse treats the Sun as one node on a galactic electrical circuit, with its 11-year cycle an induced response. In the body the receiver is the thalamus, the brain's relay center. He calls it a "thalamic GPS," a three-axis position panel whose axes he places along the sphenoid bone at the base of the skull, the vagus nerve and the heart. It locks onto the Schumann resonance, the 7.83 Hz electromagnetic hum of the space between the ground and the upper atmosphere, and onto the horizontal and vertical parts of the field. The brain's alpha rhythm, around 10 Hz, acts in his model like a strobe that keeps cell membranes aligned.

#### What a storm does

A solar flare or coronal mass ejection (CME, a burst of charged solar material) compresses the magnetosphere, the field's protective bubble, and drives an induced surge through the ground. Kruse says this jams the Schumann signal and uncouples the thalamic GPS, disturbing alpha waves. Cerebrospinal fluid, in his account, loses its charge, thickens and drains poorly, so pressure inside the skull rises; hence storm headaches. He reads glioma patients' storm-time seizures and brain swelling as field events rather than random inflammation, and tells members that a bad night can come from a CME rather than from what they ate.

Melatonin takes a hit too. About 95% of it, he says, is made inside mitochondria, and he cites a paper showing levels fall 15–33 hours after a geomagnetic spike, which he calls an "isotopic hangover." The mind is affected as well: he leans on Robert Becker's 1960s work with Friedman and Bachman showing psychiatric admissions, especially for bipolar disorder and schizophrenia, spiking during and after storms, and on Soviet research gathered under the heading of heliogeophysics and medicine.

The ground answers as well. He calls earthquakes, such as two in Panama during a recent storm, detectors of magnetic anomalies, and cites 2025–2026 statistical studies finding that quakes worldwide rise about 27–28 days after major geomagnetic storms; the storm's currents in the ground, he says, seep into fragile faults and warm them. He suggests that surges in psychiatric admissions in geologically active regions could serve as an early warning of tectonic trouble.

He adds that the field also shifts over each ordinary day. Sunlight charges the upper atmosphere, whose currents nudge the ground field by roughly 10–100 nT; after sunset those currents fade and the field settles to a quieter night baseline. As an extreme storm he cites the 1859 Carrington Event, when auroras reached the Caribbean, and ties it to the collapse of European vineyards in the years that followed. Yet he does not see a big storm as purely bad: the Sun, as the parent oscillator, may in his view send a Carrington-scale ejection that works like a defibrillator, jolting the wobbling core back into steady flow.

#### Slow uncoupling from the local field

High declination, Kruse says, does the same thing slowly. A field twisted relative to true north, or changing quickly, keeps the thalamus from locking a stable reference. That lowers tone in the right vagus nerve and makes mitochondria leak DNA, which the immune system treats as an alarm. His three signature symptoms of this uncoupling are heart palpitations, tinnitus and autoimmune flares. Tinnitus, he explains, is the sound of an "overclocked" thalamus hunting for its lost position signal; astronauts get it in the magnetic vacuum of space, and many people on the ground get it from wireless fields and the field's decline.

#### Agoraphobia as a navigation failure

Kruse reframes agoraphobia as this navigation system failing rather than as a fear disorder. The brain's grid cells and place cells, which map position, rely in his model on structured water held in order by the field. In ungrounded, sleep-short people living under blue light, that water slides toward ordinary bulk water and its capacity roughly halves, so position signals blur, which he calls "spatial aliasing." Open spaces carry more information than the weakened channel can handle, and panic follows. The fix, he argues, lies in restoring that medium, not in talk therapy or drugs alone.

In practice, the space-weather days protocol says to ground for longer than your usual 20–60 minutes during solar or geomagnetic storms and to expect possible storm headaches and a rough night or two. The brain, eye and ear conditions protocol gives his agoraphobia steps (remove wireless fields, ground daily, get morning solar infrared and lower deuterium) and, for Parkinson's, says to live where the magnetic field is strong.

### The planet's story: excursions, the magnetic forge and volcanic CO₂
- **id:** geomagnetic-excursions-evolution
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse reads Earth's field history — excursions, ozone, oxygen, oceans and volcanoes — as the large-scale version of the physics that runs the cell.
- **protocols:** none

Kruse reads Earth's magnetic history as the large-scale version of the physics he sees inside cells. In his model, periods when the field steadied allowed complex life, periods when it faltered reshaped or culled it, and the weakening under way now is another such turning point: a planetary re-tuning rather than an extinction.

#### Excursions and reversals

A reversal is a full swap of the magnetic poles; it takes about 2,000–10,000 years and then holds for 100,000 years or more. An excursion is an aborted reversal: the field weakens and wanders for a few centuries up to about 2,000 years, then recovers. The Laschamp excursion about 41,000 years ago and the Mono Lake excursion are examples. Seafloor magnetic stripes smooth excursions out, Kruse notes, but fast-settling lake and deep-sea sediments record hundreds of them, along with spikes in beryllium-10 and carbon-14 that mark extra cosmic rays. Reversals, in his view, cause extinctions because ozone is stripped for thousands of years; excursions select survivors. The field has collapsed about 500 times with only five mass extinctions, he says, but he believes several human civilizations were lost to such events.

Direct measurement of field strength began with Carl Friedrich Gauss in 1832. Earlier values come from fired pottery and lava, whose iron minerals lock in the field of the moment they cool, and researchers stitch those records into regional and global models. They show, Kruse points out, that swings can be fast: in the Levantine Iron Age Anomaly (Shaar and colleagues, 2016), the field over the Near East spiked to about double what models predict around 980 BCE and again in the early 8th century BCE, then quickly halved.

He reads the Permian "Great Dying," which killed about 96% of life, as a magnetic event in which heavy isotopes piled up while the field faltered. The survivors, in his account, were "hard" life that could keep its internal spin order with its own melanin and DHA, an "internal sun."

#### A steady field made complex life

Kruse argues that complex life arrived only once Earth's dynamo steadied. During the "Boring Billion," the long, quiet middle of Earth's history, a weak and unstable field let the solar wind strip ozone, leaving the surface scorched by UV; he cites modelling by Cooke and colleagues that puts ozone below 120 Dobson units when oxygen was 1% of today's level. Oxygen, he says, kept flipping into its reactive singlet form. Just before the Cambrian came what he calls a "magnetic flatline": in the Ediacaran period the field was 10–30 times weaker than today, hydrogen bled into space, water grew heavier and life stayed simple.

The Cambrian explosion, when about 32 animal body plans appeared, began in his account when the inner core hardened, the field stabilized and oxygen locked into its stable triplet state, letting one-handed molecules such as collagen and melanin act as spin filters. He cites a June 2025 *Science Advances* study linking atmospheric oxygen to the strength of Earth's main field over 540 million years, with joint peaks between 330 and 220 million years ago, the window in which he says the mammalian blueprint was built. He argues that Nick Lane's oxygen-centered explanation misses this, citing 2025–2026 genomic studies in *Nature* (publicized by NIOZ and the University of Copenhagen) showing that complex-cell machinery arose before oxygen was abundant. He also notes Sweetman and colleagues' finding (*Nature Geoscience*) that metal-rich nodules on the deep seabed make "dark oxygen" without sunlight, and asks why, if that ran all along, life locked onto the field only at the Cambrian; his answer turns on the formation of the ozone layer.

#### Laschamp and modern humans

At Laschamp, Kruse says, the field fell below 5% of normal and auroras reached the equator. He links the event to the "chromosome 2 fuse," the spot where two ancestral ape chromosomes fused in humans, which sits beside genes for melanin, fetal hemoglobin and metabolism; in his model it tripped and reset human metabolism. Neanderthals, he says, "short-circuited" rather than lost a war, while modern humans emerged with a larger melanin shield and a burst of cognition and cave art. He also ties excursions to collagen failure, pointing to elongated skulls such as Peru's Paracas infants, and calls today's rise in joint hypermobility a planetary effect.

#### The magnetic forge today

Kruse frames current changes as planetary rebalancing, not something humans caused. Earth, he says, is leaving a sunlight-dominated era for a "magnetic forge" like Laschamp's. He puts the present at about 9% of a Laschamp-scale collapse; at 30–50% he expects Earth's core and outer shell to decouple, his "Tau" stage. He cites Madsen and colleagues (University of Edinburgh, 2026), who used Swarm and other satellite data to show that molten iron under the equatorial Pacific switched from westward to eastward flow in 2010 and has weakened since about 2020. He follows the ECDO theory, which proposes a sudden slip of Earth's outer shell, as a staged cycle he believes has begun.

A failing dynamo, he says, sheds its energy as heat and radio waves. Electrical resistance in the core's currents (Ohmic heating) releases about 1–2 terawatts, which strengthens mantle plumes and makes quakes likelier in already risky areas, while radio bursts picked up by the ANITA balloon experiment over Antarctica are in his reading the dynamo dumping its disorder into space at the pole. He also points to reports that the Chandler wobble, a small natural wobble of Earth's spin axis, has dropped by about 98%, which he reads as the planet's transmission slipping into neutral.

#### Oceans, weather and volcanoes

Moving, salty ocean currents, the Atlantic and Southern overturning circulations (AMOC and SMOC), make their own magnetic fields and, in his model, are anchored to the dynamo. The link is the Lorentz force, the push a magnetic field gives to moving charged particles such as those in seawater; a weaker field gives the current less torque, so it stalls, loses oxygen and stagnates, which is why he calls the AMOC the planet's "vagal exhaust." As the currents slow, he reads stalled currents, a silent Atlantic hurricane season, a hyperactive Pacific and harder El Niño "sloshing" as magnetic effects; a collapsing AMOC, he says, keeps loading people in Florida with deuterium. Mining the deep-sea nodules for EV batteries, he warns, removes natural "geobatteries" that keep deep water in good order.

The Arctic tells the same story. He reads mosquitoes newly found in Iceland, and an Arctic warming about four times faster than the rest of the world, as a "magnetic Arctic flashover": as the north pole races toward Siberia, the Arctic loses the field's hold on its air and water, so "climate change" is in his phrase a software name for a hardware failure. Fish die-offs in Norway at 50–60°N, arriving far earlier than he expected, are further canaries; a fish's single tube-shaped heart works in his view as a vortex tied to the field that stalls as it weakens. He blames the failing dynamo, not human activity, for the "sixth extinction."

Warming seas release iodine, which destroys ozone far faster than chlorine, and volcanoes answer with CO₂ that dissolves as bicarbonate and locks the iodine back into the water. He welcomes this countermeasure and says humans need more CO₂, not less, during an excursion; he reads the rise from about 370 to 426 ppm, and a 5% greening of the planet roughly equal to one more Amazon, as a living shield against field loss. Finally, he maps planets onto cell states in his "Universal Scaling Law" (gas giants healthy, Earth stressed, Venus cancerous, Mars necrotic). Mars lost its dynamo, and in his account its water lost its magnetic hold, grew heavy with deuterium and boiled away into space, so colonizing it, he says, would be lethal.

In practice, Kruse treats this history as a reason to act rather than despair. Those who anchor themselves to a stable field, protect their internal water, build melanin with sunlight and escape blue-lit indoor life will, he says, carry humanity through the transition. He rejects "balance" or moderation during an excursion, and the planet-scale story is why he puts location ahead of every other health choice.

### nnEMF: wireless, wiring and the "hyper-local excursion"
- **id:** reduce-nnemf-block
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says man-made electromagnetic fields jam cell signalling, scramble electron spins, push heavy-hydrogen fuel into the cell and act like a local magnetic excursion, with low-frequency AC fields the first thing to check.
- **protocols:** nnemf-reduction, work-space, air-tube-headphones

nnEMF is Kruse's term for "non-native" electromagnetic fields: the man-made fields from wireless devices, wiring and electronics that did not exist in nature. He argues they do locally what the weakening planetary field does globally, stripping cells of the steady magnetic reference they need, scrambling electron spins and draining cell voltage. Low-frequency fields from household wiring and appliances, he says, are the first thing to check.

#### The same problem as the planet

In Kruse's model Earth's field supplies a constant, gentle "torque" that keeps electron spins aligned inside mitochondria. Wi-Fi, smart meters and Bluetooth, he says, swamp it and create a "hyper-local magnetic excursion" around the person. Cytochrome c oxidase, the enzyme that hands electrons to oxygen, loses its alignment, oxygen flips into its reactive singlet form, and the cell behaves as if starved of oxygen even when oxygen is present, a state called pseudohypoxia. Planetary decline and nnEMF, he says, "cause the same problem." A cell already damaged, carrying faulty mitochondrial DNA or a heavy deuterium load, is primed for this, so a router or smart meter can tip it over. He expects more people with electrohypersensitivity as the field weakens.

Because humans are coupled to the dynamo, he also thinks heavy use of electricity may create a small declination of its own around people, though he grants no one can be sure. The effect reaches the sea in his view: he blames nnEMF, rather than fertilizer runoff, for the white bacterial mats that spread in Norway's Oslofjord in early 2026, arguing that it slows the Atlantic's overturning current and leaves stagnant, oxygen-poor water.

#### Low-frequency AC fields first

His first suspect is AC (alternating current) magnetic fields under 100 kHz from power lines, appliances and indoor wiring. Citing a review of such fields, he says they knock spin-aligned electrons out of step and drain the charge on the inner mitochondrial membrane. The resulting singlet oxygen damages the iron–sulfur clusters of Complex I, the first step of the energy chain, so NAD⁺ falls; that is the molecule that repair enzymes, sirtuins and the body clock run on. He also cites Liboff and Blackman's work on ion cyclotron resonance: a 60 Hz field layered on Earth's steady field can match the natural frequency of calcium ions and drive calcium out of place, pushing magnesium down.

Fields also change what the cell burns. Citing a 2011 study by Nora Volkow finding that a phone held to the head raises glucose use in the nearby brain, Kruse argues that such fields break up the thin water shells around proteins and the body responds by flooding the blood with glucose, which he regards as deuterium-heavy fuel. At Complex I, deuterium then takes ordinary hydrogen's place and, he says, slows electron tunneling six- to tenfold; the cortisol surge in severe cases dumps still more glucose.

#### Wireless and the cell's own signals

5G and other wireless signals, Kruse argues, jam the terahertz signals that neighboring cells use to coordinate (ephaptic coupling, meaning communication through fields rather than synapses) and collapse the structured water around membranes. He rejects the idea that non-ionizing radiation is harmless because it cannot break atoms apart, likening it to calling a black mamba safe because it can't throw a punch. He points to a Rockefeller University patent by Jeffrey Friedman, the discoverer of leptin, and Sarah Stanley on using radio waves to switch heat-sensitive channels in cells, as proof that fields reach the leptin pathway that governs appetite and metabolism. He adds that a wind turbine's fields can lower cell voltage enough over time to allow cancer.

#### Specific exposures

Contrast MRI combines strong radio energy with gadolinium, a magnetic heavy metal, in a patient who may already be compromised. Citing a University of New Mexico study, he says gadolinium can form toxic particles with oxalate, so the risk rises after high-oxalate foods or synthetic vitamin C, and he advises contrast only when truly necessary. Phones and earbuds near the head, he says, are the commonest cause of dry mouth, because the facial and glossopharyngeal nerves that drive saliva run through narrow, exposed bony canals. Bluetooth headphones cancel sound therapy by adding the very fields it aims to undo.

Wearables harm from the inside, in his view. A tracking ring worn at night gives off enough radio energy to cut melanin production, which for one member mattered more than the low night-time oxygen the ring was recording. In hospitals, the monitors and lights of intensive care are what he calls "quantum blockers," keeping a stalled system from restarting.

In practice, the nnEMF reduction protocol says to look first for AC magnetic fields and artificial blue light with any chronic problem; not to sleep or live near smart meters, routers, 5G arrays or wind turbines; to keep phones and earbuds off the head and neck and devices off the lap; to skip wireless rings and other wearables; to avoid phones in the car, distrust airplane mode and skip smart houses; and to measure before mitigating, ideally with a credentialed assessor, because cheap fixes do little. The work-space protocol moves wiring, power supplies and appliances away from where you sit and favors daylight and incandescent bulbs, and the air-tube headphones protocol replaces Bluetooth with wired air-tube headphones or a speaker.

- **sources:** [379642](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379642/), [378697](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378697/), [378843](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378843/), [378819](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378819/), [377737](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377737/), [382846](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382846/), [380083](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380083/), [385641](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385641/), [385314](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385314/), [379576](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379576/), [373351](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373351/), [370759](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370759/)

### Astronauts on Earth: indoor, high-rise and airborne life
- **id:** nature-contact
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse says people living indoors, high above the ground, sealed in steel or in frequent flight are cut off from sun and field like astronauts, children most of all.
- **protocols:** nature-contact, ground-floor-living, flying-travel

Kruse argues that people who live indoors, high above the ground or in frequent flight are cut off from the sun and Earth's field much as astronauts are. He calls them "astronauts on Earth" and sees much chronic disease as spaceflight sickness without the rocket, with children the most exposed.

#### The indoor Faraday cage

A Faraday cage is an enclosure that blocks electromagnetic fields. Kruse describes buildings as one, cutting people off from sunlight, the planet's field and the ground. Add LED lighting and wireless fields on a planet whose dynamo is weakening, and indoor life, in his model, recreates conditions in orbit. He points to space research: NASA findings and a 2026 *Nature Communications* study showing that microgravity silences mitochondrial genes and stalls electron flow even though food, water and air stay the same, which he takes as proof that the physical environment drives the change. Gravity, magnetism and grounding are, for him, separate needs that cannot substitute for one another. Children kept indoors under blue light and off the ground, he says, have no field to guide their electron spins while they develop.

Submariners, sealed in steel for months, are his other test case. Melanoma and mental illness, which he ties to losing the Schumann signal, are among their commoner problems, along with gut and breathing trouble from rebreathed CO₂ and dental decay that older data linked to deuterium entering the teeth.

#### High-rise living

High floors add what he calls a voltage trap. The atmosphere carries a vertical electric field of about 100–150 volts per meter, so a person on the 24th floor sits thousands of volts away from ground potential and, in his account, acts like an antenna shedding electrons. He says electron-microscope images from an Australian high-rise dweller resembled those of astronauts. His answer is to live and sleep on the ground or first floor.

#### Flying

Flying, Kruse says, takes you off the ground and closer to the Van Allen radiation belts, causing "magnetic shear" and raising cell-free mitochondrial DNA (fragments released from stressed cells). Frequent ungrounded travel loads the body's water with deuterium and thickens blood, moving toward "lattice lock," his term for tissue stiffened by deuterium-heavy water. Planes and mountains raise the risk of muscle loss, and long east–west flights over the Atlantic and the South Atlantic Anomaly, he says, speed ALS, tethered cord and arachnoiditis. At cruising altitude inside a metal cabin the field's steadying effect drops, which is why he says never to eat on a plane.

In the cabin, he adds, extra wireless exposure and no ground push the body's water from its structured state (about 160 on his scale) toward ordinary water (78) or lower, weakening immune proteins and making barriers such as the blood–brain barrier leakier. He cites a man already run down by his home zip code who boarded a plane with a headache and died soon after of meningitis, an outcome Kruse calls predictable.

In practice, the nature-contact protocol moves as much of life outdoors as possible and gives children unfiltered morning UV-A and infrared on eyes and skin, bare feet on the earth and a home without Wi-Fi or artificial night light. The ground-floor living protocol says to live no higher than the first floor; if you cannot move yet, use his "High-Altitude Counter-Faraday Shield" (conductive mesh around the bed wired to deep ground rods) or copper vortex devices such as Slim Spurling coils and Lakhovsky rings with grounded carbon mats. The flying protocol cuts unneeded flights, skips food in the air, allows a magnetic necklace only in flight as "better than nothing," and expects some muscle loss after flights or time at altitude.

### Magnets, pads and powered devices
- **id:** magnetico-sleep-pad
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse's view of static magnetic pads and stimulation devices: sometimes useful stand-ins for a lost field, but always behind location, sun and grounding.
- **protocols:** magnetic-sleep-pad

Kruse sees artificial magnetic fields as a two-edged tool. A static magnetic sleep pad can stand in for a weakening planetary field, but the wrong magnet, pole or strength can "over-clamp" tissue, and every device ranks behind location, sunlight and grounding.

#### Sleep pads

As the geodynamo weakens, Kruse says, an outside static field can serve as a personal micro-shield. He recommends sleeping on a static magnetic pad (he names the Magnetico brand) all night rather than in short bursts, preferring about 20 gauss to 5, and using a north-seeking pad alongside pure DHA and raw morning UV-A. Children stuck in a poor magnetic zip code, he says, need one. A cheaper option is a coil of heavy-gauge copper pipe under the pillow: by Lenz's law, incoming wireless fields induce small opposing currents in the copper, so the coil works as a passive shield, much as, he says, the lava rock in his walls does. In El Salvador he calls a pad pointless for anyone who spends time outdoors, because the volcanic ground does the job.

Where the field is collapsing outright, he sees little point either. In the Southern Hemisphere he calls a pad, like other such devices, superfluous: the better choice there is not to "jump into the jet engine while it's running," while in the Northern Hemisphere a pad makes more sense. He adds that he never set out to promote pads; members learned in consults that he used one and spread the word.

He flags one risk. A strong vertical field from a pad at night, followed by a steeply tilted and noisy field by day, can in his model give the thalamus (the brain's position sensor) a mismatched reference, especially without strong tropical sun to steady it.

#### Poles and jewelry

Magnet makers, Kruse says, ignore polarity. The north pole in his model calms and organizes electron spins, while the south pole speeds activity and can tip oxygen into its reactive singlet form. A randomly oriented magnetic necklace creates a shifting field every time you move, rattling the tissues around the carotid arteries, and heavy static bracelets over-clamp; in one member's candle test, a bracelet slowed the burn and left unburnt wax. He allows magnetic jewelry only on flights, and an open 12-inch copper ring is his safer passive loop. He dismisses "Biomagnetic Pair" magnet therapy as charlatanism.

#### Dose has a window

Kruse cites a 2025 study by Beutner and colleagues in which isolated rat-heart mitochondria exposed to a static field far weaker than a fridge magnet raised their respiration by about 40% while producing fewer free radicals, but only within a narrow range; stronger fields lost the effect. He reads this as the radical pair mechanism, the spin chemistry by which a weak field tips the outcome of electron reactions.

#### Powered devices rank lower

For other devices his rule is location first. Relocate before trying transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS), and ground on basalt rather than buying a PEMF (pulsed electromagnetic field) device, which he calls the "loser option." In declining areas a gentle TENS unit, a small skin-electrode nerve stimulator, may be wiser than a magnetic pad. External counterpulsation (EECP), which uses leg cuffs to send pressure waves through the blood vessels, recharges vessel walls in his view, but the gain lasts only with sun and grounding. Electrolysis, he adds, can restore color to grey hair by re-pinning the follicle's local field with a burst of current.

In practice, the magnetic sleep pad protocol says to sleep on the pad all night at about 20 gauss, choose a north-seeking pad during the decline along with DHA and morning UV-A, give one to children living in poor magnetic zip codes, and use a heavy-gauge copper pipe coil as a cheap alternative.
