Skip the pad in El Salvador if you get outside, and if you live in the Southern Hemisphere put relocation ahead of any device. If you try a cheaper pad, measure the field it gives off and how you respond. Avoid heavy static-magnet jewelry, and keep magnetic necklaces for flights.

- **sources:** [384557](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384557/), [384084](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384084/), [382416](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382416/), [381192](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381192/), [378787](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378787/), [382101](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382101/), [380816](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380816/), [384114](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384114/), [384345](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384345/), [378182](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378182/), [372036](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372036/)

### Reading the field at home and in the body
- **id:** field-proxies
- **pillar:** magnetism
- **summary:** Kruse's do-it-yourself readouts of a place's magnetic quality and the body's state: beeswax candles, mosquitoes, clouds, animals, tans, blood markers, heart-rate variability and a personal scorecard.
- **protocols:** beeswax-candle-test, hrv-tracking, track-your-field

Kruse treats human health as the most sensitive magnetometer there is: fertility, cancer and deaths tracked by zip code, he says, show field change before official data do. Because those data are smoothed and slow, he gives members simple ways to read a place and their own body.

#### Reading a place

His best-known proxy is a rated beeswax candle burned where you live. On schedule means normal; faster, such as a 24-hour candle gone in 18, means field decline or a heavy wireless load; slower and clean means a strong local field; slower with leftover wax means an artificial "over-clamp," as when a member set a candle on a magnetic bracelet. He uses only beeswax, which he describes as deuterium-depleted and made of one-handed molecules, because paraffin is heavy in deuterium and spoils the reading. When the disturbance is visibly overhead, such as auroras above Finland, the test is moot.

On land he pairs candles with mosquitoes: insects active by day as well as night mark a weak spot, a method he calls "magnetic dowsing" and used to buy property in El Salvador. Insects work as sensors, he explains, because their chitin shells are piezoelectric (they turn pressure and fields into tiny currents), so mosquitoes spreading north, as they now have into Iceland, show that the magnetic barrier that kept them out has given way. In a humid region the sky offers another clue: a volcano that has lost its cloud cap signals a local "magnetic stall," a cone no longer able to hold its water.

Animals tell the same story. In his account, traditional trackers read land by behavior, not head counts: large herds splintering into small erratic groups, dull dry coats even near water, and grazing shifted to pre-dawn and dusk. His shortlist of signs that a place is failing is aurorae, sick people and dying animals, especially birds, bees and fish; he watches reports from his Gulf of Mexico shrimp supplier and names godwits, Arctic terns and albatrosses as bird canaries. Trees and animals, he adds, feel the ground currents and gases a stressed fault releases days to weeks before a quake. He also suggests long pipelines as a citizen's magnetometer, because their electrical potential swings when earth currents surge.

Instruments have limits. Even a high-grade ground magnetometer, he says, cannot see what he is tracking, which has to be measured from above by the Swarm satellites; for better ground readings he names the optically pumped magnetometer. Unable to get Swarm numbers for two years, he took three world cruises in two years to see the Southern Ocean and its people for himself.

#### Reading the body

In the blood, a rapidly shifting potassium level means, to Kruse, that the nearest agonic line (where compass north and true north agree) is moving away or that the person is spoiling their own magnetic tracking. High ferritin, the iron-storage marker, reflects a weak local field and poor choices around light and grounding. His four everyday proxies are morning blood sugar (his "magnetic dawning phenomenon"), LDL, vitamin B12 and new pigment changes. A mitochondrial enzyme (OXPHOS) test can be read as a map of your location's field, and a muscle biopsy viewed under an electron microscope shows what a place does to the shape of your mitochondria.

A tan shows only the outside, though it can still test official numbers: his clients in Calgary all report they cannot hold a tan, which makes him doubt the 56,000 nT figure quoted for the area. For what he calls the "interior tan," the state of melanin and water deep inside, he looks at homocysteine and TMAO (which he treats as "silt" markers of poor deuterium clearance), white-matter changes on MRI and slow flow on thermal imaging, which he calls the ideal fast test because it reflects how thick the blood is. He predicts that a zip-code map of homocysteine would overlay the declination map, peaking in weak-field zones such as the US West and Midwest and the South Atlantic Anomaly. He considers epigenetic clocks and saliva telomere tests unreliable during the decline.

#### His problem-solving method

Kruse adapts George Pólya's 1945 book *How to Solve It*, as his neurosurgery mentors taught it, into four steps. First, define the problem as a local voltage drop and check for AC magnetic fields and blue light. Second, work back from oxygen in its stable triplet state, restoring the field with grounding, sun and natural resonators such as basalt. Third, act with targeted cold, breathing and light, patiently. Fourth, look back: check the result by another route, write down the lessons for the next case, and judge progress on a personal scorecard: salivary D/H (the ratio of deuterium to ordinary hydrogen in saliva) below 130 ppm, ultra-weak light given off by the body turning quiet and coherent, and nerve voltage back near −90 mV, with heart-rate variability (HRV, the beat-to-beat variation in heart timing) in deep sleep as the running gauge. HRV, he cautions, cannot warn of a sudden local loss of field, the kind he blames for people dropping dead while exercising.

In practice, the beeswax candle test protocol uses a rated pure-beeswax candle and reads it alongside day-versus-night mosquito activity. The HRV tracking protocol makes deep-sleep HRV the main progress gauge, adds the everyday markers and scorecard above, and treats high ferritin and fast-shifting potassium as warnings. The track-your-field protocol adds watching your zip code's Swarm data, local animals, insects and fisheries, and the cloud caps on nearby volcanoes.

- **sources:** [383849](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383849/), [385828](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385828/), [382918](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382918/), [381998](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381998/), [374031](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374031/), [374517](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374517/), [378843](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378843/), [384084](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384084/), [379229](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379229/), [374553](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374553/), [371128](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371128/), [373184](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373184/)

## Support

### Sleep sanctuary
- **id:** sleep-space
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** sleep-cold-movement
- **summary:** Kruse calls insomnia a light-and-magnetic hardware failure, so the bedroom must be dark, cold, grounded, wireless-free and aligned with the field.
- **protocols:** sleep-space, north-south-bed

Kruse treats insomnia as a hardware problem, not a chemical shortage: in his model the body cannot sleep well when the bedroom's light and magnetic environment is wrong. That is why he says sleeping pills and melatonin supplements make things worse, and why he puts the room itself at the center of sleep repair.

His reasoning starts with melatonin. The melatonin that matters most to him is not the small amount the pineal gland releases into the blood, but the much larger amount mitochondria (the cell's energy-producing units) make inside cells overnight. Drawing on Russel Reiter's work, he calls this nightly melatonin "magnetic chemotherapy": while you sleep, it calms damaged cells and nudges cells drifting toward cancer back toward normal behavior.

Light after dark switches that repair off. Kruse points to the white LED, built by coating a blue LED with a yellow phosphor to save energy. Its strong blue peak fires the eye's daytime sensors (light-sensitive retinal cells that tell the brain clock it is day), so a screen or LED bulb at night tells the clock it is noon, and cells skip their overnight melatonin.

Darkness alone is not enough, he argues. Making melatonin needs two kinds of "containment" around the mitochondrial membrane, one electric and one magnetic. Grounding (skin contact with the earth, or a grounded sheet) restores only the electric part. The magnetic part depends on the field around the bed, which is why Wi-Fi, wiring and electric beds can ruin sleep even in a pitch-black room. He is blunt that an electric bed is a mistake.

Cold matters for a separate reason. In deep sleep the spaces between brain cells widen and spinal fluid moves through the brain in a spiral he calls a vortex. In his model a cold room contracts brain tissue slightly, which narrows the vortex and makes it spin faster. That flings deuterium (heavy hydrogen, which he says jams the mitochondrial motor) out of the brain toward the lymphatic drains in the neck.

The last piece is direction. Kruse wants the bed on a north–south line with the head to the north, so the brain's magnetic iron crystals and the iron-containing proteins in mitochondria lie parallel to Earth's field lines all night. Because the magnetic pole is now moving quickly, he says this alignment should be checked against today's field rather than set once and forgotten.

In practice, he calls the result a "magnetic sanctuary." His sleep-space protocol asks for Wi-Fi, routers and other wireless devices switched off before sleep, and total darkness, with red incandescent light or full red blockers after sunset instead of LEDs. It also means no electric bed and no Bluetooth sleep trackers. Rings and wearables, he adds, cannot measure what he thinks matters most: how scrambled indoor life has left the brainstem. The room should have a grounded sheet and be cold, ideally on the ground floor rather than high in a building. His north–south bed protocol adds the head-north alignment, rechecked as the pole moves. Where the local field is weak he suggests sleeping all night on a static magnetic pad, or, if that is unaffordable, putting a coil of heavy copper pipe under the pillow. Sleeping pills and melatonin pills stay on his avoid list.

- **sources:** [382666](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382666/), [382704](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382704/), [378783](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378783/), [384114](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384114/), [382388](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382388/), [382401](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382401/), [382394](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382394/), [382733](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382733/), [378971](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378971/)

### Sleep in his model: adenosine, myelin and the nightly flip
- **id:** myelin-sleep-battery
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** sleep-cold-movement
- **summary:** Kruse explains sleep as the nightly recharge of an electrical grid: adenosine signals the membrane is tiring, myelin recharges as a proton battery, the brain flips polarity to purge deuterium, and how you wake shows whether it worked.
- **protocols:** sleep-space, binaural-sleep-audio

Kruse explains sleep as the nightly recharge of the body's electrical grid rather than simple rest. In his model three things happen in sequence: a chemical alarm signals that the grid is tiring, the brain's white matter recharges like a battery, and the brain reverses its electrical polarity to clear deuterium (heavy hydrogen). When any step fails, he says, sleep disorders follow.

#### The adenosine alarm

During the day, mitochondria hold a very strong electric field across their inner membrane (he gives 30 million volts per metre). In his view this field is what lets the membrane let in ordinary hydrogen and keep heavy hydrogen out. Hours of waking work create friction that slows electrons, so the voltage sags and the water inside cells drifts from its structured, charge-holding state toward ordinary "bulk" water.

To avoid a blowout, which he calls Marcus inversion (electron flow stalling once it is pushed past its best rate), the cell strips phosphate groups off its ATP fuel. The adenosine left over is his "smoke alarm": it tells the brainstem's arousal network that the grid is tiring and taking on deuterium, and that triggers sleep. He adds that an ungrounded day also builds up oleamide, a fatty signal that eventually shuts the connections between brain cells and stalls the thalamus (the brain's relay and pacing center). It is cleared overnight. For this reason he places sleep in the state of brain water and the heat of the spinal fluid, not in microtubules (tiny protein tubes inside neurons).

#### Myelin as a proton battery

Kruse hails a 2026 hypothesis by Felix Scholkmann and colleagues as support for his model. Myelin, the fatty wrapping around nerve fibers, is not passive insulation but a proton battery charged during sleep. Myelin basic protein, packed with positively charged amino acids, is the "anode" that grips protons. Proteolipid protein, which spans the membrane, is the "wire" that carries them to ATP synthase, the motor that makes ATP.

Because myelin has few mitochondria of its own, he says mitochondria ship working ATP synthase to it overnight in small membrane packages. At the same time melatonin cools the brain by 1–2 °C, which tightens the relevant enzymes so deuterium is filtered out as the battery refills.

Several conclusions follow in his model. Sleep need scales inversely with white-matter volume: bats sleep about 20 hours, humans about 8, elephants about 3.5. Sleep loss, marathons, multiple sclerosis and brain injury drain the battery, and an indoor, blue-lit life lets myelin water collapse in a way no sleeping pill can fix. He also says there is no genetic night owl. A chronic night owl, to him, is an ungrounded person whose cellular battery is so low the master clock cannot find its starting line, and environment, not clock genes, resets it.

#### The nightly polarity flip

By day, Kruse says, electrons moving across Earth's field lines create a "biological Hall voltage" that helps hold the membrane field. At sunset, melanin in the skin and brain, with the hippocampus acting as a switchboard, should reverse polarity. Following Robert O. Becker's direct-current research, he describes current then flowing outward from the body's core to its edges. This "nocturnal Hall current inversion" is his main deuterium purge engine, and in dream sleep the brainstem paralyzes the body while it runs.

Screens, Wi-Fi, jet lag across time zones, a poor local field and too little near-infrared light all stop the flip and leave the spinal fluid loaded with deuterium; grounded sleep lets melanin make the switch. He reads sleep apnea and restless legs as emergency jolts. Apnea uses an airway collapse and adrenaline surge to restart a stalled brain circuit, and restless legs use leg contractions to restart a stalled pathway in the leg arteries. Insomnia and bipolar swings, he says, reflect a broken brainstem-to-gut nerve circuit. That circuit depends on melanin, and when it is clogged with lipofuscin (his "rusted melanin," degraded pigment debris), the flip cannot start.

#### Reading the morning after

Kruse treats the way you wake as a readout of whether the night's work got done. After restorative sleep, in his model, cell water has climbed back from the ordinary state he scores as 78 toward the structured state he scores as 160. Meanwhile the vagus nerve's overnight bicarbonate flush and the pumping of the abdomen have moved stagnant fluid out of the tissues toward the bladder. A good night therefore leaves you feeling dry and lean. Puffy eyes or stiff fingers mean the fluid stalled and deuterium stayed put.

Vision is his second gauge. Waking to sharp, high-contrast sight means the light-handling circuits of the eye and brain came through the night clear. Fog or a heavy head means the repair window ran too long and the base of the brain did not flush its deuterium during dream sleep, so the brain starts the day short of stable oxygen.

#### In practice

He points to his sleep-space protocol (a dark, cold, grounded, wireless-free bedroom with the bed north–south) so the battery and the flip can work. He pairs it with daytime sun that includes near-infrared, and morning sun and grounding to reset a night owl. His binaural sleep audio, a theta-to-delta tone glide while falling asleep, is meant to sync the brain's nightly fluid pulsing. He judges the result by how dry, clear-eyed and clear-headed you wake. For sleep, MS or white-matter problems he recommends changing the environment, including relocating, rather than taking pills.

- **sources:** [384036](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384036/), [385509](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385509/), [383741](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383741/), [380983](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380983/), [380918](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380918/), [375640](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375640/), [380967](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380967/), [372782](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372782/)

### Cold thermogenesis and the brain's fluid vortex
- **id:** cold-thermogenesis-skin-50
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** sleep-cold-movement
- **summary:** Kruse calls cold a "quantum vice" that compresses tissue to keep deuterium out — an override for poor sun or field, with lineage and timing caveats.
- **protocols:** cold-thermogenesis

Kruse calls cold thermogenesis (deliberate cold exposure) a "quantum vice": a squeeze that tightens tissue so deuterium (heavy hydrogen) cannot get in. It matters in his model because cold is the main override when sunlight or the local magnetic field is poor, as in winter, at high latitude or in a bad location.

#### How the vice works

When nerve tissue is chilled it contracts, and cells must reorganize their fats to keep their membranes from freezing stiff. In white matter, Kruse says, this squeeze narrows the spacing in myelin's proteins so they clamp onto protons, and narrows the entry channels so the deuteron (deuterium's nucleus, about twice the mass of ordinary hydrogen) no longer fits. The result in his model is cell water restored to its structured, charge-holding state, which he scores as "160" against 78 for ordinary water, even when your location is poor.

He adds a point about phase change. Heavy water freezes at 3.8 °C rather than 0 °C and changes state differently from ordinary water, so a sharp cold shock at the skin surface changes local water conditions in a way he says helps shake deuterium loose.

#### Cold and dopamine

Kruse leans on Pavel Šrámek's study from Charles University in Prague, in which ten young men sat neck-deep for an hour in water at 32, 20 and 14 °C. At 14 °C, norepinephrine rose about 530% and dopamine about 250%, which he contrasts with roughly 200% from Adderall. He stresses that the dopamine stayed raised for two to three hours and drifted back without dipping below baseline, so there is no crash, tolerance or addiction. He reads ADHD as deuterium loading in the brain's hypothalamic and limbic regions and says cold works better than stimulants. The daily dose he draws from this is short: 60–180 seconds on bare skin, cold enough to make you want out, ideally in the morning.

#### Where cold fits, and its limits

In his hierarchy, cold is the override for winter and poor locations and should come before any diet change. Used as the only fix in a place like California, though, he calls it unsustainable. Cold and sauna can work against each other, he says, but only for someone who doesn't understand why to use each.

He sets limits. For East and Southeast Asian maternal lineages (haplogroups D5b1 and F) in a weak field, water at 55–65 °F can "freeze" the energy engine, because the short-lived electron pairs the cell relies on need a strong field. Those people should first maximize morning UV and infrared light, ground fully and shield from wireless fields. Timing matters too. At dawn, neck-deep water absorbs the infrared part of sunlight, scrambles the light's angle and acts like a Faraday cage, so he prefers feet in the surf with the rest of the bare body in the air. In the tropics, natural water is always cooler than the body, so ordinary swimming counts as cold therapy.

#### Targeted cold and the head's fluid vortex

Kruse also uses cold on specific spots. Ice on the roof of the mouth, under the chin or on the back of the neck contracts tissue and blood vessels and narrows what he calls the cranial "vortex chamber," so spinal fluid spins at a sharper angle and faster. Deuterium, being heavier, cannot hold the tight orbit and is thrown out to the salivary and lymphatic drains. Sleeping in a cold room does the same overnight. He also says cold packs the inner mitochondrial membrane more tightly, so its first energy-extracting complex strips electrons from food faster, and he uses groin cold in his erectile-dysfunction advice.

The belly is another target. When the gut has stalled and nothing can be taken by mouth, he leans on cold, light and ground together. Local cold on the abdomen, in his model, locks the local field in place, raises the oxygen available to tissue and spins deuterium out. Violet (380 nm) and red (670 nm) light on the skin restores the direct current that runs through collagen. Grounding steadies the signal so healthy mitochondria can restart the gut's muscular wave.

#### In practice

His cold-thermogenesis protocol is a daily 60–180 seconds of cold on bare skin, ideally in the morning, leaning on cold more heavily in winter or a poor location. At sunrise he wants feet in the surf rather than full immersion, and in the tropics he counts natural-water swimming. He adds targeted ice on the palate, under the chin or on the back of the neck, cold on the abdomen with skin light and grounding for a sluggish gut, and a cold bedroom. People from D5b1 or F lineages living in a weak field should hold off on deep cold until they are well grounded and sunlit.

- **sources:** [385515](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385515/), [382381](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382381/), [382413](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382413/), [379370](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379370/), [378971](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378971/), [374910](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374910/), [378701](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378701/), [382383](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382383/), [379918](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379918/), [373625](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373625/)

### Movement, and when exercise becomes a toxin
- **id:** morning-movement
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** sleep-cold-movement
- **summary:** Kruse supports daily outdoor movement but says exercise turns harmful when the body is deuterium-loaded or unpinned, or when it happens in sealed, high-CO₂ gyms, so environment comes before fitness.
- **protocols:** exercise

Kruse supports daily movement, but argues that exercise only helps a body whose environment is already right. In a body loaded with deuterium (heavy hydrogen) or "unpinned" (not anchored by a stable magnetic field, grounding and sunlight), he says exercise becomes a toxin. His rule is environment first, fitness second.

#### Why movement matters

In Kruse's model the real purpose of exercise is electrical. Bone and collagen are piezoelectric (they make a small current when squeezed or bent), so movement releases current that supports the spiral flow and charge of the blood. That keeps deuterium circulating and away from the mitochondria, and moving joints spin deuterium out of themselves. Mechanical and gravitational loading, together with a stable, flat local field and grounding, also keep the elastin in artery walls healthy.

He adds a pump. Citing a 2026 Nature Neuroscience mouse study by Greenawalt, Drew and colleagues, he says abdominal muscle contractions physically move the brain and drive the fluid flow that cleans it during waking hours. Astronauts lose this pump as their core weakens in microgravity. He thinks the researchers stopped short: in his model the motion does more than wash the brain, it sorts its water, spinning heavy water away from light. He calls this "centrifugal fractionation."

He also reads one study's gene data as showing that single workouts produce only weak, messy changes, while weeks of steady training produce an ordered shift. Long-running pulsed electric current in sea squirts produced the same shift, which in his model shows that sustained current, not effort itself, rebuilds the cell.

#### When exercise turns harmful

Kruse cites a Nature Aging study, shared by Daniel Tawfik, that defines muscle aging as an energy crisis in which the key energy-making enzymes decline. He agrees that structured training beats counting steps. But in his view exercise is a toxin when cell water is deuterium-loaded or when oxygen has flipped into its reactive "singlet" state instead of its stable "triplet" state. Deuterium slows the flow of protons, extra demand stalls electrons in the energy chain, and singlet oxygen damages membranes. Deuterium-loaded people, he says, lose lifespan when they train, most visibly astronauts and people on GLP-1 weight-loss drugs. He names the strength coach Charles Poliquin as someone he had warned.

He lists specific traps. Compression-and-cooling machines such as Vasper, he says, cause bursts of reperfusion damage in energy-poor muscle. Building muscle in old age is one of the costliest things a cell can do and steals energy from DNA repair, immunity and overnight brain clearance, leaving "a big frame on a bankrupt core." Watching television and then working out undoes the benefit, because screen light loads the cell with deuterium and disrupts its clock, and years of running ungrounded in rubber-soled shoes destroy joints. He uses Mark Sisson's joint replacements as his example. Blue-lit gyms are a problem when you are unwell, and flights and altitude raise the risk of muscle loss.

#### The air inside gyms

Gym air is a separate trap. Outdoor air holds about 415 parts per million (ppm) of carbon dioxide, while sealed, crowded gyms often reach 1,200 to more than 2,000. He says sleep and focus slip above roughly 800 ppm, and cites the 2012 Lawrence Berkeley Lab study in which decision-making fell on six of nine scales at 1,000–1,500 ppm. Hard breathing pulls that air, along with solvent fumes and fine particles, deep into the lungs.

In his model, excess carbon dioxide taken in during exertion acidifies the spinal fluid, collapses structured water and lets deuterium lock the tissue's water in place. He says years of indoor team training set professional players up for CTE (a degenerative brain disease) after retirement, naming Reggie White, Damar Hamlin and J.J. Watt. In a declining magnetic field, he warns, even light indoor sessions risk sudden collapse.

#### Overtraining and the brain

Overtraining harms the brain too. Kruse presents a Cell Metabolism study (Huang and colleagues) in which excess lactate in strained muscle makes it release mitochondrial "pretenders": vesicles that cross into the hippocampus, block the delivery of healthy mitochondria and occupy their docking sites at synapses. In the study's UK Biobank data, moderate exercise protected against dementia while vigorous exercise beyond a threshold sharply raised the risk.

He sees the same pattern in fit people who drop dead in competition, such as a coach who collapsed at a Bangkok HYROX event, or a healthy 17-year-old who collapsed while exercising. To him, such a person's spinal fluid was already locked with deuterium and needed clearing ("de-fragging") before any training. He says marathon running wrecks the heart's own spiral flow and that professional athletes die early. He tracks where such sudden deaths cluster by ZIP code and says it is now survival of the wisest, not the fittest.

#### In practice

His exercise protocol is daily movement outdoors, grounded and in sunlight, at moderate rather than extreme intensity. Order matters to him. First leave the wireless "soup," ground and sleep in a low-EMF bedroom, then get morning sun with its infrared, and only then add training. He would not start a sick, unpinned person on Zone 2 or interval training, and wants a deuterium-loaded person cleared before training at all. He also warns against going straight from a screen to a workout, running ungrounded in rubber soles, training in sealed or blue-lit gyms, using compression machines while deuterium-loaded, chasing marathons, and making muscle-building a goal in old age.

- **sources:** [379828](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379828/), [379737](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379737/), [382120](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382120/), [377230](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377230/), [377231](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377231/), [377319](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377319/), [379629](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379629/), [375540](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375540/), [382055](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382055/), [372152](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372152/), [372482](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372482/)

### The vagus nerve: deuterium drain and its stimulation
- **id:** vagus-nerve-stimulation
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** vagus-sound
- **summary:** Kruse calls the vagus nerve the body's main deuterium drain, draws on what he saw when surgeons cut it, and tunes it with voice, sound and gentle stimulation, with firm guardrails on devices.
- **protocols:** tens-vagus, vagus-nerve-stimulation, castor-oil-navel, vagus-and-device-therapies

In Kruse's model the vagus nerve, the long nerve running from the brainstem to the heart, lungs and gut, is the body's main exhaust pipe for deuterium (heavy hydrogen). Together with lymph and spinal-fluid flow, it carries disorder out of the system. He calls it the body's "renormalization operator," and when its tone fails he says heavy hydrogen backs up into the brain. Much of his advice on breathing, sound and nerve stimulation is aimed at keeping this drain open.

#### The body's exhaust pipe

Kruse says the vagus supplies a steady rhythmic signal that keeps the barrier cells of the gut, lungs and blood vessels electrically charged. That charge keeps potassium in place and the water around those cells structured, which he says physically screens out deuterium. When vagal tone fades from wireless exposure, artificial blue light and lack of grounding, heavy hydrogen leaks in. It then backs up through the gut's nerve network into the brain past the fourth ventricle (the fluid chamber at the base of the brain).

The traffic runs both ways. He describes a sensor on the floor of the fourth ventricle that reads the spinal fluid. Deuterium makes that fluid denser and less able to hold charge, the sensor reads the drop, and vagal tone is turned down. In his model this leaves the heart prone to atrial flutter (a fast, regular rhythm in the upper chambers), a pattern he says he sees often in North America. He also notes that the vagus leaves the skull through the jugular foramen, an opening it shares with the jugular vein, so he reads a squeezed jugular vein at the top of the neck as a nerve being strangled.

He ties the vagus to digestion through what he calls the bicarbonate "tailpipe." In a strong field, the vagus drives the pancreas and duodenum to release about two liters of bicarbonate a day, and this negatively charged carrier binds deuterium and sweeps it out of the body. Sugar under blue light, or a disturbed field, "welds the tailpipe shut." He also describes a "universal" 2 Hz rhythm that keeps tissues in sync, and says immune alarm signals and deuterium jam it.

He blames a blocked drain for a wide range of problems: drug-induced restlessness (akathisia), the agitation young children show waking from anesthesia, scalp hair loss, hearing loss and even earwax build-up. In an acute lung infection, he reads sudden fluid in the lungs as the vagal exhaust collapsing there.

#### The eye as a vagal switch

Kruse links the eye to the vagus through the ciliary ganglion, a small nerve relay behind the eye that joins the nerve moving the eye to vagal branches around the orbit. Full-spectrum light on the iris and focusing muscles, he says, sets the idle speed of the gut's bicarbonate flush. Glasses and contact lenses send a false "dark" signal that slows the drain and starts deuterium backing up. Rubbing the eyes triggers the oculocardiac reflex (pressure on the eye slowing the heart), and he calls it a quick "piezo-vagal reset" for days when light or field is lacking.

#### Left and right are not the same

Kruse gives each side of the nerve a job. The right vagus is the "cardiac operator": it damps chaotic signals to the heart so they cannot lock into racing or fibrillating rhythms. The left vagus is the "input operator": it monitors the gut and liver so their incoming deuterium load does not overwhelm the mitochondria. He links childhood nephrotic syndrome (a kidney disorder that leaks protein) to fading right-vagus control of the kidney.

That split sets his firmest rule: electrical stimulation goes on the left side only. Nearly all approved implants and ear devices (taVNS, stimulation through the skin of the ear) are restricted to the left side, he notes, because the right neck vagus sends thick fibers straight to the heart's pacemaker. Stimulating it sends the signal down to the heart instead of up to the brainstem, and can slow the heart dangerously, block its rhythm or stop it.

#### What a cut nerve taught him

Kruse traces his interest to two years of general surgery at Charity Hospital, before his neurosurgery training. There he performed hundreds of vagotomies, cutting the vagus to reduce stomach acid in ulcer patients. Some of those patients' Parkinson's disease improved, which led him to use vagus stimulators early for neurodegeneration.

He reads the operation's familiar after-effects as gut organs filling with deuterium once their drain was severed. These include gallstones, dumping syndrome (food rushing too fast into the intestine) and gastroparesis (a stalled stomach). His first spleen rupture without trauma came two weeks after one of these operations. In ALS, he says, the backflow takes a different route: deuterium runs from the base of the brain down the spinal cord's central canal and locks up the spinal fluid.

#### Tuning by breath, voice and sound

Before devices, Kruse ranks everyday ways to tone the vagus. At the top is slow belly breathing with long exhales, which he calls probably the most powerful repeatable vagal stimulus. Next come exercise, the cold diving reflex, mind–body practices, laughter and social contact, then humming, chanting, gargling, massage, gentle neck movement and slow nature walks. Mouth breathing works against all of it. He describes once ending up in an emergency room after one of his own deuterium experiments misfired, and settling it with vagal exercises in front of staff who never saw the cause.

Sound has a special place. Neolithic chambers such as Newgrange resonate near 110 Hz, the pitch of a man's voice, and he says they were built to amplify chanting. Falling water and chanting "OM" match his 2 Hz rhythm. A 128 Hz tuning fork on the sternum in the evening, and the "Vagus Rx" in his Time Subtraction blog, round out the passive toolkit. He also says ear stimulation and chewing can shrink surfer's ear.

#### Devices and their guardrails

Any stimulation, in his view, works only if the nerve has something to push against. He calls the vagus the body's "master centrifuge," and says grounding and UV light act as the "magnetic stator" that gives it torque. Without them, deuterium settles in the nerves and brain chambers whatever device is used.

Kruse treats a TENS unit (a small device that sends pulsed current through skin pads) as a blunt tool, and says most commercial units are harsh enough to scramble electron spins and over-conduct through melanin. His home settings are gentle. He wants a biphasic waveform (one whose charge reverses each pulse, so nothing builds up under the pads) at 0.5–2.0 mA, below sensation or a faint tingle and never enough to twitch muscle. Pulse width should be 200–250 µs, at a steady 25 Hz, for 15–30 minutes once or twice a day. He reserves 40 Hz for brain rhythms, not the vagus.

For the vagus itself, the ear clip goes on the left tragus or inner ear fold, with the return pad on the bone just behind the same ear. A separate pad pair over the upper abdomen and the matching mid-back serves the gut's nerve plexus. Pads never go on the neck, where they can trigger the carotid pressure sensors, on the temple, or anywhere on the right. The black negative lead goes nearest the target nerve. Among commercial stimulators he rates the Neuropod best for Parkinson's and other neurodegeneration. He reports that vagal tumors (glomus tumors) shrink when stimulation is applied below them, and he would not use vagus hydrodissection (injecting fluid to free the nerve).

He also offers powerless alternatives to TENS that avoid current altogether. One is resting grounded with a smooth piece of black basalt or volcanic clay on the bone behind the ear or at the temple. Others are wearing a plain, unclosed 12-inch copper ring around the head or keeping a paramagnetic stone in the pillow. A copper ring at the neck, he says, does the same job as a vagus stimulator by replacing energy that missing melanin should supply. Another passive option is castor oil in the navel at night, his "umbilical castor capacitor." He describes the navel as an embryonic scar where nerves and vessels permanently cross, rich in nerve endings, and says castor oil's chiral, polar main fat sends a small current up the vagus that restarts the bicarbonate flush.

#### In practice

He starts with daily slow belly breathing, humming or chanting, and nasal breathing (his vagus-nerve-stimulation protocol), with sunlight on bare eyes and grounding to give the nerve its torque. He adds a castor-oil pack on the navel at night. His TENS vagus protocol keeps settings gentle and stimulation strictly on the left side. The basalt, copper and other passive device therapies serve anyone who wants to avoid electrical current.

- **sources:** [378089](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378089/), [378784](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378784/), [378785](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378785/), [378797](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378797/), [378263](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378263/), [379369](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379369/), [381465](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381465/), [381219](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381219/), [374963](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374963/), [375567](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375567/), [371411](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371411/), [372047](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372047/)

### Tuning forks, binaural beats and sound
- **id:** tuning-forks
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** vagus-sound
- **summary:** Kruse treats sound and vibration as low-risk inputs that recharge the body's piezoelectric tissues, using two tuning forks, analog drums and bowls, humming, and a binaural sleep glide.
- **protocols:** tuning-forks, binaural-sleep-audio

Kruse treats sound and vibration as a gentle, low-risk way to recharge the body's electrical tissues. Pills and peptides, he argues, force metabolism and set off feedback loops that backfire. Sound works with the body's own structure instead, and he calls it a "positive reflexivity trade": an intervention that does not undermine itself. As Earth's field weakens, he adds, people have to supply with sound the "de-fragging" (shaking deuterium loose and restoring order) that the planet used to do for them.

#### How vibration makes current

Bone, tendon and cell membranes are piezoelectric (they produce a small electric current when bent or squeezed). In Kruse's model, a tuning fork's steady vibration flexes these tissues rhythmically and generates a small direct current. That current pushes the voltage of inflamed tissue back toward a healthy negative "healing zone." He gives the shift as roughly +30 mV inflamed to −50 mV healing, and says it is why a fork can ease a stiff joint or sciatic pain.

#### Two forks, two jobs

He uses a 128 Hz fork, pressing its stem on stiff joints, along the sciatic nerve or along the vagus nerve's path. In the evening, the same fork on the sternum or neck acts as a "vagal brake." It quiets the stress branch of the nervous system and tells the brainstem to shift into rest.

His second fork is 40 Hz, the frequency of the brain's gamma rhythm (a fast brain wave tied to focus), used in the morning and at lunch. He grounds the choice in the MIT Picower Institute's decade of 40 Hz gamma-stimulation research in Alzheimer's mice and people. In his view it acts as an acoustic pump that resonates with the brain's support cells, thins the brain fluid and opens the glymphatic system (the brain's waste-clearing flow). He says this stops brain melanin breaking down into a toxic by-product and lifts mood and focus.

Placement matters for clearing. He sets the 40 Hz fork under the occiput (the back of the skull where it meets the neck), over the fourth ventricle at the base of the brain. There, in his model, it works like a plunger loosening deuterium "silt" from the brain's drain. The 40 Hz fork also appears in his other routines: on the spine in the Volcano Protocol, and on the hard palate while chewing mastic gum. He even counts tai chi on the beach as a 40 Hz practice.

#### Analog sound and bone conduction

Kruse separates the slow "push" of breath pressure from the fast "snap" of sound pressure. A large frame drum or gong vibrates the skull bones beside the inner ear and behind the nose by bone conduction (sound traveling through bone rather than air). Because bone and its wrappings are piezoelectric, each shockwave becomes a small voltage spike that, in his model, breaks heavy-water bonds and briefly thins the spinal fluid. Popping your ears on a plane, which needs a firmly closed throat, is a small version of the same snap.

He wants that sound to be analog. Digital speakers drive their cones with pulsed electromagnetic fields and add an electronic hum he calls a "vortex jammer." Himalayan bowls (often a seven-metal alloy including copper), gongs and throat singing make rich natural overtones that the brain reads as nature. Circling a singing bowl around the head creates a rotating field of sound that, he says, helps spin the spinal fluid when the body's own magnetic lift is weak. He calls this "mechanical magnetism," and his own warning goes with it: forcing the vortex too hard can cost neurons around the thalamus.

#### Tones for sleep

For sleep he uses binaural beats. Each ear hears a slightly different tone (for example 400 Hz and 404 Hz), and the brainstem perceives the 4 Hz difference as a beat the brain then follows. About 20 minutes before bed he plays a glide through the theta range (4–7 Hz) to quiet the analytical frontal brain, then pure delta (0.5–3.5 Hz) while falling asleep. In his model delta synchronizes the brain's nightly fluid pulsing and maximizes the overnight removal of deuterium (heavy hydrogen).

Humming or drumming before bed does a related job. He calls it a "pre-glymphatic flush": it stirs heavy water loose so deep, non-dream sleep can carry it away.

He adds two "Solfeggio" tones. He says 528 Hz matches the vibrations of structured water's hydrogen bonds and widens the structured water layer at cell membranes, keeping potassium inside cells. He calls 432 Hz a harmonic of Earth's Schumann resonance and of a slow solar oscillation, and says it steadies the field-sensing iron proteins after a day of technology.

#### Voice, chant and resonant spaces

Kruse traces his older "Shake, Rattle and Roll" prescription to ancient acoustics. Neolithic chambers such as Newgrange, Wayland's Smithy and Malta's Ħal Saflieni Hypogeum resonate between 95 and 120 Hz, centered near 110 Hz. Pilot brain-mapping studies found that a steady 110 Hz tone shifts activity away from the left-side language areas and quiets the prefrontal cortex. Because 110 Hz sits in the male voice range, he concludes the chambers amplified chanting, and he calls this a form of de-fragging suitable even for children. Falling water and chanting "OM" tune the vagus.

Humming and chanting are the body's built-in version, an "internal chewing pump" in his phrase, with Mongolian throat singing the best example. Low humming vibrates the opening at the skull base where the vagus nerve and the deep drainage of the face exit together. He even suggests a cat's purr, and the sounds our own head and neck make, may be self-repair rather than noise. Harmless snoring, where oxygen levels stay steady, is to him the soft palate acting as a built-in tuning fork through the night, and silencing it with surgery or CPAP may remove that help.

#### In practice

His tuning-fork protocol is a 128 Hz fork on stiff joints, the sciatic path or the vagal path, and on the sternum or neck in the evening, plus a 40 Hz fork in the morning and at lunch, including under the back of the skull. He adds analog sound (a frame drum, gong or singing bowl) and low humming or chanting, especially before bed. His binaural sleep audio is a 20-minute theta glide before bed followed by delta while falling asleep, with 528 or 432 Hz tones as options. Recorded audio should play through wired, shielded air-tube headphones or an external speaker in a dark, tech-free bedroom. Bluetooth earbuds, he says, put the very wireless radiation the sound is meant to undo right beside the brain.

- **sources:** [382662](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382662/), [382660](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382660/), [382658](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382658/), [379233](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379233/), [379369](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379369/), [374232](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374232/), [380979](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380979/), [374390](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374390/), [372913](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372913/), [370854](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370854/)

### The nose, palate and teeth
- **id:** nasal-nitric-oxide
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** breathing
- **summary:** Kruse calls the sinuses the body's nitric-oxide factory and an early-warning system, and treats congestion, palate, sleep-airway and dental choices through that lens.
- **protocols:** mastic-gum, sinus-hum-reset, nasal-breathing, dental-care

Kruse sees the nose as far more than an air filter. In his model the sinuses are the body's main factory for nitric oxide, a gas that keeps blood vessels healthy, and a blocked nose is an early warning of trouble in the arteries and brain. His advice on congestion, mouth breathing, jaw growth and dentistry all follows from that.

#### The nose as a nitric-oxide factory

Nitric oxide, like ordinary oxygen, is paramagnetic: it has an unpaired electron, so it responds to magnetic fields. Kruse says the sinuses make it in quantity only when a person is continuously "pinned" (anchored by a stable local field, grounding and sun), and he calls the nose "the keeper of vascular paramagnetism." Breathed through the nose, nitric oxide acts in his view as a lubricant that thins the blood, and he notes that the nasal lining is rich in melanin.

Chronic congestion and mouth breathing are warning signs in his model because mouth breathing bypasses this supply. He says the long leg arteries suffer first, so congestion points toward peripheral artery disease, and that losing the reflex of sneezing in bright sun is another early sign. He treats sinus congestion and hay fever as a collapse of the water inside the skull rooted in location. After facial trauma, a knocked sphenoid bone (the bone behind the nose) can block nasal nitric oxide and bring delayed vertigo. He even says each nostril helps clear one side of the brain, which is why he never had his own nose fixed.

#### Mouth breathing and the growing face

Kruse calls switching to mouth breathing a "mechanical Warburg shift," borrowing the term for cells falling back on a cruder energy mode. Mouth breathers blow off carbon dioxide, which raises blood pH and makes hemoglobin hold oxygen too tightly. In that state, he says, deuterium (heavy hydrogen) cannot be sorted out, blood thickens and oxygen struggles to reach the mitochondria. To him, a change of airway is really a change in the physics of one gas: losing nasal nitric oxide switches off the body's built-in magnetic and isotope filter.

The face pays too. Citing Becker and the 1981 monkey and 1983 child studies of "collapsed faces," he says the tongue resting against the palate during nasal breathing acts like a capacitor, supplying the steady pressure that tells the upper jaw to grow wide. With the mouth open, the bone loses that electrical signal and grows into a long, narrow face. He reads dark circles under a mouth-breathing child's eyes as pooled blood from failed drainage behind the face, and calls tongue and jaw retraining a form of deuterium depletion for the skull.

#### Mastic gum and the palate

Mastic gum is his first choice for congestion. He credits its rare earth elements, which is why he says falim gum is not equivalent. Its dense chew also strengthens the tongue and jaw muscles that hold the airway open. Citing research that such exercises reduce obstructive sleep apnea, he calls it dynamic airway therapy, worth two weeks before CPAP, where a mouthguard is only a static fix. Chewing it while pressing a 40 Hz tuning fork to the hard palate, his "cranial-palatal reset," stimulates the nerve link between the face and the vagus nerve. For a mouth-breathing child he starts with carbonated water, tongue-to-palate retraining and mastic gum.

He reads the night-time airway tools through the same lens. CPAP, in his view, helps mainly through vibration rather than air pressure, standing in mechanically for the tongue's pumping at the back of the face. Taping the mouth shut at night raises the pressure across a carbon-dioxide-sensing center in the brainstem and, in his model, restarts a spinal-fluid spiral that heavy water had stalled.

He warns of the "tailpipe trap": widening a drain while losing its spin. Spinal fluid moves through the skull as a spiral, and mechanical palate expansion (such as MARPE) that ignores that spiral can flatten it so deuterium lags behind. Before any expansion he wants intra-oral red and near-infrared light (660 and 850 nm), then targeted cold, then alternate-nostril breathing, which sends alternating pressure across the sinuses and keeps the fluid spinning.

#### Teeth and gums

Teeth, in his view, are piezoelectric posts that feed current into the skull base and light-carrying crystals grown from the same embryonic tissue as the brain and retina, so dull enamel signals inherited deuterium. He reads cavities as deuterium in the trigeminal nerve (the main nerve of the face). He suspects the curing light used for composite fillings, prefers gold inlays, and for a child's porous new molars uses deuterium-depleted water and mastic gum. Even receding gums can regrow, he says, once deuterium stops blocking light-driven repair.

He holds up the Maya as his model. They set jade inlays in their teeth with an antibiotic cement and, he says, avoided decay while eating fruit and corn all day, grounded. Tribal Maya in El Salvador who keep the practice tell him they need no modern dentistry. Jade and enamel are both piezoelectric, so chewing builds a field that keeps the water in the tooth's fine tubules structured and stops deuterium in saliva from reaching the pulp. Jade also re-emits light, acting like a "dopant" (an added material that changes how a semiconductor behaves) that recharges the tooth's faint natural glow.

Modern materials do the reverse in his view. Plastic and mercury-amalgam fillings act as insulators or as antennas for wireless fields, and he says fluoride and mercury treatments dim the tooth's light. A better-conducting tooth, he adds, helps the skull shed excess charge and deuterium and keeps its fluid spiral turning.

#### In practice

He names four routines. One is mastic gum for congestion and airway strength. Another is his sinus hum reset: hum low through the nose with the mouth closed for 5–10 minutes, aimed at the blocked side; lie with that side up and the head tilted about 45° down for 5–7 minutes; then do 5 minutes of gentle carbon-dioxide-retaining breathing, with a small breath, a 3–5 second nose pinch and 10 seconds of easy nasal breathing. He follows it with 20–30 minutes grounded in morning sun away from routers. The third is constant nasal breathing, with the tongue on the palate and the mouth taped at night. The fourth is his dental-care approach: gold inlays rather than plastic or amalgam, deuterium depletion and repair through light.

- **sources:** [379936](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379936/), [380919](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380919/), [379826](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379826/), [378701](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378701/), [384810](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384810/), [377614](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377614/), [377062](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377062/), [381025](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381025/), [383898](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383898/), [374758](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374758/), [373255](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373255/), [371443](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371443/)

### Jade, the Maya and the solid-state skull
- **id:** jade-maya-skull
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** vagus-sound
- **summary:** Kruse reads the Maya's jade tooth inlays as a solid-state tool that kept teeth light-filled and decay-free, grounded the skull and helped the brain shed deuterium as the field failed, and he suggests jade against the temple as a test for some migraines.
- **protocols:** dental-care

Kruse believes the Maya understood something modern dentistry has lost. They set jade into their teeth, and in his reading this was not decoration but a solid-state device, a piece of crystal that works with the body's own electricity and light. It protected the teeth, grounded the skull and, he argues, helped the Maya stay clear-headed when the magnetic field over their land collapsed. He sees in it lessons for living through the present decline.

#### Builders for a weakening field

Kruse says the Maya began among El Salvador's volcanoes and, as they moved north away from them, built pyramids to mimic volcanic flux, with observatories alongside that suggest they linked a magnetic field in the sky to their volcanoes and pyramids. He reads the pyramids as passive magnetic transducers that "pinned" proton spin as the planet's dipole weakened, "magnetic lighthouses" that held off a collapse into what he calls atavism (a slide back to older, simpler biology), and the Great Pyramid as a planetary deuterium filter rather than a tomb. He ties this to a physics finding of a universal magnetic field threading space, imaged by the LOFAR radio telescope, which he says fits his "Universal Stator" idea. The Maya, he adds, also found ways to bring that protection to individuals, and jade is his main example.

#### Jade in the teeth

Enamel and dentin, in his account, are biological semiconductors that manage the body's light. Teeth give off ultra-weak photon emission (UPE), the faint light living tissue releases, as a by-product of the living pulp. Translucent jade (nephrite or jadeite) acts as a crystal "dopant," an added material that changes how a semiconductor behaves: it captures ambient light and re-emits it, which he says recharges the tooth's light field and keeps the fluid in its fine tubules holding charge, so the tooth does not stagnate into decay.

Jade and enamel are both piezoelectric, producing a charge when squeezed. Chewing therefore builds an electric field where jade, the Maya cement and enamel meet. That field, he says, keeps the water in the dentinal tubules structured (his "EZ" water), which blocks deuterium in saliva from reaching the blood through the tooth's pulp.

He thinks the antibiotic effect of the Maya cement was partly photodynamic, driven by light, not just chemical. By steadying the tooth's faint emission, jade turned the tooth into a "UV lighthouse" that kills microbes, keeps the pulp cells from switching to fermentation (the Warburg shift) and resists plaque biofilms, which he calls primitive atavistic colonies. His bet is that the Maya used inlays once mouth disease appeared. Tribal Maya in El Salvador who still use them, he says, told him they no longer need modern dentistry.

#### Grounding the skull

Because jade conducts better than damaged enamel, Kruse says it improves the skull's grounding and lets the brain dump excess charge and deuterium. In places where the field is declining, he suggests, this may help keep the spinal fluid's vortices turning, and even de-frag the fluid's structure through the sphenoid bone at the skull base without a cochlear or Neuralink-type implant. By contrast he sees plastic and mercury-amalgam fillings as insulators or antennas for wireless fields that dim the tooth's light.

He goes further for the incisor inlays. Jade there, he says, was an ancient form of TMS (transcranial magnetic stimulation) that let the Maya stay conscious when the South Atlantic Anomaly reached them after their Classic period, and it was critical for clearing the drag of deuterium's kinetic isotope effect, the slowing of reactions by heavier atoms. He calls jade the first auricular vagus nerve stimulator (VNS) and, in a Patreon post (CPC #87), the Maya's original "Neuralink" against the last magnetic excursion.

#### A test for migraine

Kruse offers jade as a home test. For a member with right-eye throbbing and vestibular migraine (migraine with dizziness), he suggested finding a piece of green jade or nephrite that fits the eye socket and holding it against the right sphenoid and temple area while scrolling. If the headache and vomiting don't come, he says, that would show the migraine is "solid-state," a problem of the head's electrical and light environment rather than its chemistry.

#### In practice

His dental-care approach favors gold inlays over plastic composites and amalgam, deuterium depletion and light-driven repair; jade is his model for what a filling should do. The jade-against-the-temple test is his suggestion for vestibular migraine. Behind both sits his larger point: in a magnetic decline, conductive, light-handling natural materials in and around the skull help the brain keep shedding deuterium.

- **sources:** [371503](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371503/), [371442](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371442/), [371698](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371698/), [371699](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371699/), [371857](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371857/), [373219](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373219/), [373272](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373272/)

### Releasing lattice lock: Volcano Protocol, hypnosis and shoreline air
- **id:** lattice-lock-release
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** other
- **summary:** Kruse's routines for releasing lattice lock: a body-scale copy of the planet's volcanic response, a water-and-salt flush, hypnosis working from the top down, and sunrise sea spray.
- **protocols:** volcano-protocol, hypnosis-defrag, shoreline-wave-breathing, spinal-lattice-lock

"Lattice lock" is Kruse's name for body water that has turned stiff and deuterium-loaded (heavy hydrogen), jamming the cell's energy motor. He offers three routines to release it: the Volcano Protocol, hypnosis and sunrise shoreline air. He backs them with a water-and-salt flush. His main target is the spine, but he applies them to the lock wherever it appears.

#### What is being released

In his model, tethered cord syndrome and adhesive arachnoiditis (scarring of the membrane around the spinal nerves) are one disease at different times, together making up his "spinal lattice lock." Tethered cord is a "fetal matrix freeze" inherited from parents' deuterium-loaded germline. Arachnoiditis is an "adult matrix freeze," acquired as screens, Wi-Fi, jet lag and a poor local field stop the brain's nightly polarity flip and leave the spinal fluid deuterated. Proteins then fall out of solution as sticky webs, and the body plasters nerve roots to the spinal sheath with scar. That scar is containment, not malfunction, in his view; he says nature does not make mistakes. Epidural steroids, anesthesia, spine surgery, wireless exposure and chronic mouth breathing all speed it up.

The lock can also show up as unsteadiness. He says an acquired ataxia (loss of coordination from the cerebellum) can follow disembarkment syndrome, the lasting sense of rocking after a voyage, as well as prescription-drug complications or long-term creatine abuse. His practice uses the same de-fragging approach on the spinal cord and cerebellum.

#### The Volcano Protocol

When Earth's field declines, he says, volcanoes release carbon dioxide that shields brains, and the Volcano Protocol copies that response at body scale. It is meant for tethered cord, arachnoiditis, ALS and adhesions, and for anyone on the Pacific Rim. It has three parts. First, carbonated water paired with deuterium-depleted water, plus traditional sauna valued for the carbon dioxide it builds rather than its heat, creates a carbon dioxide–bicarbonate loop in the spinal fluid that works against deuterium.

Second, a high-power water jet on the mid and lower back (vertebrae T5–T12) with a 40 Hz tuning fork on the spine shears the coiled collagen of the spinal sheath. That produces a piezoelectric current (electricity made by mechanical stress) that, in his model, shakes deuterium loose from clumped nerve roots: a spinal "de-frag," his word for clearing deuterium and restoring order. A cranial version adds mastic gum and the fork on the hard palate.

Third, sunrise grounding in a magnetically pinned, low-latitude place supplies 380 nm violet light (read by neuropsin, a UV-A sensor in the eye and skin) and solar near-infrared. In his model this rebuilds the mitochondrial membrane's charge, keeps oxygen in its stable form and halts scar-building. The payoff he describes is freer spinal-fluid flow and a flexible heart rate variability (HRV, the beat-to-beat variation that he uses as his gauge). He says anyone promising a timeline is lying.

#### Clearing the motor and flushing the pipes

Kruse pairs deuterium-depleted water (DDW) with hypertonic saline (salt water stronger than blood, 3% in his use) and UV light. He calls DDW clearing at the sub-atomic level, at the ATP motor itself: it keeps new deuterium out and removes the heavy-hydrogen "bridges" that let charge leak across the inner membrane. The saline is plumbing on the large scale. Borrowing from its neurosurgical use for brain swelling, he says the salt surge pulls deuterated fluid out of the brain's chambers and pushes it down through the base of the brain into the spinal "exhaust." The lasting drop in brain water after saline is, to him, really a purge of heavy hydrogen.

He gives each part a role. Saline is the burst that clears the nerve-root sleeves and the venous spaces behind the eyes. DDW is the maintenance that stops deuterium returning, and cold is the final gear. Combined with UV light he calls it "tanning the interior": thinning the inner fluid until light signals reach the pineal and pituitary glands again. He applies it especially to children whose growth has stalled, aiming to act before the lock sets for good.

#### Hypnosis

Kruse treats hypnosis not as relaxation but as a top-down amplifier. Trance shifts the cortex from fast rhythms into slower alpha and theta waves, mimicking the start of the nightly polarity flip; he cites Becker's 1961 work and Bukalo's study of nerves firing in reverse. In his model it sends a direct-current shift down the spinal cord that lowers resistance around the nerves and clears "electronic dams" holding the lowest nerve roots.

Slow belly breathing during trance raises carbon dioxide into bicarbonate, which thins the spinal fluid, and calm suppresses cortisol surges. That is why he wants the fork and water jet applied straight afterward, while the person is still deeply relaxed. He says hypnosis also helps sleep by removing the day's electrical noise.

#### Shoreline air

Breaking waves electrify their spray, a process called the Lenard effect. Heavier droplets keep a positive charge and fall back, while the finest spray carries a negative charge, so the shore is blanketed in negative ions. Kruse adds that a curling wave acts like a centrifuge, flinging heavy water outward while light water leaves as spray.

Breathed at sunrise, he says, these ions stimulate the thinly insulated smell nerves, and the charge travels down a brainstem-to-gut nerve tract to the gut's nerve centers. There it neutralizes "rusted melanin" (degraded pigment debris), restores structured water and turns a rigid HRV flexible. Any net negative-ion exposure counts, including falling water. Ion-poor indoor air, such as a dentist's office, is the reverse, and he blames it for lung disease.

#### Why relief doesn't last

Kruse explains why massage or vibration relief often fades within about two days. Pressure supplies the energy to break heavy-hydrogen bonds and briefly loosen the tissue. But while the person's deuterium load stays high and the field around them stays weak, the water stiffens back to its locked state. Prolotherapy (injections meant to provoke tissue repair) can help in his view, but he expects it to fail in heavily deuterated, thickened "rhino skin." The same rule covers a successful de-frag: return to a bad environment and the lock comes back.

#### In practice

His Volcano Protocol and spinal lattice lock protocols combine carbonated and deuterium-depleted water, sauna, the water jet with the 40 Hz fork on T5–T12, and sunrise grounding on pinned volcanic ground, while avoiding flights across time zones. Progress is tracked by HRV in deep sleep. His hypnosis de-frag starts with learning hypnosis or self-hypnosis. Fifteen minutes before a session he drinks a glass of deuterium-depleted or carbonated water, does the session grounded in a pinned place, and applies the fork and jet right afterward. His shoreline wave breathing means breathing breaking-wave spray every morning with the sunrise. He ranks peptides as temporary and pairs 3% saline with deuterium-depleted water and UV as a gentler osmotic reset. Morning infrared light, grounding and a good location are what he calls the lasting fix.

- **sources:** [381076](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381076/), [380979](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380979/), [380967](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380967/), [380923](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380923/), [380921](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380921/), [381078](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381078/), [381079](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381079/), [377904](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377904/), [374372](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374372/), [375493](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375493/), [371600](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371600/), [370972](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/370972/)

### Time, mind and consciousness
- **id:** time-and-consciousness
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse ties the felt speed of time, memory, wisdom, belief and consciousness to light, water and oxygen's spin in the brain.
- **protocols:** time-expansion-novelty

Kruse treats the mind as a physical readout of light, water and magnetism. In his model, how fast time seems to pass, how well we remember, what we believe and even whether we are conscious all depend on the electrical state of brain cells. Fixing that state, not willpower or drugs, is how he says to change the mind.

#### Why time seems to speed up

When days blur together and life seems to rush past, Kruse calls it "time compression." In his model the mitochondria of the thalamus (the brain's relay and pacing center) are stalled in Marcus inversion, a state where electron flow jams because it is being pushed too hard. Inner processing then lags behind the outside world. Losing stable oxygen also speeds up protein turnover and aging, so the body's clocks run fast even though wall-clock time has not changed. This, he says, is how people "lose time" as Earth's field weakens.

Memory depends on contrast. He says monotony melts the brain's memory-forming structure, because a vivid memory needs a sharp burst of reactive oxygen and a flash of light inside the cell to switch on the genes for BDNF (a growth factor that builds connections between nerve cells). Sameness flattens that signal and memories smear together.

He also says biological time is manufactured. Light itself experiences no time; time appears when matter slows light down and when the cell's iron proteins step electrons down one by one, and the brain builds a slightly delayed "present" from those signals. Oxygen's spin sets the direction. Reactive "singlet" oxygen subtracts biological time, stable "triplet" oxygen adds it, and places with higher energy density slow time locally, which he says is why ancient sites were built where they were.

#### What consciousness needs

In Kruse's model consciousness needs three coupled inputs. The first is cell water in its structured, charge-holding state, which he scores as "160" against 78 for ordinary water. The second is 380 nm violet light reaching neuropsin (a UV-A sensor in the eye and skin). The third is carbon dioxide as a buffer. Because water, not microtubules, gates the process, he reads recent work on a 71-terahertz water vibration as undercutting Hameroff and Penrose's Orch-OR theory, which places consciousness in microtubules. Anesthesia, unconsciousness and neurodegeneration, he says, share one fallback energy state.

He calls microtubule theories that leave out the surrounding water "dry biophysics." Anesthetics, in his view, work by disordering the water around proteins, not by dissolving into fat. Deuterium in that water adds mass that anchors the tubule's proteins so they cannot vibrate fast enough to hold memory. He credits Robert Becker, rather than Itzhak Bentov, as the first to link physics to consciousness, and cites Becker's finding that the body's direct-current polarity reverses under anesthesia.

He reads Nikola Tesla's remark that his brain was only a receiver literally, through David Bohm's physics: the brain picks up thought rather than generating it. A June 2026 preprint (Kansala, St. Jean, Rouleau and Murugan) on weak, non-heating stimulation patterned like a learning signal convinces him the brain responds to pattern, not power. In his model that is why wireless signals and evening blue light harm thinking. He adds that the Arc gene, copied from an ancient virus, builds virus-like shells that carry memory between nerve cells and give the brain a magnetic memory; once a person is pinned, he says, depressive memories can fade.

#### Wisdom needs quiet

Kruse ties wisdom to timing inside the cell. He says the brain needs about 7.2 seconds free of collisions with heavy hydrogen for its electrons to cross into the stable triplet state, and a noisy, deuterium-loaded interior never gets that quiet. When the repair window is overrun, thinking drops into an older, survival-driven mode. He borrows a member's image for this: an operating system downgraded from a modern computer to DOS. He reads today's widespread apathy, anxiety and brain fog as people who are "cognitively lattice locked," their brain water too stiff for clear thought.

The reverse is waking with the answer to yesterday's unsolved problem. To him, that shows the brain reached coherence overnight.

#### Belief, mood and the field

Beliefs and habits, to Kruse, are field imprints, recorded the way cooling volcanic basalt locks in Earth's magnetism; he calls habits "magnetic craters." Anxiety and dread are readouts of cells melting down. Brain fog is the thalamus losing its spin-filtered current, and he cites zebrafish that learned about half as fast under alternating magnetic fields. Depression follows when melanin breaks down and diverts the brain's chemistry toward a by-product that silences BDNF.

He does not think the mind can simply override a bad environment. It can break the rules, but pays for it in entropy (disorder), and bad thinking is meant as feedback. That is why he rejects Joe Dispenza's feel-good approach: first feel what the environment is telling you and move somewhere better, then expect to feel good. In a magnetic decline, he adds, the six people closest to you matter more, because they can tell when your own dopamine is leading you astray.

His treatment logic follows. He calls depression a collapse of energy density, and says antidepressants knock one "billiard ball" while the deuterium (heavy hydrogen) stays, so he clears deuterium first and then adds sun and grounding. Speech and prayer follow the same rules. A grounded, sunlit speaker spin-aligns the oxygen in the airway so words carry, while an ungrounded speaker under fluorescent light produces "empty words."

#### In practice

His time-expansion protocol pairs novelty with environmental repair. He recommends new routes, hobbies and learning, journaling or mindfulness, and slow, deep breathing, alongside fixing light, water and magnetism, and choosing your closest company with care. For prayer or intention: ground first, cut nearby EMF and avoid fluorescent light. For brain fog he prescribes dawn barefoot on surf, sand or dew, sunrise on bare eyes without glasses or contacts, and routers off at night. Where the place itself is the problem, he puts moving ahead of mindset work.

- **sources:** [383764](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383764/), [383771](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383771/), [385066](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385066/), [379275](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379275/), [379628](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379628/), [379592](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379592/), [380134](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380134/), [385186](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385186/), [383781](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383781/), [382666](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382666/), [373390](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373390/), [372469](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372469/)

### Drugs and supplements as "atomic impurities"
- **id:** atomic-impurities
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse says foreign compounds add electron traps to the body's semiconductors, that some drugs only seem to work in a deuterium-loaded body, and that pills skip the light-driven steps that make the body's own versions work.
- **protocols:** pills-he-avoids

Kruse argues that most drugs and supplements do more harm than good because of what they do to the cell's electrical wiring. In his model the proteins of the mitochondrial membrane behave like semiconductors, passing electrons along a chain. A foreign compound acts like a stray atom in a computer chip: it traps electrons instead of passing them on.

#### Electron traps

Borrowing Ramon Sun's term, he calls such compounds "atomic impurities." His list includes GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, statins, synthetic peptides, NMN and other NAD⁺ precursors, metformin, rapamycin and Epitalon, which he calls "a GLP-1 in a different box." Each adds traps to a network that is already out of alignment, deepens the leak in the blood–brain barrier and speeds the cell's loss of energy as heat. Statins, he adds, drain the mitochondrial membrane's charge, and no drug can fix a bad location.

NAD⁺, a molecule that shuttles electrons in energy production, is his clearest example. He says the ratio of NAD⁺ to its partner NADH simply reflects the membrane's charge, so it rises without pills when you remove AC fields and add morning sun and cold. He cites a May 2026 Nature Metabolism study of more than 1,100 blood samples showing whole-blood NAD⁺ stays flat from age 20 to 100 and doesn't change with exercise or diet. Since there is no deficiency to correct, NR and NMN push the system rather than repair it, unlike vitamin C curing scurvy.

He pictures NAD⁺ as the cell's "protonic capacitor," holding charge in the way albumin holds fluid in the blood. When it runs low, signals leak. NMN, to him, is too weak a chemical jump-start to overcome the kinetic isotope effect (the slowing of reactions when heavy hydrogen stands in for light hydrogen).

Injected peptides cause a similar problem in his view. The body cuts its own peptides from a larger precursor on a light-timed schedule set by the brain's master clock. Flooding the blood with synthetic versions reads as a surplus, so the clock shuts down native production, and the user ends up dependent on the needle.

#### Drugs that only seem to work

Kruse argues that several popular drugs help only because the patient is already deuterium-loaded. Metformin works by slowing Complex I, the first step of the mitochondrial energy chain. He says that makes sense only when the cell's NADH carries heavy hydrogen, his "NADD⁺." Once the cell's water is restored and deuterium is kept out of the chain, metformin becomes a poison. He applies the same logic to statins.

Methylene blue is his "ungrounded quantum bypass." It hands electrons from NADH almost straight to the end of the chain, skipping two steps. In a cell whose water is already degraded, or in a place where the field is unstable, he says this forces current through a system that cannot contain it. He cites isolated brain-mitochondria studies in which it raised hydrogen peroxide output up to fifteen-fold. Because it absorbs red and near-infrared light strongly, he warns against pairing it with strong tropical sun, and calls its focus boost a brief rush that clears no deuterium.

Creatine draws the same objection. It raises the brain's phosphate reserve and gives a burst of mental energy and a false sense of recovery. In his image it is coal shoveled into a steam engine whose pipes are clogged. He links long-term creatine abuse to an "empty sella," a flattened pituitary gland in its bony seat.

SSRI antidepressants he calls "deuterium bombs." Many are fluorinated, and fluorine is the most electron-hungry element. He says these drugs thicken the spinal and tissue fluid and lower its ability to hold charge, letting deuterium settle around the pelvic nerves. In his model that is why they numb sexual response and emotion, and why post-SSRI sexual dysfunction is physical rather than psychological.
