#### Pills that skip the light-driven steps

Kruse says pills skip steps that sunlight is supposed to run in mitochondria. Melatonin and vitamin D pills bypass the light-driven routes that make the body's own versions work, and he cites an observational study of more than 5,000 older adults in which vitamin D pills were linked to faster cognitive decline in people with normal levels. Magnesium helps at first, since it assists dozens of mitochondrial pathways. But a cell running at low voltage cannot hold onto it, which he calls the "missing dopant" problem.

He goes on through the list. Chronic vitamin C blocks melanin renewal, supplemental copper damages the body's own melanin, and glycine cannot rebuild iron-containing heme proteins while the field declines. Aspirin is a mitochondrial toxin that lowers melanin. Modafinil, in a low-voltage, unpinned brain, breaks apart into reactive fragments that cause headaches, anxiety and fog. He does accept sulforaphane and allicin as short-term aids for the liver, nerves and thick blood. He credits hesperidin, which is chiral (one-handed) like melanin, with quenching reactive singlet oxygen.

#### Fever, antioxidants and hospital fluids

For about 25 years Kruse has rejected the antioxidant story. Radicals and oxygen, he says, are fuel for the cell's light-driven engine; the real problem is a weak magnetic field that cannot contain them. Isolated ascorbic acid in a low-oxygen body can turn pro-oxidant and drain glutathione (the cell's main internal antioxidant). Whole-food vitamin C comes with flavonoids such as quercetin and rutin that let it recycle itself.

Fever, in his model, is a repair program, a "hypothalamic de-frag" that uses infrared heat to speed enzyme repair. Acetaminophen (Tylenol) breaks it, because its breakdown product uses up glutathione, and he calls giving it to infants after their shots especially harmful.

Deuterium also gets into methyl groups (the small chemical tags that switch genes on and off) from synthetic supplements and processed food. Heavier tags are harder to add or remove, so to him "altered methylation" is atomic mass jamming the machinery. He says MRI gadolinium contrast, like CT contrast, enters the brain and stays. He calls thyroid hormone replacement a mistake and lists hyperbaric oxygen, supplemental oxygen and ozone as risky. By contrast he calls Lactated Ringer's, the IV fluid he used for 25 years as a surgeon, the hospital's "stealth" deuterium-depleting fluid, because its lactate keeps the cell's inner water charged in a way sugar-water drips cannot.

Medium-chain triglycerides (MCTs) earn his approval for a related reason. They skip fat storage and go straight to the liver, which turns them into ketones, and ketones, he says, shift the brain's AMPA-receptor loop toward less excitatory glutamate and more calming GABA. Burning MCTs also yields deuterium-depleted metabolic water at once, the thin, low-viscosity water he says the vagus nerve needs to power the body's two-litre bicarbonate exhaust, which clears "silt" from the brain.

#### The isotope problem

Isotopes add a second layer. Citing Cameron Borg, Kruse says living enzymes place light hydrogen exactly where electrons and protons must tunnel and park deuterium (heavy hydrogen) in harmless spots. Chemical synthesis scatters heavy atoms at random, so even a "deuterium-depleted" pill can put a heavy atom on a working hinge. Whole food eaten in sunlight lets the body filter isotopes smoothly. Concentrated pills force it to spend energy constantly ejecting mismatched atoms.

Lithium is his sharpest case, a "subatomic monkey wrench." Natural lithium mixes two isotopes with different nuclear spins, which respond differently to magnetic fields. He describes the thalamus as an FM radio that locks the body clock to Earth and Sun, and says the mixed signal jams it. Lithium also disorders structured brain water (he cites Gilbert Ling), weakens the vagus nerve's deuterium drain and settles in the thyroid. Biology chose magnesium, he notes, and he singles out poorly balanced commercial lithium supplements. He does separate the two isotopes. The nucleus, in his account, is the atom's magnetic antenna, and lithium-6, with a nuclear spin of 1, holds quantum coherence longer than lithium-7, with a spin of 3/2. In his framework lithium-6 is the dopant that lets the vagus nerve lock the heart's vortex in phase with Earth's dynamo, which is why a mixed, unbalanced dose scrambles the signal.

#### Reflexivity

He frames the whole category with "biological reflexivity," borrowed from George Soros's idea that investors move the markets they bet on. Every drug, and every biomarker a person chases, changes the biology it targets. Effects arrive on different timescales, and success can undermine the very reasoning behind the intervention. Peptides injected into a low-voltage body, he says, set off self-reinforcing loops that speed burnout.

#### In practice

His "pills he avoids" list includes oral vitamin D3, chronic vitamin C, NMN, NR and NAD⁺ injections, metformin, rapamycin, statins and aspirin. It also covers GLP-1 drugs, Epitalon and sleep or repair peptides (he names BPC-157, CJC-1295, Ipamorelin and Pinealon), melatonin pills, and chronic magnesium pills. Isolated antioxidants, copper supplements, lithium and modafinil round it out, along with methylene blue, creatine, SSRIs, acetaminophen for fever, thyroid hormone replacement, and hyperbaric or supplemental oxygen. In their place he puts the inputs he says the pills imitate: morning sun, grounding, cold, removal of AC fields and a good location.

- **sources:** [371117](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371117/), [383708](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383708/), [382667](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382667/), [382155](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382155/), [379373](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379373/), [378299](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378299/), [377752](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377752/), [382656](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382656/), [374080](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374080/), [382654](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382654/), [372909](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372909/), [373079](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373079/)

### His critique of "centralized medicine"
- **id:** centralized-medicine
- **pillar:** support
- **group:** body-model
- **summary:** Kruse argues that modern medicine dropped physics for biochemistry and drugs, recast the sun as a hazard, and urges members to verify for themselves and act on light, field and ground.
- **protocols:** iv-fluids-acute-collapse

Kruse uses "centralized medicine" for the mainstream system of drugs, guidelines and institutions, and argues it has built itself on the wrong layer of biology. Beneath biochemistry, in his model, runs a deeper operating system of light, water, magnetism and membrane voltage, and medicine was trained not to look at it. Understanding that history is, for him, the reason to take health into your own hands.

#### How the split happened

He traces it to the 1910 Flexner Report, funded by Carnegie and Rockefeller foundations. He grants that it improved medical training, but says it tied accreditation to a chemistry-set model of the body. That pushed out biophysics, naturopathy and chiropractic and gave rise to a drug industry that barely existed before. Disease was redefined as a local chemical shortage rather than a failure of field, spin and structure, his "pharma over physics." In his image, molecules are only the letters of biology; the light signal is the sentence and the spin of oxygen and metals is the meaning.

Dropping biophysics, he says, is what made drugs that slow the mitochondrial energy chain, such as statins, look necessary. It also hid the fact that the chain's final enzyme, cytochrome c oxidase, makes deuterium-depleted water so the cell can run on light hydrogen. He goes further, claiming the same interests' biotech work began loading the food and water supply with deuterium (heavy hydrogen) in the 1940s.

Money follows the same split. He describes a health pyramid in which investment flows to drugs and diagnostics at the top, while the base with the most leverage gets little: light, magnetism, deuterium, water and daily rhythm. Medicine targets cholesterol, he says, because only molecules can be patented.

#### Blind spots he names

Biochemistry, in his view, assumes Earth's magnetic field never changes, so its textbook diagrams fail once oxygen flips into its reactive state. The same mindset assumes slow, steady change and cannot see magnetic excursions (rapid swings in the field). He sorts thinkers into "parrots," who repeat isolated mechanisms, and "eagles," who see the whole system from above, and says the dimension most people miss is the magnetic and spin layer. Doctors themselves, he argues, live in the same indoor, deuterium-loaded state as their patients, so they cannot perceive the problem and reach for more drugs. Intensive care, he adds, has no algorithm for clearing a deuterium-locked patient.

The mindset also builds human guidelines on nocturnal rodents, animals with inverted daily rhythms and different skulls and eyes. He calls this the "nocturnal mouse fallacy." Birds, which are active by day and came through the extinction that ended the dinosaurs, are to him the better model: like us they use the morning sun to prime the energy motor and melanin to protect their water. Studying human diabetes in mice, he says, is like repairing a 747 from a submarine manual.

Statin trials, in his reading, share one uncontrolled variable. Every participant lives indoors under wireless fields and drinks ordinary deuterium-rich water, so any drug that slows mitochondria will look protective. A heart left too weak for a sudden heart attack gets labeled "stable," which he calls the stability of a cemetery. He says consensus is not science, dissenters such as Ignaz Semmelweis get branded cranks, and Nobel prizes reward ideas that sell drugs.

He extends the critique to people and history. The longevity industry (David Sinclair, Aubrey de Grey and the Valter Longo fasting school) and diet experts (Ben Bikman, Peter Attia, Andrew Huberman) stay trapped in biochemical models and ignore light. He no longer engages nutrition writers such as Sally Fallon or Chris Masterjohn for the same reason. Health influencers who sell subscriptions, supplements and drug or peptide regimens are, to him, "time thieves" who build dependency. He ties the American Cancer Society's founding and SV40 contamination of polio vaccine to Rockefeller and intelligence networks. He notes that Executive Order 13139 still allows experimental drugs to be given to troops without consent. He also warns that AI chatbots default to consensus and flatter their users, citing the invented eye disease "bixonimania" that chatbots repeated as real.

#### How the sun became the enemy

His longest history concerns sunlight. He traces sun worship to the Sumerians around 3000 BC, a legal right to sunlight in Justinian's Roman code, and Victorian prestige for pale skin. The 1903 Nobel Prize went to Niels Finsen for treating a skin form of tuberculosis with light, and by 1933 sunlight was being used against 165 diseases. From the 1980s the message flipped: the sun causes cancer, block all UV, vitamin D deficiency is a crisis, take a pill instead of the source.

To him it is telling that the sun was recast as a carcinogen in the same century the supplement was patented. He reads Hawaii's 2026 ban on six sunscreen chemicals as the first official sign that we have been poisoning our own light antenna.

#### Cautionary tales

His stories share one moral: institutions protect shareholders and products before people. The Radium Girls, dial painters in the 1910s and 1920s, were told radium paint was harmless and taught to shape their brushes with their lips while executives handled it behind screens. Radium settled in their bones, and their lawsuit helped create workplace safety law. Harvey Wiley's "Poison Squad," twelve volunteers fed food preservatives for five years, forced the 1906 Pure Food and Drug Act, and he says that today, with constant low-dose additives, "we are all the twelve men." He also cites Richard Ablin, who discovered PSA and says it became a misused prostate-cancer screen.

He also shares a timeline of confident medical advice that aged badly. It runs from industry-funded reassurance about smoking around 1950 and Ancel Keys's low-fat advice in 1958, through thalidomide and the long-hidden dependency risk of benzodiazepines. Then come Vioxx, which ended in a $4.85 billion Merck settlement with no criminal charges, and OxyContin's "under 1%" addiction claim, drawn from a short letter about hospitalized patients. It closes with statins for people without heart disease, where about 250 must take the drug for one to benefit. The lesson he draws is that a doctor's confidence is itself a risk.

#### In practice

Kruse's prescriptions are about mindset rather than a protocol. "Verify, don't outsource your thinking," and start from wherever your own understanding is. Because institutions replaced a moral "should" with a legal "must," and because politicians and other non-clinicians now dictate what doctors may prescribe, he treats refusing to comply with an ungrounded establishment as an ethical duty. And he asks members to accept "Personal Quantum Solvency": food, exercise and drugs cannot fix a changing magnetic environment, so act on location, light and grounding.

- **sources:** [384740](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384740/), [378864](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378864/), [380785](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/380785/), [381130](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/381130/), [384445](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384445/), [377915](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377915/), [377244](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/377244/), [378126](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378126/), [375652](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/375652/), [384551](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/384551/), [373674](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373674/), [372807](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372807/)

## Conditions

### Cancer as a voltage failure
- **id:** cancer-voltage
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse says any lasting fall in the voltage of the mitochondria's inner membrane can produce cancer, a protective retreat and an electrical short circuit that the environment, not drugs, must reverse.
- **protocols:** cancer-glioma

Kruse sees cancer as an electrical failure rather than mainly a genetic one. In his model the cell's power plants, the mitochondria, hold a strong charge across their inner membrane, and anything that lets that voltage sag for long enough can tip a cell into cancer; he even names the fields around wind turbines, given years of exposure. The tumor, to him, is a retreat the cell makes to survive, so the fix lies in restoring the environment rather than in drugs.

#### How a cell falls into cancer

Kruse describes a healthy cell at about −70 millivolts and a failing one at −15 to −30. At that lower level, he says, a gate in the inner membrane (UCP-2) loses the voltage that keeps it sealed. Water rich in deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen with an extra neutron, then floods the mitochondria's interior.

In his account deuterium's extra mass brakes ATP synthase, the spinning motor that makes the cell's energy currency, ATP. Complex IV, the last step of the energy chain, then stops making the cell's own clean, low-deuterium water. Without that water the field across the inner membrane short-circuits.

He traces the slide one step further back as well. NAD+, the carrier that ferries electrons into the energy chain, can pick up deuterium in place of light hydrogen, and once that buffer is lost oxygen turns reactive, the membrane charge drops, and deuterium floods first the cell's fluid and then the mitochondria's interior. The share of damaged mitochondrial DNA in the cell (its heteroplasmy) climbs as this happens.

The cell then falls back on fermentation, the ancient oxygen-free way of burning sugar, and begins dividing out of control. This switch is called the Warburg shift, after Otto Warburg, who saw cancer as damaged respiration rather than mutation; Kruse says his voltage model completes Warburg's picture, and he calls cancer an atavism, a throwback to an older way of living, rather than a disease.

#### The "matrix fire" and its causes

Kruse calls the stalled interior a "matrix fire." Oxygen that can't be used cleanly flips into singlet oxygen, a reactive form that attacks membranes and DNA, and the faint light cells naturally give off (ultra-weak photon emission) turns into chaotic noise. The growth pathways cancer drugs target (PI3K, Akt, mTOR) are, in his reading, readouts of that fire, not its cause.

He names four things that uncouple Complex IV: missing near-infrared light; nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields from wireless devices and wiring); deuterium; and the wrong copper isotope, since he says Complex IV is built to run on heavy copper-65. Like weight gain, he suggests, cancer can be a "safe mode": a lower-energy state that staves off sudden organ failure and buys time, which can be won back once the interference is removed.

#### A body-wide electrical circuit

Kruse draws support from Björn Nordenström, a radiologist at Sweden's Karolinska Institute who built on Robert O. Becker's work. Nordenström described a second circulation in the body, an electrical one running through blood vessels and the fluid between cells, which he called "biologically closed electric circuits." Between 1978 and 1983 he treated lung and breast tumors by inserting electrodes and passing a weak direct current, which drew white blood cells to the tumor and shut down its blood supply.

Chinese doctors later used this "electrochemical therapy" on more than 10,000 patients, Kruse notes, reporting success above 70% in some solid tumors, while Western medicine looked away. He tells a similar story about Georges Lakhovsky, who in 1925 at the Salpêtrière hospital in Paris infected geraniums with a tumor-causing bacterium; the one plant circled by an open copper ring shed its tumor while the rest died. Work like this was buried, he says, because a copper ring is free and chemotherapy is not. To him it confirms cancer as a bioelectric short circuit.

#### Magnetism, the brain and the night

Magnetism sits at the root of his model. Kruse says oxygen keeps its safe, stable form (triplet oxygen) only when a person is "pinned": grounded, sunlit and living under a stable local magnetic field. His shortest chain for glioma, a brain tumor, is no pinning, then singlet oxygen, then glioma. Glioblastoma, he argues, starts when foreign magnetic fields break the junctions that link mitochondria into one network, and restoring that connection can extend survival beyond what doctors expect.

Because Earth's field is weakening, he expects a magnetic excursion (a large, rapid drop and shift in the field) to drive many cancers. Without spin pinning, chromosomes misalign as cells divide, and the mutations oncology tracks are debris left by that failure.

He reads Australia's melanoma epidemic as an early example. It began on the west of the continent and has spread to the Sydney coast, which he ties to the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), a vast and growing weak patch in Earth's field now reaching into the Indian Ocean. The skin's layered network of small arteries works, in his model, as a "distal refinery" that sheds heat and deuterium through cold and sweat. Once those vessels lose their melanin, the light-absorbing pigment, they can no longer shield the skin's mitochondria from the combined load of light and nnEMF, and melanoma follows.

Night is part of the picture. Citing studies from Russel Reiter's group, Kruse says cancer cells drift back toward normal oxygen-burning at night, when mitochondria release melatonin, and return to fermentation by day. He calls this nightly repair "magnetic chemotherapy," and says light at night switches it off.

#### In practice

His priorities for cancer and glioma: get magnetically pinned first; ground; take natural UV-A and infrared sunlight and don't fear UV-B; remove nnEMF; and for glioma, sleep in a cold bedroom. At high latitudes, he adds, naturally low-deuterium water won't protect someone who lives indoors under artificial light. He believes UV-A light, life on a zero-declination line and tree resins such as mastic gum, which he says are naturally low in deuterium, can help turn cancer cells back from fermentation toward normal respiration. He calls a ketogenic diet unwise as a cancer strategy because many aggressive tumors (prostate, glioblastoma, triple-negative breast) burn fat too, so cutting glucose alone can select fat-burning clones. Nor does he see drugs as the main answer, pointing to a 15-year Oxford trial in the *New England Journal of Medicine* in which surgery or radiation for localized prostate cancer did not lengthen survival compared with monitoring.

- **sources:** [385641](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385641/), [378783](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378783/), [379984](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/379984/), [382301](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382301/), [378767](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378767/), [385873](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/385873/), [378207](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/378207/), [382394](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/382394/), [374830](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/374830/), [383346](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/383346/), [373641](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373641/), [371193](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/371193/)

### Diabetes and blood sugar
- **id:** diabetes-light-field
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse treats insulin resistance as a protective circuit breaker, and type 1 and type 2 diabetes and the dawn sugar rise as light, deuterium and magnetic problems rather than food problems.
- **protocols:** type-1-diabetes, type-2-dawn-sugar

Kruse treats diabetes as a problem of light, deuterium and magnetism, not of food. In his model the pancreas is one of the body's main exits for deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen that jams the cell's energy machinery, and diabetes follows when indoor light and an unstable magnetic environment block that exit. He holds that type 2 diabetes is reversible and that counting carbohydrates and insulin units treats only the surface.

#### Insulin resistance as a circuit breaker

Kruse calls insulin resistance a protective shield, not a defect: a circuit breaker that trips when a cell is offered more fuel than it can safely burn. Indoor spaces, he says, are dominated by blue light and magnetically "dead," and running the cell's energy chain under that light upsets the electrons' spin, which drives insulin spikes. He puts high blood sugar and insulin down mainly to light and to a lack of "pinning" (being grounded under a stable local field), and says meal timing under LEDs drives type 2 diabetes more than food does.

In a low-field room full of nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields), he says, burning glucose is a "dirty burn" that throws off flares of the faint light cells emit and leaves deuterium behind. Extra fuel would stall ATP synthase, the cell's energy motor, and could tip the cell toward cancer, so refusing it is self-protection. Forcing glucose in with injected insulin is, in his phrase, a "thermodynamic crime," like pushing more current through a transformer that is already shorting; he says it damages DNA and the melanin lining the blood vessels.

The shield works, in his account, through the glycerol-3-phosphate shuttle, a back-door route that carries hydrogen into the mitochondria. He says it runs only during insulin resistance and favors light hydrogen, so deuterium is left behind in the cell fluid instead of jamming the energy motor. He points to animals that use this on purpose: the bar-tailed godwit flying 11,000 km without food, and the naked mole-rat surviving without oxygen. High-dose insulin, by contrast, forces deuterium in; he calls it a "biophysical degausser" because it strips cell membranes of their charge.

The godwit, he adds, makes the flight with high blood sugar and full insulin resistance, burning its own fat and gut, yet performs at its peak. What protects it is the pecten oculi, a comb of blood vessels packed with melanin inside the bird's eye, which he calls a "photonic heat sink": it turns the metabolic noise of the flight into usable current and shields the bird's magnetic compass. Humans do the same, in his account, with the choroid plexus (the tissue that makes spinal fluid) and the retina's pigment layer. High sugar is survivable when that melanin shielding is intact, and diabetics, he says, have stripped theirs with nnEMF and blue light. Research on night-active mice misses this, because day-living birds and humans share a melanin-led clock.

Fat burning gives the cell another way to set sugar aside. Burning saturated fat, Kruse says, pushes electrons backward through Complex I, the energy chain's entry point, and an iron-sulfur sensor there reads this reverse electron flow as a signal to skip glucose and fructose. That is why, to him, high blood sugar in the cold can be healthy.

#### The beta cell's jammed motor

In type 2, Kruse says, the beta cell (the pancreas cell that makes insulin) is usually full of insulin but can't release it. Release needs a burst of energy from ATP synthase, the cell's rotary motor. Table sugar and corn syrup, which he says plants load with deuterium, eaten under blue light without balancing red light, break up the cell's structured water and let deuterium jam that motor. "Beta-cell fatigue" is therefore, to him, an isotope jam; left long enough, singlet oxygen (a reactive form of oxygen) damages the cell until the body removes it.

The pancreas normally clears deuterium through what he calls the vagal "tailpipe." The vagus nerve drives an enzyme, carbonic anhydrase, to make bicarbonate, which binds deuterium and carries it out through the pancreatic ducts into the gut. Sugar under blue light, or a magnetic excursion, knocks the enzyme out of alignment and "welds the tailpipe shut," so deuterium backs up into the beta cells.

Clearing that exhaust, Kruse argues, lets the cell resolve its own diabetes whatever the carbohydrate intake. Only once the flush runs, shown by a well-formed (Bristol type 4) stool, can beta cells make insulin again, so the key is clearing deuterium from the vagal nerves that serve them. He says sunlight lowers blood glucose by about 27%, that advanced type 2 patients have regained insulin secretion with deuterium depletion, and that beta-cell stem cells can recover through the light-driven repair Robert O. Becker described. Type 2 diabetics also lose less weight on GLP-1 drugs, he says, because their energy chain is already stalled ("Marcus-inverted," pushed past the point where electrons can flow), so the drug strips muscle rather than fat.

#### The dawn rise and a shifting field

The morning rise in blood sugar, the "dawn phenomenon," is in his reading a failure to catch the morning magnetic shift, not a hormone surge. At daybreak the upper atmosphere ionizes and the local field changes, and healthy cells use that wave to wake. A diabetic's damaged membranes can't capture it, so voltage drops and the liver dumps glucose to keep the cell's battery alive.

Kruse calls diabetics "magnetic messes" who all need an agonic line, a place where compass north and true north coincide, which he treats as the zone of most coherent field. As the poles move, these lines drift across continents, leaving people under a field their ancestry wasn't adapted to, and he expects a weakening field to "mint" millions of new diabetics.

India is his main example. He has tracked India's diabetes alongside Australia's melanoma for about ten years and treats them as one disease of the South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA), the vast weak patch in Earth's field, which he believes has pushed into the southern Indian Ocean and reached the southern tip of India. A population that ate deuterium-rich staples for 5,000 years without diabetes now has about 170 million diabetics, and he notes new cases of both types among sun-rich, darkly pigmented people on the volcanic island of Réunion.

The damage, he says, travels through the pancreas's blood supply. Beta cells draw up to a fifth of the pancreas's blood flow from a dense capillary bed, much like the brain's, fed from the superior mesenteric artery, the main artery to the gut. A shifting field and nnEMF load that artery with deuterium and block the body's own melanin-making, "bleaching" the arterial tree even while the skin stays dark. He says surgeon friends in India found more disease in the aorta and this artery in diabetics, and he reads the rise in pancreatic cancer since 2015 as the same process with more deuterium.

#### Type 1

Type 1 he frames as a deuterium and magnetic problem too, worse at high latitudes and mildest near the equator. Blue light without sunlight's near-infrared can't be used, so the body stores the energy as deuterium in tissue and fat ("mammals who turn blue light into fat"), and the pancreas, a midline isotope regulator, stops filtering. Oxygen must stay in its stable triplet state at all times for beta cells to rebuild, he says, and mouth breathing undermines this by cutting nasal nitric oxide. Rising insulin needs despite sun, or a sunburn, mean too little pinning.

Each insulin dose given without other routes forces more deuterium into cells, he says, which is why doctors now meet "insulin-resistant type 1s," whose cells are so packed with heavy water that insulin can no longer open them. Type 1 children are often hyperactive, he adds, because their bodies are trying to make the movement-driven vibration a godwit uses in flight.

#### The back door to glycogen

For those other routes Kruse points type 1 families to Dr. G. Moody's book *The Back Door to Glycogen* (the "Moody Method"), which he treats as a record of thinking from before insulin became the only answer. Moody held that movement, heat and cold can carry sugar into storage as glycogen without insulin's "hammer." Kruse reads the back door as chewing (his "mastication pump"), sunlight and cold, and he redefines type 1 as a lost flow of fluid through the brain's ventricles more than a missing hormone. Banting and Best's insulin won, in his telling, because a chemical path could be sold, in the same era that he ties the first modern wave of type 1 to the field shift after the Carrington Event.

Whether and when beta cells restart can't be predicted, he says, even though the physics they need (unbroken light, water and magnetism) is known; childhood is the best window, before the pancreas becomes locked by deuterium. In children he reads red eyes as a good sign, deuterium leaving through the tear and eyelid glands, and a deep tan as beta cells making insulin again.

He accepts that light and field alone may not regrow beta cells the immune system has destroyed. For those cases he points to stem-cell-derived beta cells and plasmapheresis (filtering antibodies out of the blood plasma), each protected by sun and pinning.

#### In practice

For type 2 and the dawn rise, Kruse's protocol is to ground, strip out nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields) and take in solar infrared; to go out barefoot facing the sunrise during the morning magnetic shift, ideally on an agonic line; to use incandescent rather than LED light and a short burst of red light while grounded to blunt a spike; and to avoid sugar eaten under screens and LEDs. To clear the tailpipe he names copper resonance rings over the neck and solar plexus, or deuterium-depleted water (DDW) with 3% saline. For type 1 his protocol adds incandescent or red bulbs, bare-eyed sunrise, a red and near-infrared panel over the pancreas and brainstem, DDW, seasonal high-fat food with nothing after dark, cold water, and at least 90 minutes a day of barefoot grounding on an agonic line; he also recommends Moody's book and its back-door routes of chewing, sun and cold. He judges progress by a well-formed (Bristol type 4) stool more than by glucose readings, and in children by a deep tan.

### Heart, cholesterol and arteries
- **id:** heart-and-cholesterol
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse calls cholesterol the membrane's insulator and light sensor, treats ApoB, Lp(a) and homocysteine as signs of repair and sludge, and traces heart and artery disease to lost melanin, lost field and missing sun.
- **protocols:** heart-protocols

Kruse rejects the idea that cholesterol causes heart disease. In his model cholesterol is the insulation and light sensor of cell membranes, and heart and artery disease come from broken-down melanin, a weakening magnetic field and too little sunlight. Forcing cholesterol down, to him, removes one of the body's defenders.

#### Cholesterol as insulator and light sensor

Kruse calls cholesterol the main stabilizer of the inner mitochondrial membrane: it sets how stiff and well insulated the membrane is, which lets it hold its strong electric field. Medicine pictures arteries as pipes clogged with grease; he pictures membranes as capacitors that store charge. When an ungrounded person under wireless signals and blue light loses the structure of their cell water, cells make more cholesterol to patch leaking membranes, so cholesterol arrives like a fireman, not an arsonist.

It is also, in his account, a light sensor. Sulfated cholesterol, carried mainly in HDL, is a "summer antenna" tuned to UV-B: when UV-B strikes it in the skin it makes vitamin D3 and releases charge. LDL is the sulfur-poor winter or indoor form that can't catch UV-B, so a high LDL signals light stress, with the liver making ever more LDL to catch energy it can't see.

He calls cholesterol a "second magnetic fat" alongside DHA (the omega-3 fat in seafood), the two working as a spin filter in membranes and nerves. He reads a Korean study of 12.8 million adults, and a review in *BMJ Open*, as showing that low cholesterol is the real danger, with the highest-cholesterol elderly living longest.

#### Statins, ApoB and Lp(a)

Statins, Kruse says, block the pathway that makes both cholesterol and CoQ10, an electron carrier in the energy chain. The membrane then loosens, its insulation leaks and its voltage collapses; he calls statins "quantum de-magnetizers" and links them to muscle pain and breakdown, memory loss and heart-rhythm problems. They also keep Complex IV, the energy chain's last enzyme, from reaching full efficiency, so a patient may dodge a heart attack now but, held in a low-energy fermenting state, later slide into amyloidosis (protein deposits in organs), ataxia (loss of coordination) or dementia.

Lp(a), a cholesterol particle cardiology now targets, he sees as an emergency patch the body sends to insulate a failing vessel wall. He cites a large trial in which a drug cut Lp(a) by 80–90% without reducing heart attacks or strokes, and sees no point in testing for it.

ApoB, the main protein on LDL particles, plays the same role. Stalled mitochondria give off flares of faint light that tell the nucleus to make more ApoB and Lp(a), which he calls "dielectric patch kits" for leaking water at the vessel wall; the sulfur bridges in Lp(a), he says, hold charge like a capacitor. Stripping these particles out with drugs is, to him, shooting the repair crew while the power plant burns.

#### How arteries fail

Heart attacks were barely recognized before about 1911, Kruse notes, and he ties the rise of heart disease to Earth's weakening field since the 1859 Carrington Event and to electrification. A healthy vessel lining, in his model, carries a strong negative charge that repels LDL, built from collagen, sulfated cholesterol and melanin (the dark pigment he treats as a light-to-electricity converter). A weakening field, wireless signals and blue LEDs strip that charge, the lining's nitric oxide output falls, and LDL slips under it.

Elastin, the springy protein that lets arteries recoil with each beat, works alongside melanin. Kruse notes that the same embryonic cells (the neural crest) give rise to both the nerves of the great vessels and the pigment cells that put melanin in them. Elastin acts as a spring tuned to the sun's infrared and melanin as a broad light-catcher, and together they carry the current that keeps the lining negative. Deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen, lodged in elastin turns the spring into a brittle stick, so each heartbeat cracks the wall a little; rising ApoB is the body grouting the cracks, and this is how he believes peripheral artery disease (PAD), the narrowing of arteries outside the heart, begins.

A high coronary calcium score despite good deuterium numbers, he says, means melanin loss, and the scan maps where melanin is missing from the arterial tree. Charged by light, melanin holds metals and calcium in solution, and light-activated vitamin K2 keeps calcium bound to its proteins. When nnEMF (non-native electromagnetic fields) and blue light make melanin drop its metals, calcium is left in the artery wall like a scab. A deuterium-loaded aorta, he adds, "freezes" into calcified scar to cut its energy costs, choosing what he calls "the symmetry of the stone," and the organs it feeds inherit that stiffness. So calcified arteries and valves need the field restored, not calcium-channel blockers or peptides.

High blood pressure is, to Kruse, the loss of a "second pump": blood moving through Earth's field is pushed along by a magnetic (Lorentz) force, and when that assist fades the heart has to push harder. Light at night, he adds, remodels the heart. In heart failure he points to a protocol pairing hypertonic (extra-salty) saline with the diuretic furosemide, credited with better urine output and kidney numbers and fewer deaths and readmissions; to him the salt works by raising the dielectric constant of blood water, its capacity to hold charge, toward the healthy value of 160, which matters more than removing fluid.

#### Homocysteine and the blood's vortex

Kruse says the four-chambered heart spins blood into a vortex that pushes deuterium out toward the vessel wall, helped by the lift Earth's field gives moving blood. When that field fails, homocysteine, an amino-acid by-product, pools as "isotopic sludge" and scrapes the lining like "endothelial sandpaper," setting off the light flares that summon ApoB and Lp(a).

He treats raised homocysteine as a gauge both of a place's danger to mammals and of heart-attack risk, a "Rosetta Stone" for the heart. To him it means that chewing and the field's lift are not keeping blood moving well enough.

#### The heart's own brain

The heart has a "little brain" of its own. Citing Armour's mapping of the roughly 40,000 neurons of the intrinsic cardiac nervous system, Kruse says these cells sense how thick and deuterium-laden the blood entering the right atrium is and set the force and rate of each beat to keep the blood vortex fast enough. He calls the heart's magnetic field 60 times stronger than the brain's, and points to transplanted hearts, which beat without any vagus nerve input, as proof of its independence.

Heart-rate variability (HRV), the beat-to-beat change in rhythm, is in his reading this mini-brain's direct readout, and it is the first thing to turn chaotic as the field weakens. Deuterium backing up from the gut can then load the heart's own nerves, and he ties sudden deaths of fit young people in Brazil, under the South Atlantic Anomaly (the large weak patch in Earth's field), to such a stall.

#### One root with the brain

Heart and brain disease share one root in his view: failure of the body's non-visual light sensing, the light sensors in skin and tissue outside the eye's vision. Strong sun on the chest lets skin melanin release adenosine, the same molecule emergency doctors use to reset a racing heart, which steadies rhythm. He says sunlight lowers Lp(a), ApoE and calcium scores. Atrial fibrillation set off through the vagus nerve he traces to a deuterium-loaded cardiac nerve plexus. Deuterium in the spinal fluid, he adds, is read by a sensor on the floor of the brainstem as a weaker signal, which turns the vagus down and primes the heart for atrial flutter.

#### In practice

His heart protocol: get full sun on the chest, and for a high calcium score combine UV light with grounding. Skip Lp(a) testing, don't push cholesterol below about 200 mg/dL, and take no statins during the field decline, even with familial hypercholesterolemia. He watches homocysteine as a better warning sign than cholesterol. For vagally triggered atrial fibrillation he suggests self-hypnosis to raise CO₂ and calm the cardiac plexus, or simple tricks such as rubbing the eyes, dunking the face in cold water and chewing mastic gum. For calcified arteries or valves, he says, restore the field and cut wireless and night LED light rather than adding drugs.

### The heart's vortex: bicuspid valve, Lasix and rescue drips
- **id:** aortic-vortex
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse treats the aortic valve as a vortex generator; he says a two-leaflet valve, salt-stripping diuretics or a heavy deuterium load break the vortex and starve the brain, while catecholamines, hypertonic saline and Lactated Ringer's can restart a stalled system.
- **protocols:** heart-protocols, pregnancy-birth-children, iv-fluids-acute-collapse

Kruse argues that the heart's job is not just to push blood but to spin it. The aortic valve, in his model, shapes each beat into a centred vortex that carries the pulse's energy up the aorta, sorts deuterium (heavy hydrogen) toward the vessel wall and drives the spinal fluid's flow through the brain. When that vortex breaks, whether from a malformed valve, a drug that strips the blood's salts or a deuterium-loaded body, he says the heart, brain, hair and thyroid fail together. He calls this map of linked failures a "failing stator" and expects to see more of it as Earth's field moves through an excursion.

#### A circle versus a slit

A normal aortic valve has three leaflets. Kruse says they open symmetrically into a round opening that preserves the left ventricle's own twist, so blood rises up the ascending aorta as an organised, centre-aligned laminar flow (smooth layers that do not mix). Because the stream stays centred, wall shear stress, the frictional drag of blood on the vessel lining, stays at a minimum and the pulse's kinetic energy is conserved. He adds that the aortic wall carries POMC and melanin in its elastin and fibrillin, and that the smooth vortex lets them sort deuterium.

Some people are born with a bicuspid aortic valve (BAV), which has only two leaflets. He calls it a "vortex destruction event." A two-leaflet valve opens as an ellipse or slit and fires blood off-centre. He cites 4D-flow MRI, a scan that tracks blood motion in three dimensions over time, showing an abnormally tight right-handed helical jet striking the outer curve of the ascending aorta, like a firehose aimed into a bend in a pipe.

The consequences follow in his account. The off-centre jet wastes the pulse's energy as heat instead of delivering it to the brain and organs. Its shear damages the lining and the POMC cells in the wall, which lose their thin, deuterium-poor boundary layer, so the wall "browns" (calcifies and degenerates) and stretches into the aneurysm typical of BAV. The heart, in his phrase, is fighting a gear mismatch from day one: it thickens to overcome its own internal friction until it collapses hydraulically, which he gives as the reason BAV so often ends in early heart failure.

#### Why a valve forms with two leaflets

Medicine calls BAV a congenital anomaly; Kruse calls it an energy problem in the germ line. Methylation, the tagging of DNA that times the steps of development, needs a steady supply of ATP and well-structured water. A female fetus forming her eggs keeps them in what he calls "satellite mode," with Complex I (the energy chain's entry point) switched down to keep deuterium out. If the mother drinks water at 155–200 parts per million (ppm) deuterium and lacks the sun and field to sort it, he says, deuterium floods the germ line.

The heavy atom then jams methylation. A carbon–deuterium bond is shorter and tighter than a carbon–hydrogen bond, so enzymes struggle to move methyl groups onto DNA, and the result is hypomethylation (too few tags). The precise timing needed to build a three-leaflet valve is lost, and the embryo falls back on an older two-leaflet design. He points to reptiles such as snakes, turtles and lizards, whose bodies do not need strong vortex power, and calls BAV a "Pre-Cambrian" throwback. Congenital anomalies in general are, to him, "epigenetic scars" of a deuterium-heavy environment.

He says the literature already points his way without knowing it. He cites links between variants in the folate pathway (such as MTHFR) and obstruction of the heart's outflow tract, including aortic stenosis. He also cites studies finding abnormal DNA methylation in BAV aortas, global hypomethylation alongside heavily tagged cardiac control genes, while the vessel is still normal in size, before blood-flow damage could explain it.

#### Brain, hair and thyroid

The brain feels a broken vortex first, Kruse says, because it is about 2% of body mass yet draws about a fifth of the heart's power. An off-centre pulse makes the spinal fluid's own vortex chaotic, the beating cilia and the brain's glymphatic (waste-clearing) flow lose their driving force, and deuterium silt builds up at the obex, the lower tip of the fourth ventricle. He links this to the early fatigue, dizziness and racing heart many people with BAV report later in life.

Heavy water that settles in the third ventricle, he says, causes a "dielectric flashover" in the hypothalamus, the brain's hormone hub, disrupting the stress-hormone axis (HPA), sleep and hormone production. Hair follicles are among the body's fastest-cycling tissues and very sensitive to methylation. With a deuterium-loaded germ line and ordinary 155 ppm water, their mitochondria hit what he calls the "4.3-second stutter," his figure for how often a deuterium atom strikes at that level, and the follicles miniaturize. That, he says, is why large meta-analyses tie baldness at the crown to coronary heart disease. More broadly he reads hair loss as the brain's matrices and central retinal pathways being "cooked" by disorder, a sign that foretells dementia.

The thyroid is drawn in last. Ordered to make more hormone so stalled mitochondria spin faster against heavy water, it burns itself out, which he gives as the reason Hashimoto's thyroiditis and alopecia so often appear together.

#### Salt, potassium and Lasix

Kruse treats the blood's salts as "dopants," the impurities engineers add to tune a semiconductor. Sodium keeps blood and spinal fluid able to hold charge. A loop diuretic such as furosemide (Lasix) strips that sodium, the dielectric constant (the water's capacity to hold charge) falls, and the vortex loses its grip. He compares it to the ocean's overturning circulation (AMOC): when seawater freshens, the sinking current that carries oxygen to the depths stalls. In the heart, the left ventricle's natural cycloid vortex weakens, oxygen delivery drops, and the heart's pacemaker nodes fire fast or erratically, producing atrial flutter in a structurally normal heart. Lasix also flushes potassium, which he says collapses the structured water inside cells.

That is how he reads the heart-failure protocol that pairs hypertonic saline with furosemide: giving the salt back keeps the charge high while the volume comes off. Lasix alone, in his phrase, is a volume fix for a dielectric problem, like lightening a struggling conveyor belt while cutting power to its motor.

#### Restarting a stalled vortex

Kruse says he saw sudden "acute lattice lock," his term for body water abruptly stiffening with deuterium, in intensive-care patients during COVID while working in a Missouri hospital. In organ-donor protocols, he notes, high-dose dopamine drips are used to keep organs perfused, and at that dose dopamine acts much like racemic epinephrine. He says he watched several apparently dead patients revive on Levophed (norepinephrine). In his reading dopamine, a highly conductive and paramagnetic catecholamine, restores conduction in the brain's striatum and the heart, offers a bypass around the drag of deuterium's kinetic isotope effect (the slowing of reactions by heavier atoms), restarts the heart's vortex and "thaws" a lock that had been diagnosed as brain death. Absent brainstem reflexes, he argues, are often a hydraulic "magnetic stall" rather than death.

He calls every epi-pen, dopamine drip and 3% saline bolus a "magnetic de-fragger," says other drugs can do the same only if they are deuterium-depleted first, and says he built his own protocol for this in Missouri. His complaint is that these tools are used at the harvest end of the timeline instead of as first-line rescue.

He gives Lactated Ringer's, a standard IV fluid, a similar role. Its lactate, he proposes, feeds the glycerol-3-phosphate (G3P) shuttle, which hands electrons straight to CoQ10 and Complex III and so bypasses the deuterium-jammed Complex I step he calls the "NADD+" stall; its pH shift, he suggests, also helps eject deuterium by changing how fast protons tunnel in the mitochondrial matrix. He says he used the G3P shunt, among other measures, to de-frag lattice-locked clients.

#### In practice

Before conception, Kruse wants the mother on 92.5 ppm deuterium-depleted water and a methylation-rich diet (choline, methionine and B vitamins from pasture-raised animal foods), not synthetic folate. Anyone born with BAV, he says, should drink 92.5 ppm water for life so the heart's motor is not wrecking the aortic wall under a heavy load. He never wants Lasix used alone and gives salt back with the diuretic. In hospital he asks for Lactated Ringer's rather than glucose or plain saline drips, and in a sudden collapse he wants clinicians to consider catecholamines and hypertonic saline early; intravenous saline is a clinician's tool.

- **sources:** [372947](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372947/), [372949](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372949/), [372951](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372951/), [372952](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372952/), [372982](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372982/), [373058](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373058/), [372547](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372547/), [372924](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/372924/), [373625](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373625/), [373174](https://forum.jackkruse.com/posts/373174/)

### Neurodegeneration and MS
- **id:** neurodegeneration
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse traces Alzheimer's, Parkinson's, ALS, MS and even depression to lost light, field and vagal tone, with deuterium backing up from the gut, misplaced copper and melanin breakdown doing the damage.
- **protocols:** ms-agonic-therapy, brain-eye-ear-conditions

Kruse traces Alzheimer's, Parkinson's, ALS and multiple sclerosis to lost sunlight, a weak or unstable magnetic field and a failing vagus nerve. In each, he says, deuterium (heavy hydrogen), misplaced copper and broken-down melanin do the damage. The misfolded proteins medicine targets are, to him, the body's defenses rather than the cause.

#### Alzheimer's starts outside the brain

Citing an 86,000-patient study, Kruse argues Alzheimer's is a body-wide disease. In his model the vagus nerve is a cable whose steady pacing keeps the linings of the gut, lungs and blood vessels charged so they can keep deuterium out. Being unpinned (not grounded under a stable field), nnEMF (wireless and wiring fields) and blue light drain that tone.

In the lungs, he says, the structured water then collapses and incoming oxygen flips into singlet oxygen, a reactive form. In the gut, singlet oxygen damages fats and the lining can no longer sort out heavy hydrogen, so deuterium leaks into the blood. The blood carries both up to the thalamus and hippocampus, where deuterium jams the brain's navigation cells and memory and sense of place fail. He also describes a back route: when the gut's morning bicarbonate flush stalls, heavy water climbs the vagus to the floor of the brainstem.

#### The brain's isotope centrifuge

Kruse asks why the brain, about 2% of body weight, takes 15–25% of the heart's output. His answer is that blood flow powers a vortex of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF, the clear fluid bathing brain and spinal cord) between the third and fourth ventricles, the brain's central fluid chambers. Driven by each heartbeat and steered by tiny hairs lining the ventricles, the vortex spins light hydrogen away from deuterium and vents the heavy isotope through the vagus.

Under the South Atlantic Anomaly, a large weak patch in Earth's field, he says life pays a constant "Landauer tax": with little outside field to lift the vortex, each thought costs more heat. He calls dementia the biological version of the planet losing its magnetic memory, and expects people carrying heavy deuterium to fare worst as the old field fades.

#### Plaques as "deuterium trash cans"

Citing Stephanie Seneff and Anthony Kyriakopoulos, Kruse says that once the vagal drain and melanin fail, cells deliberately fold amyloid and alpha-synuclein into rigid fibrils, using cardiolipin (an inner-membrane fat) and copper to do it. These fibrils are vaults that lock deuterium away from the energy chain, which is why he calls plaques "deuterium trash cans." He also blames misplaced copper: the energy chain's last enzyme should run on heavy copper-65, and the wrong isotope there, he says, always goes with neurodegeneration.

#### Thick fluid: depression and SSRIs

Kruse proposes renaming major depression "Isotopic Viscosity Syndrome": a brain whose fluid has thickened as the field declines, which he says is why depression is surging. He cites a 2025 *JAMA Psychiatry* study by Kochunov and colleagues finding that depression is marked by local drops in brain blood flow that pull neighboring neurons out of sync, a functional change that tracks how severe the symptoms are.

SSRIs, the most common antidepressants, can make this worse in his view. Sertraline carries two chlorine atoms that grip electrons and make nearby water denser and more viscous (thicker and slower-flowing), so the spinal fluid turns to "jello." On scans this shows as enlarged ventricles and a shrinking brain; the hairs lining the ventricles can't beat through the sludge, and heavy water presses on the pituitary and brainstem. The brain, using a fifth of the body's power, drowns first.

#### Parkinson's

In Kruse's model the substantia nigra's neuromelanin, a dark pigment bound to iron, is a magnetic battery that keeps the spins of incoming oxygen aligned. nnEMF, artificial light or dehydration warp it, oxygen goes singlet, and Lewy bodies (clumps of misfolded alpha-synuclein) build up as corrupted proteins the cell can't clear. Skin cells facing the same voltage drop can escape into cancer; dopamine neurons can't, so they die.

Parkinson's strikes where the field is weakest, he says, so patients who learn to pin themselves magnetically will win. Cutting the vagus nerve lowers risk, in his reading, because it blocks deuterium flowing from gut to brain. From his years at Charity Hospital, moving from general surgery to neurosurgery, he recalls that patients who had a truncal vagotomy (cutting the main vagus trunks, once common for ulcers) developed less Parkinson's. With the stomach and pancreas cut off, deuterium could no longer back up into the brain, an accidental "isotopic firewall." This, he says, gives the Braak hypothesis, the idea that Parkinson's begins in the gut, its missing mechanism: the gut is the body's main deuterium sink.

The entry point, he says, is the area postrema, a small patch on the floor of the fourth ventricle with no blood–brain barrier. Deuterium backing up from the gut gets in there, gathers in the lateral ventricles and reaches the basal ganglia and substantia nigra beside them, where it jams ATP synthase and uncouples the neuromelanin shield; medicine sees the result as Lewy bodies and tremor.

Every Parkinson's patient is heavy with deuterium, Kruse says, which makes light-driven repair very hard, and L-DOPA, the standard drug, only treats symptoms. He reads Sardinia's historically low Parkinson's rate as low deuterium rather than the L-DOPA in its fava beans: a traditional diet rich in folate and fiber, strong UV-A light and local field conditions. He reports a man with severe Parkinson's who had not walked or talked in three years talking and walking 50 yards on the beach after four weeks at Costa del Sol in El Salvador.

#### Why the adult brain degenerates

Kruse links neurodegeneration to infancy. Babies are unusually fat because the brain uses most of their energy, and body fat feeds it until the solar clock matures. An adult brain stripped of its light shielding runs that program in reverse, its cells turning senescent and storing fat droplets, but the skull has no fat reserve, so the brain degenerates instead. Complex brains, he adds, appeared only after Earth's field strengthened, and degeneration is life's retreat when it weakens again.

#### ALS and MS

ALS he calls a waking form of dream-sleep paralysis. With the body clock destroyed, the brainstem can't tell day fields from night fields and sends the paralysis commands of REM sleep while the person is awake. He says it is rare in tanned tropical people and worsened by long flights across time zones, especially over the weakening South Atlantic field.

He also sees ALS as the Parkinson's backflow aimed downward. Instead of rising into the brain, deuterium drains through the obex, the narrow point where the fourth ventricle opens into the spinal canal, and settles around the nerve roots, "lattice-locking" the spinal fluid until the motor neurons leaving there burn out. Motor neurons go first because they have the highest energy demand and the most complex mitochondrial networks.

He reads the genetic finding behind a 2026 Breakthrough Prize, a repeated stretch of DNA in the C9ORF72 gene shared by ALS and frontotemporal dementia, as a weak point rather than the cause. The repeats fold into knots that are sensitive to hydrogen's mass; when the chemical tags around them carry deuterium, the knots stiffen and the gene can't be read properly, so its proteins misfold. ALS is 1.2–1.5 times more common in men and starts later in women, which he credits to periodic pelvic flushing in women that keeps the bottom of the spinal fluid "highway" clear; the advantage fades after menopause, when the pelvic water stagnates.

MS, in his account, is spin failure at the nodes of Ranvier, the gaps in a nerve's insulation where signals jump. Its rise with latitude reflects collapsing water structure and melanin, not low vitamin D, and the misfolded proteins in MS are readouts of a poor local field. Hence his "agonic line therapy": living where compass north and true north coincide, which he says matters more than deuterium-depleted water.

#### In practice

For MS his protocol puts relocation first: move to a low-latitude spot with a flat, stable field on a good agonic line, then use grounded Mu-metal shielding (a magnetic-shield alloy), block non-native frequencies and use targeted coherent light. Off a good line he adds deuterium-depleted water, a seafood diet rich in DHA and iodine, and red and infrared light; he favors relocation over sleeping pills and says thin MS patients shouldn't dry fast. For Parkinson's he says to get magnetically pinned by living where the field is strong, points to sunlit beach relocation like the El Salvador case, and rates the Neuropod the best vagus nerve stimulator for neurodegenerative disease.

### Immunity, inflammation and the body's barriers
- **id:** immunity-barriers
- **pillar:** support
- **app:** hold
- **summary:** Kruse describes the body's barriers as isotope filters that fail together, inflammation as a containment breach carried by positive charge, and infections and parasites as signs of a deuterated terrain.
- **protocols:** mold-inflammation

Kruse sees the body's barriers (blood–brain, gut, retina, spinal cord) as filters built to keep deuterium, a heavy form of hydrogen, away from the mitochondria, and says they fail together. Inflammation, in his model, starts when mitochondria lose their electrical containment, and infections reveal the state of the body's terrain more than the strength of the germ.

#### Barriers as isotope filters

The blood–brain barrier's main job, Kruse says, is to keep deuterium out of brain mitochondria, whose strong membrane field can't tolerate its drag. The barrier's lining cells sort light hydrogen from heavy using one-handed (chiral) molecules, tuned by morning UV-A light. Wireless signals and blue LEDs make calcium channels in those cells leak, the tight seals between cells loosen, and deuterium floods the spinal fluid.

The brainstem senses the breach and releases GDF15, a stress signal from struggling mitochondria; he calls the surge a "mitokine storm." It shows up first as fatigue, poor focus and psychiatric distress and can eventually push brain tissue into fermentation and brain cancer.

Because mitochondria link into one network across the body, he says, barriers never fail alone. The retinal barrier (macular degeneration), the gut barrier (fatty liver) and the spinal barrier (MS) go down together, and treating them as separate organ diseases misses the shared cause.

#### Inflammation as a containment breach

Mitochondria descend from bacteria and still carry bacterial markers: circular DNA, cardiolipin (an inner-membrane fat) and bacterial-style proteins. Inside the mitochondrion these are harmless; once they leak, the immune system's sensors read them as infection. What decides whether inflammation fires, in Kruse's account, is whether the inner membrane keeps these contents electrically and magnetically contained. Autoimmunity is then a clean-up of a damaged grid rather than a mistake, with antibodies tagging broken mitochondrial parts.

Inflammation itself, he says, is a build-up of positive charge. That charge was once carried mostly by spare protons (ordinary hydrogen nuclei) but, as the field weakens, increasingly by deuterons, the heavier nuclei of deuterium. That is why, to him, minor problems now progress faster than textbooks predict and worsen with each generation.

Severe allergic reactions fit the same picture. When a deuterium-loaded person exercises, the blood grows thicker and hotter; if the body's electrical brake fails, inflammation surges, and anaphylaxis is the body blowing a fuse, a desperate vagal reset that stops the engine before a total burnout.

#### Viruses as scouts

He frames viral illness the same way. Letting a virus replicate is a cell's emergency shutdown when its field fails, and a cytokine storm (a runaway immune flare) is containment failure, with the cell dying because its containment dissolved rather than because the virus killed it. The spike protein, which he describes as strongly positively charged, short-circuits membrane charge along the same final path as chronic disease. He is blunter about the mRNA shots: once the injection has "integrated," he says, no one recovers. The damage waits until a person stops doing the right things, then catches up with the inner mitochondrial membrane, and the spike protein problem never goes away.

Fast-replicating viruses, Kruse argues, are the biosphere's scouts, adapting to environmental shifts before complex life does. He reads the unusual person-to-person spread of the Andes strain of hantavirus as a way of skipping a degraded outer environment by jumping straight between sheltered human bodies. In the noisy water of a weak-field region, ubiquitin, the cell's tag marking proteins for destruction, starts tagging the host's own respiratory proteins, leaving an energy gap the virus exploits in the vessel lining and lungs.

The large cortisol surge of acute hantavirus is, to him, the brain sensing the energy chain stalling and dumping more glucose, and with it more deuterium, to force current through. The fluid that floods the lungs in fatal cases is the vagal exhaust collapsing and the lung's air–water interface shorting out.

#### Tolerance needs light and timing

Immune tolerance, Kruse says, depends on mitochondria signaling "self." Citing Zhang and colleagues in *Nature* (2025), he says steady EPO signaling to dendritic cells (the immune system's scouts) switches on calming regulatory T cells; magnetically stable mitochondria keep that signal steady, while wireless and blue light scramble it so the immune system reads chaos as invasion. Eating indoors after dark puts the gut's clock out of step with the brain's, which he says tips the thymus toward inflammatory T cells. Vitamin A, in his account, is a pigment whose light- and field-driven shape changes program immunity in the gut, airways and urinary tract.

In one family he read positive antibodies against voltage-gated calcium channels as proof that their "melanin–water battery" had run down. Calcium then floods cells and either "petrifies" them, as in Lambert–Eaton myasthenic syndrome (a muscle-weakness disorder), or "shreds" them, as in PANS/PANDAS; he calls Lambert–Eaton the "astronaut on Earth" syndrome. He likewise ties a Toronto child's primary immunodeficiency to too little magnetic flux in that city.

#### Infections read the terrain

To Kruse more deuterium means more bacterial overgrowth, and candida means a deuterated gut. In PANS/PANDAS, the sudden childhood tics and OCD linked to strep, he sees the strep as a clean-up crew rather than the cause. Mold illness and Lyme lower alpha-MSH, a skin and immune hormone made alongside melanin, and he says mold troubles only people who can't make melanin.

Chronic infections such as Epstein–Barr virus, Lyme and filarial worms, he says, thrive in heavy, low-oxygen water once the body reverts to a primitive "Archean" state; they mislead doctors but are scavengers of deuterium decay, not its cause. In a child stuck in a "magnetic stall," even pinworms may be tolerated as crude backup immune regulators, though their presence still signals a child too heavy to clear its own waste.

The same logic applies to Protomyxzoa rheumatica, a blood parasite some practitioners target directly. Kruse calls it a marker of a collapsed water table: its sugary biofilms (protective slime layers) trap deuterium, thicken the blood and lower its dielectric constant, the water's capacity to hold charge, and deuterated dietary fats harden them. In one member he blamed an indoor, tech-heavy life in the Arizona desert, whose groundwater carries fluoride, which he calls the most damaging atom for water's dielectric.

He says thousands have reversed mold illness on clean, low-EMF land with equatorial sun. Infrared sunlight expands the body's structured water and squeezes toxins out of membranes, and barefoot grounding speeds blood and spinal-fluid flow to flush them. Binders, charcoal and glutathione fail, he says, when cell voltage has collapsed, because detox needs energy.

#### In practice

Kruse's mold and inflammation protocol: move to a clean, low-nnEMF property (low in wireless and wiring fields) with clean indoor and outdoor air; maximize equatorial sun; and ground barefoot at an equatorial shoreline. For inflammation and viral illness he says to ground, get sunlight, re-pin to a good field and clear deuterium, and to eat by daylight. He advises against relying on binders, charcoal, glutathione or antifungals while cell voltage is collapsed. For parasites and biofilms he treats the medium rather than the bug: UV-A to build melanin and 3% saline to clear deuterium, until the water's dielectric climbs back toward a healthy 160 and the immune system can clear the biofilm itself.
