## Glossary

| Term | Plain meaning (in his usage) |
|---|---|
| 100th meridian | John Wesley Powell's humid-East/arid-West line, which he treats as a moving boundary of good water. |
| 110 Hz (chamber resonance) | The pitch at which Neolithic stone chambers ring; he says it amplified chanting and calms the brain's language and planning areas. |
| 128 Hz fork | A tuning fork used on stiff joints, nerves and, in the evening, the sternum or neck as a calming vagal brake. |
| 14 ångströms (tunneling limit) | The farthest an electron can tunnel between respiratory proteins (1.4 billionths of a meter), a limit he credits to Dutton. |
| 160 vs 78 | His numbers for the dielectric constant of structured, charge-holding cell water (160) versus ordinary bulk water (78). |
| 2 Hz rhythm (carrier) | A slow, universal rhythm he says keeps tissues and stem cells in sync; falling water and chanting "OM" match it. |
| 30 million volts per meter (30 MV/m) | The electric field he says a healthy inner mitochondrial membrane holds, likened to a lightning bolt. |
| 380 nm light | Violet/UV-A dawn light that neuropsin reads to set the body clock; he calls it the "mTOR switch.". |
| 4.3-second stutter | His figure for how often a deuterium atom strikes the system on ordinary 155 ppm water. |
| 40 Hz fork | A tuning fork at the brain's gamma frequency, used mornings, on the spine and on the hard palate. |
| 43% red light engine | Red and infrared (about 43% of sunlight) spinning the liver's ATP synthase on light hydrogen, so it needs less food fuel. |
| 4D-flow MRI | A scan that tracks blood motion in three dimensions over time; he cites it showing a bicuspid valve's tight helical jet. |
| 71-terahertz vibration | A mitochondrial vibration (Song et al., 2026) he reads as a timing signal pacing the nucleus and telomeres while the membrane holds charge. |
| Acetylcholine | The vagus nerve's chemical messenger; raised by baking soda in his account. |
| ACTH | The pituitary hormone cut from POMC that drives the adrenal glands; he says sunrise light helps make it. |
| Acute lattice lock | A sudden lock in an already loaded person when demand spikes, as in exercise or infection. |
| Adaptive healing | Robert Becker's idea, as he uses it, that tissue must rebuild for a new environment, so people may feel worse before better after moving. |
| Adhesive arachnoiditis (adult matrix freeze) | Scarring of the membrane around the spinal nerves; to him the acquired, adult form of spinal lattice lock. |
| Adipose capacitor (fat capacitor) | Body fat acting as an electrical buffer and energy store that also holds deuterium away from vital organs. |
| Agonic line | A line where declination is zero, so compass north equals true north; he treats living near one as protective. |
| Akathisia | Drug-induced inner restlessness, which he links to a blocked vagal drain. |
| Akkermansia | A mucus-protecting gut bacterium that declines when the body clock breaks. |
| AMOC | The Atlantic's great overturning current; he says its slowdown leaves deuterium in East Coast surface water. |
| AMOC / SMOC | The Atlantic and Southern ocean overturning currents, which he says generate their own magnetic fields tied to the dynamo. |
| Amyloidosis | Abnormal protein deposits in organs; one of the later costs he attributes to statins. |
| Anaphylaxis | A severe allergic reaction, which he frames as the body blowing a fuse. |
| Angular momentum | The quantity of spin or rotation, which cannot simply appear or vanish; one of the two pillars of his thesis. |
| ApoB | The main protein on LDL particles; to him a repair patch, not a villain. |
| Arachidonic acid | An omega-6 fat that makes inflammatory signals, which he says keep running when DHA signals are jammed. |
| Archean state | A primitive, low-oxygen, heavy-water body state in which chronic infections thrive as scavengers. |
| Area postrema | A patch on the floor of the fourth ventricle with no blood–brain barrier, where he says gut deuterium enters the brain. |
| Astronauts on Earth (foreign astronaut) | A person living indoors, ungrounded, high up or flying often, cut off from sun, ground and field as if in orbit. |
| Atavism | A throwback to an older, more primitive way of living; how he frames cancer's switch to fermentation. |
| Atavistic retreat | Tissues reverting to older, simpler, looser designs after the chromosome 2 fuse blows. |
| Atavistic shunt | A fat-starved body falling back on ancient emergency metabolism and consuming itself. |
| Ataxia | Loss of coordination; another later cost he attributes to statins. |
| Atomic impurity / rogue dopant | A foreign compound (drug, supplement or toxin) acting like a stray atom in a chip that traps electrons. |
| ATP / ATP synthase | The cell's energy currency / the rotary motor in mitochondria that makes it, spinning near 9,000 RPM in his figure. |
| Atrial flutter | A fast, regular rhythm in the heart's upper chambers that he ties to a deuterium-dulled vagus. |
| Auricular VNS | Vagus nerve stimulation through the ear; he calls Maya jade its first form. |
| Autophagy / apoptosis | The cell's self-recycling / its orderly, programmed death. |
| Back door to glycogen (Moody Method) | Dr. G. Moody's pre-insulin idea that movement, heat and cold can move sugar into storage without insulin; Kruse reads it as chewing, sun and cold. |
| Ballast (deuterium) | The small amount of heavy hydrogen he says a vortex needs to hold its spin, which is why he does not aim for zero. |
| Bandgap | The energy step that lets a protein act as a semiconductor; he says it narrows when structured water collapses. |
| Basal ganglia | Deep brain structures near the lateral ventricles that he says collect backwashed deuterium in Parkinson's. |
| Basalt | Dark, magnetite-rich volcanic rock that he says holds a flat field, insulates against electrical noise and is ideal to ground on. |
| Base chain / layer two | His Bitcoin analogy: DNA is the base chain; daily clock-timed chemical tagging is a layer two that adjusts genes without rewriting them. |
| BDNF | The brain's growth and repair signal, which he says needs light and a burst of reactive oxygen to switch on. |
| Beeswax candle test | Burning a rated pure-beeswax candle at home; burning faster or slower than rated is his readout of local field quality. |
| Bh / Bz | The horizontal part of the local field (Bh, parallel to the ground) / the vertical part (Bz, diving into it). |
| Bicarb hack | Oral baking soda used to copy and restart the bicarbonate flush and draw deuterium out. |
| Bicarbonate blanket | Volcanic CO₂ dissolving into seawater during a field decline, changing the water's electrical properties. |
| Bicarbonate tailpipe (bicarbonate flush) | The vagus-driven release of about two liters of pancreatic bicarbonate a day, which he says carries deuterium out through the gut. |
| Bicuspid aortic valve (BAV) | An aortic valve born with two leaflets instead of three; he calls it a "vortex destruction event" that fires blood off-centre. |
| Biofilm | A protective slime layer microbes build; he says it traps deuterium. |
| Biological Hall voltage | The daytime voltage he says forms as electrons move across Earth's field lines, helping hold the membrane field. |
| Biological LENR | Low-energy nuclear reactions he proposes at crista tips, such as copper-63 becoming copper-65, clearing isotopic waste at body temperature. |
| Biological Nazaré | Crista tips focusing a proton wave the way Portugal's Nazaré canyon focuses ocean swells, driving ATP synthase. |
| Biological reflexivity | George Soros's idea, borrowed: every drug or tracked biomarker changes the biology it targets and can undermine itself. |
| Biological SCRAM | A cell's emergency shutdown, a term borrowed from nuclear reactors, when field loss breaches the membrane. |
| Biologically closed electric circuits | Björn Nordenström's idea of a second, electrical circulation running through blood vessels and the fluid between cells. |
| Biophysical degausser | His name for high-dose insulin, which he says strips cell membranes of their charge. |
| Bioprecipitation | Rain seeded by living things, such as fungal ice-nucleating proteins, which he calls the biosphere's backup rainmaker. |
| Blood–brain barrier | The lining that controls what passes from blood into the brain; to him an isotope filter. |
| BMI paradox | Heavier people surviving acute stress that kills the very lean, because their fat buffers the surge. |
| Bohr effect | CO₂ making blood slightly more acidic so hemoglobin releases oxygen to tissues. |
| Bone conduction | Sound traveling through the skull bones rather than through the air. |
| Braak hypothesis | The idea that Parkinson's begins in the gut and travels to the brain; he supplies a deuterium mechanism. |
| Brachistochrone | The curve along which a falling object arrives fastest; his description of the crista junction's shape. |
| Bristol type 4 | A smooth, well-formed stool; his sign that the morning bicarbonate flush is working. |
| Brunhes–Matuyama reversal | The last full swap of Earth's magnetic poles, about 780,000 years ago; older rock set before it can oppose today's field. |
| Bus bar | His electrician's term for the junctions linking mitochondria into one shared power network, so failure spreads body-wide. |
| Buteyko breathing | Deliberately breathing less to raise CO₂ in the blood. |
| C3 / C4 plants | Plants that favor carbon-12 (wheat, rice, temperate vegetables) / plants that load carbon-13 and deuterium (maize, cane). |
| C9ORF72 repeat | A repeated DNA stretch shared by ALS and frontotemporal dementia; to him a mass-sensitive weak point, not the cause. |
| Cap cloud ("capilla") | A cloud that sits on a volcano's summit; to him, water held in place by the rock's field and a sign of a healthy spot. |
| Capacitor | Something that stores and releases electrical charge; his model for membranes, structured water and body fat. |
| Carbonic anhydrase | An enzyme that makes bicarbonate, which he says carries deuterium out of the pancreas. |
| Cardiac operator / input operator | His roles for the right vagus (steadying the heart) and the left vagus (monitoring gut and liver deuterium load). |
| Cardiolipin | A cone-shaped fat found almost only in the inner mitochondrial membrane, which he calls its insulating sleeve. |
| Carrington Event | The huge solar storm of 1859; his benchmark extreme storm and the start of the current field-decline era. |
| Catecholamines | Dopamine, noradrenaline and adrenaline; he says melanin is broken back down into them when it can't be maintained. |
| CD₃ (deuterated methyl) | A methyl tag in which all three hydrogens are deuterium, the heaviest form he says the clock must swap out at night. |
| Cell danger response | Robert Naviaux's term for a cell's defensive shutdown mode. |
| Cell voltage (−70 vs −15 to −30 mV) | The charge across a cell membrane: about −70 millivolts when healthy, falling to −15 to −30 millivolts as cells fail or ferment. |
| Centrifugal fractionation | Body movement spinning heavy water away from light water in the brain and spinal fluid. |
| Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) | The clear fluid bathing the brain and spinal cord. |
| cGAS-STING | A cell alarm triggered by stray DNA that drives inflammation. |
| Chandler wobble | A small natural wobble of Earth's spin axis whose near-disappearance he reads as the planet's "transmission in neutral". |
| Chappuis band | Ozone's broad absorption of yellow-orange light, which he says casts a "shadow" around 589 nm. |
| Chimera / mitochondrial monolith | A person carrying two genetically distinct cell lines / his term for such a person whose cells still share one mitochondrial lineage. |
| Chiral / chirality | Having a "handedness," like left and right hands; chiral molecules, he says, filter electron spin. |
| Chiral handshake | His name for the merger of an ancient iron–sulfur motor with a fluid cell body that formed complex cells. |
| Choroid plexus | The brain tissue that makes spinal fluid; he says humans use it, with the retina's pigment layer, as birds use the pecten. |
| Chromosome 2 fuse | The leftover telomere loop where two ape chromosomes fused in humans, which he says "blows" under extreme stress or field loss. |
| Ciliary ganglion | Small nerve relay behind the eye that he says links light on the iris to the vagus. |
| Circuit breaker (insulin resistance) | His view of insulin resistance as a cell refusing fuel it can't safely burn. |
| CISS (chiral-induced spin selectivity) | One-handed molecules letting through electrons of only one spin direction; his "spin filter" for electron flow. |
| Clock dampener | His term for FOXO3 pausing cell division to slow the cell clock and allow repair. |
| CME (coronal mass ejection) | A burst of charged material thrown off by the Sun that can trigger a geomagnetic storm. |
| CO₂–bicarbonate loop | Carbonated water and retained carbon dioxide building bicarbonate, which he says draws deuterium out. |
| Cognitively lattice locked | Brain water too stiff and deuterium-loaded for clear thinking, which he says drops cognition to a survival mode. |
| Coherence time | The quiet interval between deuterium strikes; he says buying low-deuterium water buys coherence time. |
| Coherent / coherence | Electron spins moving in step, which he says lets enzymes signal instead of burning energy as heat. |
| Cold thermogenesis | Deliberate cold exposure on bare skin or by immersion, his main override when sun or field is poor. |
| Complex I | The entry point of the mitochondrial energy chain; he calls it a UV-A-driven node. |
| Complex II | A second entry point to the energy chain that takes electrons from fat burning and needs very precise spacing. |
| Complex III / Q-cycle | The chain's middle step; he calls its recycling loop a "deli slicer" shaving light frequencies off food electrons. |
| Complex IV (cytochrome c oxidase) | The last enzyme of the energy chain, where electrons meet oxygen to make deuterium-depleted water; he says it should run on copper-65. |
| Containment breach | His view of inflammation: mitochondria leaking contents the immune system reads as infection. |
| Copper ring / copper coils | Open copper rings (after Lakhovsky) and coils (after Spurling) used as passive antennas and shields under the pillow or around the head or neck. |
| Copper-65 | The heavier natural form of copper, which he says belongs in Complex IV and bone. |
| CoQ10 | An electron carrier in the energy chain whose production statins also block. |
| Corrupt bit | A misfolded protein whose information has turned to noise. |
| Cortisol awakening response | The natural rise in cortisol after waking, which he says morning infrared triggers to start the bicarbonate flush. |
| Cosmic shields | His three protective layers: the interstellar hydrogen filter, the Sun's heliosphere and Earth's geodynamo, which he says are failing together. |
| Counterfeit timestamp | His phrase for phone blue light telling the body clock it is always noon. |
| Cradle cap (infant seborrheic dermatitis) | A scaly infant scalp rash he reads, like jaundice, as an early sign of mitochondrial trouble. |
| Cristae | The inward folds of the inner mitochondrial membrane, which he treats as transistors. |
| CRPS | Complex regional pain syndrome: severe, lasting burning pain after an injury. |
| CTE | Chronic traumatic encephalopathy, a degenerative brain disease he links to years of indoor, high-CO₂ team training. |
| Current of injury | Robert Becker's term for the small natural electric current that steers tissue repair. |
| Cyanide-like lock | A whole-chain electron stall caused physically by field loss, not by poison. |
| Cytokine storm | A runaway immune flare, which he sees as a containment failure. |
| Dawn phenomenon (magnetic dawning phenomenon) | The early-morning rise in blood sugar, which he reads as failure to sync with the dawn magnetic shift. |
| De-frag | Shaking deuterium loose from tissue and restoring order, for example with sound, hypnosis or a fork and water jet. |
| Declination | The angle between compass north and true north; he wants it near 0° and calls it a "geographic multiplier.". |
| Dehydroascorbate | Spent vitamin C, which glutathione recycles back into its working form. |
| Deuterium (deuteron) | Heavy hydrogen, with an extra neutron and about twice the mass; in his model it jams mitochondrial motors. A deuteron is its nucleus. |
| Deuterium bomb | His name for glucose, because plants pack deuterium into storage sugars. |
| Deuterium Ghost | A normal pulse-oximeter reading while tissues are actually starved of oxygen. |
| Deuterium trash can | Protein plaques he says the brain builds on purpose to wall deuterium away from the energy chain. |
| Deuterium-depleted water (DDW) | Water low in heavy hydrogen; healthy mitochondria make it, and he recommends drinking it at roughly 25–105 ppm. |
| Deuterons | The nuclei of deuterium; he says they increasingly carry the positive charge of inflammation. |
| DHA (magnetic fat) | The long omega-3 fat in seafood, retina and brain, which he calls a light-conducting "magnetic fat.". |
| Diamagnetic | Weakly pushed away by a magnet; his description of singlet oxygen. |
| Diamond / coal | His image for ordered 160 water versus disordered 78 water, drawn from entropy-based gravity papers. |
| Dielectric booster | Rising CO₂ and global greening raising Earth's charge-holding capacity as its magnetic shield weakens. |
| Dielectric constant | How much electric charge a substance can hold and how strongly it buffers electric fields. |
| Dielectric constant (160 vs 78) | How well water stores charge; he scores structured cell water at about 160 and ordinary or disordered water at 78. |
| Dielectric patch kits | His description of ApoB and Lp(a) sealing leaking water at the vessel wall. |
| Dirty burn | Burning glucose in a low-field, high-nnEMF setting, which throws off light flares and leaves deuterium behind. |
| Disembarkment syndrome | A lasting sense of rocking after a voyage; one route he names to acquired ataxia. |
| Distal refinery | His name for the skin's layered network of small arteries, which sheds heat and deuterium through cold and sweat. |
| Dopant | An added material that changes how a semiconductor behaves; he applies it to jade in teeth. |
| Doping / dopant | Deliberate impurities that tune a semiconductor; he applies it to the cell's chemical tags and to foreign chemicals that trap electrons. |
| Dry biophysics | His label for consciousness theories that ignore the water around microtubules. |
| Dual-axis transistor | His description of neuropsin reading both sunlight and Earth's magnetic field. |
| Dumping syndrome | Rapid stomach emptying after vagus surgery; one of the signs he reads as gut organs filling with deuterium. |
| Dynamo-Isotopic Calculator | The tool he says should replace the UV index by weighing sun, field and local deuterium together. |
| ECDO | A theory that Earth's outer shell can suddenly slip relative to its core, which he follows as a staged cycle already begun. |
| Elastin | The springy protein that lets arteries recoil; deuterium turns it brittle in his model. |
| Electric and magnetic containment | The two conditions he says mitochondria need to make melatonin; grounding restores only the electric one. |
| Electrochemical therapy | Treating tumors by inserting electrodes and passing a weak direct current, as Nordenström and later Chinese doctors did. |
| Electroculture | Using buried wires, magnets, antennas and stone to shape the soil's electrical and magnetic environment to boost plant growth. |
| Electromagnetic terroir | Gene activity following the local magnetic field and ground currents, as wine follows its soil. |
| Elovanoids / neuroprotectin D1 | Healing signals made from DHA that he says deuterium mistunes. |
| Empty sella | A flattened pituitary gland in its bony seat, which he links to creatine abuse. |
| Endothelial sandpaper | His image of homocysteine scraping the vessel lining in thick, turbulent blood. |
| Energy resistance (Picard) | Martin Picard's idea of how much the cell resists energy flow; health sits in a "just-right" zone. |
| Entropy | Accumulated disorder, which he says telomeres record instead of age. |
| Environmental acidosis | His term for protons flooding cells when structured water collapses, so oxygen is released but cannot be used. |
| Ephaptic coupling | Neighboring cells communicating through fields rather than synapses; he says wireless signals jam it. |
| Epigenetic drift | Slow change in the chemical marks on DNA, which he treats as a passenger of field damage. |
| Error rate (declination) | Declination read as the mismatch between Earth's spin and its magnetic dynamo; the wider it is, the worse body rhythms and spins mesh. |
| Exclusion-zone (EZ) water / structured water | Gerald Pollack's ordered, charge-holding water layer that forms against surfaces; his "160" state. |
| Excursion / reversal (geomagnetic) | An aborted pole reversal where the field weakens and wanders for up to ~2,000 years / a full pole swap; he says excursions select survivors, reversals cause extinctions. |
| Fab plant | His image of the mother filtering deuterium and processing nutrients like vitamin K for the baby. |
| FADH₂ | An electron carrier that enters the energy chain after Complex I; fat burning routes more electrons through it. |
| Faraday cage | An enclosure that blocks fields; he says buildings act as one, cutting people off from sun and field. |
| Fatty Liver Sludge Shunt | Deuterium pouring into the portal vein after a late meal eaten under LEDs. |
| Fenton reaction | Iron-driven chemistry that makes destructive radicals. |
| Ferritin | The iron-storage protein; he reads high blood ferritin as a sign of a weak local field and poor light and grounding. |
| Ferron | An iron core in ferritin or cytochromes acting as a tiny magnetic compass needle and capacitor. |
| Ferron (polarization wave) | A ripple of electric order moving through a ferroelectric material; he says UV-A on melanin launches one through potassium-held cell water (distinct from his use of "ferron" for iron cores). |
| FICZ / AhR | A compound UV-A makes from tryptophan in skin / the aryl hydrocarbon receptor it switches on, which he treats as a light antenna. |
| Fine-structure constant (about 1/137) | The number that sets how strongly light grips matter; he says structured water tightens that grip and deuterium loosens it. |
| Flash-wash | His term for sugar's quick burst of light protons used to clear deuterium in an emergency; fat is the long-game fuel. |
| Follicular fluid | The fluid surrounding a developing egg; he says gut deuterium can be sent into it. |
| Four-phase sun day | His daily sequence: sunrise red/infrared, mid-morning UV-A, brief midday UV-B, sunset red/infrared. |
| Fourth ventricle | A fluid space in the brainstem where he says the body filters deuterium. |
| FOXO3 | A gene common in very long-lived people that can pause cell division to protect the genome. |
| Fraunhofer lines | Narrow dark gaps in the sun's spectrum, which he reads as a solar "barcode". |
| Frosted lens | His image of heavy water under the retina scattering light before melanin can convert it. |
| G3P shuttle | The glycerol-3-phosphate side route that passes electrons past Complex I, leaving heavy hydrogen behind. |
| Gastroparesis | A stalled stomach that empties too slowly. |
| GDF15 / mitokine storm | A stress signal from struggling mitochondria / a surge of it, which he links to fatigue and later cancer. |
| Geodynamo | The churning molten iron in Earth's core that generates the planet's magnetic field. |
| Geological ark | His name for El Salvador, whose volcanic, iron-rich geology he says keeps the field flat and stable. |
| Geomagnetic jerk | A sudden lurch in how fast the field is changing, which he says can tilt a local field within a year. |
| GLP-1 drugs | Weight-loss injections such as semaglutide, which he says force a crude deuterium purge and damage gut and stem cells. |
| Glucagon gene network | A gene cluster on chromosome 2, switched on at sunrise, that he says runs the bicarbonate flush and makes GLP-1. |
| Glutathione | The cell's main internal antioxidant, which he says acetaminophen and isolated ascorbic acid drain. |
| Glycation | Sugars bonding to proteins or amino acids without an enzyme; he ties it to deuterium and tissue "sludge.". |
| Glycerol-3-phosphate (G3P) shunt | A side entrance for electrons that bypasses a jammed Complex I, trading voltage for survival ("slow and foggy rather than fast and dead"). |
| Glycerol-3-phosphate shuttle | A back-door route carrying light hydrogen into mitochondria, which he says runs during insulin resistance and leaves deuterium behind. |
| Glycolysis | Breaking down sugar without mitochondria (fermentation). |
| Glymphatic system | The brain's waste-clearing fluid flow, most active in deep sleep. |
| Grid cells / place cells | Brain cells that map where you are, which he says depend on field-anchored structured water. |
| Ground fault (SAA) | His description of the South Atlantic Anomaly as a break in the ocean's electrical circuit that stalls the Atlantic current. |
| Grounding (earthing) | Bare skin contact with earth, sand, rock or sea, or a low-resistance indoor connection, letting the body share the earth's charge. |
| H lineage | His own northern European haplogroup, whose "leaky" mitochondria he says shed heat rather than couple tightly. |
| Hantavirus | A virus family; he reads the Andes strain's person-to-person spread as an adaptation to a degraded environment. |
| Haplogroup | A maternal lineage traced through mitochondrial DNA (e.g. L1, H, D5b1, F); he calls it your blueprint, to be matched to location. |
| Heme | The iron-holding core of hemoglobin and the cytochromes, which he calls the body's magnetic antenna. |
| Heme clock | Light-timed heme production that switches off the liver's sugar output at night. |
| Hesperidin ((2S)-hesperidin) | A specific form of a citrus compound he times with morning sun exposure in his rescue protocols. |
| Heteroplasmy | The share of damaged mitochondrial DNA in a cell. |
| Homocysteine | An amino-acid by-product he reads as a gauge of blood "sludge," deuterium load and heart risk. |
| Homocysteine "check engine light" | Rising homocysteine as his sign that light-dependent B vitamins and the interior tan are failing. |
| Horvath clock | A test estimating "biological age" from DNA marks, which he says not to rely on. |
| HRV (heart-rate variability) | Beat-to-beat variation in heart timing; deep-sleep HRV is his main progress gauge, rigid when lattice-locked and flexible when free. |
| Human Lagrangian | His proposed equation linking field strength, melanin and solar dose to the ratio of light to heavy hydrogen a cell should hold at a given place. |
| Hunger mirror | Hunger driven by a deuterium-jammed energy motor that the brain misreads as famine. |
| Hyaluronic acid | The slippery gel between tissue layers, which he says deuterium stiffens so layers grind. |
| Hydraulic failure | His umbrella term for reflux, polyps and colon cancer as one backed-up bicarbonate flush. |
| Hyper-local magnetic excursion | Wi-Fi, smart meters and other wireless devices recreating, around one person, the effect of a planetary field collapse. |
| Hyperfine noise trap | Extra nuclear-spin "noise" (for example from deuterium) that makes a radical-pair magnetic sensor less sensitive to weak fields. |
| Hypertonic saline | Extra-salty saline; he says it helps heart failure by raising the water's dielectric constant. |
| Hypomethylation | Too few methyl tags on DNA; he says deuterium jams the tagging enzymes and erases the timing that builds a three-leaflet valve. |
| Ice-nucleating proteins | Proteins that seed ice, and so rain, at relatively warm temperatures; he cites fungi that release them. |
| IGRF | The International Geomagnetic Reference Field, a standard global field model he says smooths away local changes. |
| Inclination (dip) | The angle at which field lines dip into the ground; he wants 0–45°, meaning fairly flat lines. |
| Inductance–capacitance (LC) circuit | The simplest tuned antenna; how he explains an open copper ring gathering and concentrating natural frequencies. |
| Induction zone | An active fault zone (such as New Madrid) that gives off electromagnetic signals under stress and makes the local field spiky. |
| Ink without the book | His image for a vitamin D pill: the product without the light-run code that regulates it. |
| Inner mitochondrial membrane | The folded inner wall of the mitochondrion, where the energy chain sits and voltage is held; its charge is his key health variable. |
| Input / exhaust | Field lines gathering toward the north ("input") and spreading apart over the south ("exhaust"), which he says leaves the Southern Hemisphere more exposed. |
| Insulin resistance | Cells resisting insulin; to him a protective shield, not a defect. |
| Insulin-resistant type 1s | Type 1 diabetics whose cells are so loaded with heavy water that injected insulin no longer works well. |
| Interior tan | Melanin and structured water deep inside the body, which he says matter more than skin tan. |
| Internal rigor mortis | Deuterium pulling the far-from-balance ATP synthase motor toward a settled, lifeless state. |
| Intersystem crossing | A forced spin flip in the energy chain that turns safe triplet oxygen into singlet oxygen. |
| Intrinsic cardiac nervous system | The heart's own "little brain" of about 40,000 neurons, which he says senses blood thickness and deuterium. |
| Intussusception | The bowel telescoping into itself; he calls it a deuterium motor stall. |
| Ion cyclotron resonance | Liboff and Blackman's idea that an AC field riding on Earth's field can match calcium's natural frequency and push it out of place. |
| Isotope centrifuge | The many steps of glycolysis and the Krebs cycle sorting light hydrogen from deuterium. |
| Isotope jam (beta-cell fatigue) | His reading of type 2 diabetes: beta cells full of insulin but too deuterium-jammed to release it. |
| Isotope mass gate | Complex IV making water that excludes deuterium. |
| Isotope shield | Structured water keeping deuterium away from the nucleus and DNA. |
| Isotopic backwash | The bicarbonate flush stalling and pushing heavy water back into the gut lining. |
| Isotopic firewall | His term for how vagotomy blocked deuterium from backing up from gut to brain. |
| Isotopic flashover | A deuterium nucleus bridging the membrane's huge electric field and shorting it. |
| Isotopic gearbox | A mitochondrial lineage seen as a fixed gear ratio for ATP synthase, matched to the field and sun where it arose. |
| Isotopic hangover | The fall in melatonin 15–33 hours after a geomagnetic spike. |
| Isotopic insufflation | Deuterium and tritium drawn down from the upper atmosphere into water and food where the field is weak. |
| Isotopic ossification | His term for an infant brain that fails to prune connections and lay down myelin under deuterium load. |
| Isotopic Viscosity Syndrome | His proposed name for major depression: a brain whose fluid has thickened with deuterium. |
| Janitor (field as) | His image of a healthy field sweeping deuterium to the edges of the body's flows so it can be cleared. |
| Jugular foramen | Opening at the skull base where the vagus nerve leaves alongside the jugular vein. |
| Karst | Limestone riddled with water channels; he says saltwater-soaked karst carries electrical noise for miles, a "voltage siphon.". |
| Kinetic isotope effect | Heavier isotopes reacting more slowly; he says deuterium slows energy-chain and fat-burning enzymes two- to sevenfold. |
| L-DOPA | The standard Parkinson's drug; he says it only treats symptoms. |
| Lactated Ringer's | An IV fluid he calls the hospital's "stealth" deuterium-depleting drip. |
| Lambert–Eaton myasthenic syndrome | A muscle-weakness disorder with antibodies to voltage-gated calcium channels; his "astronaut on Earth" syndrome. |
| Landauer erasure / Landauer liquidation | Clearing corrupted information, such as a misfolded protein, which must release heat / stored order lost as heat on a large scale, draining ATP and NAD⁺. |
| Landauer tax | The extra heat cost of each thought when a weak outside field gives the brain's fluid vortex no lift. |
| Larmor radius | The circle an electron traces in a magnetic field; he says it widens as the field weakens until tunneling fails. |
| Laschamp excursion | The excursion about 41,000 years ago when the field fell below 5% of normal; his model for today's decline. |
| LAT1 | The transporter that carries large amino acids into the brain; he says a phenylalanine and tyrosine backlog crowds it. |
| Lattice janitor | His name for ubiquitin, which he says tags deuterium-loaded proteins for replacement. |
| Lattice lock | Stiff, deuterium-loaded body water with a jammed energy motor and a cell blind to sunlight. |
| Lenard effect | Charge separation when water splashes or waves break, filling shore air with negative ions and, he says, restructuring water. |
| Leptin | The fat hormone that reports stored energy to the brain; he calls it the master photoelectric transducer and its steady signal a "carrier wave.". |
| Levophed | The hospital brand of norepinephrine, a catecholamine he says revived apparently dead ICU patients. |
| Lewy bodies | Clumps of misfolded protein in Parkinson's, which he calls corrupted data the cell can't clear. |
| Light ferry boats | His phrase for hormones such as ACTH carrying light and magnetic information, not just chemistry. |
| Light hydrogen | Ordinary hydrogen, without deuterium's extra neutron. |
| Lipofuscin (rusted melanin) | Melanin degraded into debris that he says clogs the brainstem's sleep centers. |
| Lithium-6 / lithium-7 | The two natural lithium isotopes; he says lithium-6 holds quantum coherence longer and acts as a dopant for the vagus. |
| Lithospheric straightener | Magnetized basalt bending a tilting field back to horizontal at ground level. |
| Local stator rule | His rule that the rock underfoot, not global averages, decides the field a body actually experiences. |
| Lorentz force | The push a magnetic field gives to moving charged particles, such as those in salty seawater. |
| Lp(a) | A cholesterol particle he sees as an emergency patch for a failing vessel wall. |
| Lumbo-sacral de-frag | His term for female ejaculation as a flush of deuterium from the lower spine and pelvic nerves. |
| Lumisterol / tachysterol | By-products that sun-made vitamin D turns into once enough is made, his reason it can't be overdosed. |
| Magnesium-25 | A form of magnesium with a magnetic nucleus that he says lets the field boost ATP production. |
| Magnetic Arctic flashover | His term for Arctic warming and northward-spreading insects read as a magnetic, not greenhouse, failure. |
| Magnetic buffer tanks | His description of the clock proteins Period and Cryptochrome as charge stores tuned to Earth's field. |
| Magnetic castration | His phrase for shaving a man's beard, which he says removes an antenna anchor. |
| Magnetic chemotherapy | Night-time melatonin made in mitochondria, which he says calms damaged cells and pulls cancer-prone cells back toward normal. |
| Magnetic dam | A strong horizontal field component that holds protons in place; he says it once made parts of the Southern Hemisphere livable. |
| Magnetic de-fragger | His name for rescue tools (epi-pens, dopamine drips, 3% saline boluses) that he says restart a stalled, deuterium-locked system. |
| Magnetic dead zone | A place where the field is too weak or unstable for grounding on grass alone to be enough. |
| Magnetic flatline | The very weak field of the Ediacaran period just before the Cambrian. |
| Magnetic fog | Local iron, basalt and wireless fields at the surface that mask the core's signal in ground readings. |
| Magnetic forge | The field-dominated era he says Earth is entering, like the Laschamp period. |
| Magnetic null | Local and global fields canceling so the body feels almost no field. |
| Magnetic sanctuary | A bedroom that is dark, cold, grounded, wireless-free and aligned north–south. |
| Magnetic stall | A local failure of the field, signalled for example by a humid-region volcano losing its cloud cap. |
| Magnetic susceptibility | How strongly a material responds to a magnet; he says C4 plant ash raises the soil's. |
| Magnetic zipcode | The magnetic field where you live, which he says must match your haplogroup. |
| Magnetite (titanomagnetite) | Iron-oxide minerals that hold permanent magnetism; he says they lock the field into volcanic rock. |
| Magnetosphere | The protective bubble Earth's field forms around the planet. |
| Maillard oven | A body or place that "browns" tissue from inside, like food browning when cooked, through heat, heavy hydrogen and lost grounding. |
| Maillard-orange shift | Melanin or tissue "browning" when overloaded with deuterium. |
| Mammalian sphinxing | Facing east at sunrise on all fours so hands and feet touch the earth while catching dawn light. |
| Marcus crash | A keto flu that never lifts because fat-derived electrons pile up in a stalled chain. |
| Marcus inversion (inverted region) | From Rudolph Marcus's theory: electron transfer slowing once the driving force passes its optimum, so pushing harder moves fewer electrons. |
| Marcus inversion well | A place or season whose field holds a person in that stalled state. |
| MARPE | A device that widens the upper jaw by splitting the palate's midline. |
| Master centrifuge | His name for the vagus as the spinner that throws deuterium out of nerves and brain chambers. |
| Mastication pump | His term for chewing as a driver of fluid and energy flow. |
| Matrix fire | The chaotic damage inside a stalled mitochondrion: reactive oxygen and scrambled light. |
| Mayan trap | Choosing a place for one factor alone (such as an agonic line) while neglecting light and water. |
| MCTs (C8) | Medium-chain fats that go straight to the liver and must be burned at once, yielding light water and ketones. |
| MDL 3094 | A consolidated federal lawsuit over GLP-1 drugs, mostly about gastroparesis. |
| Mechanical dither | Regular earthquake shaking that, in his account, clears background noise so clock genes can pick up Earth's rhythm. |
| Mechanical magnetism | His phrase for analog sound pressure (drums, gongs, bowls) helping spin the spinal fluid. |
| Mechanical Warburg shift | Mouth breathing switching off the nose's filtering, by analogy with cells falling back on crude energy production. |
| Meckel's cave | A fluid pocket beside the brainstem holding a trigeminal nerve hub; he says it keeps fourth-ventricle water moving. |
| Melanin | The body's dark pigment, which he treats as a light-harvesting, spin-filtering semiconductor and charge store in skin, eyes, brain and organs. |
| Melanized fungi | Fungi armored with melanin that he says survived past weak-field eras and keep the water cycle going. |
| Melanopsin | A blue-sensing light receptor in the retina that informs the body clock. |
| Melatonin | The night hormone and antioxidant; he says mitochondria also make it, which needs both electric and magnetic containment. |
| Menadione | The step through which injected vitamin K1 is converted to MK-4, using up glutathione in his account. |
| Metabolic syndrome | The cluster of high blood sugar, abnormal lipids and related problems; adult seborrheic dermatitis is a marker of it. |
| Methyl groups | Small chemical tags that switch genes on and off; heavier when deuterium-loaded. |
| Micro-field | A small local field held by nearby rock that can differ from the regional field. |
| Mismatch Freeze | Skin cholesterol stuck in its winter (LDL) format after years of blue light and nnEMF. |
| MITF | The control switch of pigment cells; he reads greying hair as its failure. |
| Mitoception | A cell sensing its own distress through brief singlet-oxygen bursts. |
| Mitochondria | The cell's tiny power units, which he treats as light- and field-driven electrical devices. |
| Mitochondrial DNA leak | Mitochondrial DNA escaping into the cell or blood, which the immune system treats as an alarm. |
| MK-4 | The animal form of vitamin K that manages calcium; he says the mother makes it from K1 and passes it on slowly. |
| Molecular concrete | Rigid bonds between deuterium and singlet oxygen that jam enzymes. |
| Motional induction | The small field produced when salty, conductive waves move over magnetic black sand. |
| MTHFR | A folate-pathway gene whose variants he cites in links to heart outflow defects such as aortic stenosis. |
| mTOR | The cell's build-or-clean-up regulator, which he calls the cell's deuterium sensor, set by 380 nm light. |
| Muons | Heavy cousins of electrons made when cosmic rays hit the air; he says they act as positive dopants that cancel cell charge. |
| Myelomalacia | Softening of the spinal cord, usually seen as permanent; he says it can reverse. |
| Myopia | Short-sightedness; he links its epidemic to eyes losing full-spectrum light. |
| NAD+ | A key carrier that ferries electrons into the energy chain; it can pick up deuterium in place of light hydrogen and lose its buffering role. |
| NAD⁺ / NADH | The cell's main electron carrier, in its charged and loaded forms; it also fuels repair enzymes like SIRT1. |
| NADD+ | His term for NAD⁺ loaded with deuterium. |
| NADH | The loaded, electron-carrying form of NAD⁺. |
| NAION | A stroke of the optic nerve causing vision loss, which he calls the spaceflight eye syndrome produced on Earth. |
| Nanotesla (nT) | The unit of magnetic field strength; Earth's surface field runs roughly 22,000–65,000 nT. |
| Near-infrared (NIR) | Invisible light just past red that reaches deep into tissue; he says it builds structured water. |
| Nephrite / jadeite | The two minerals sold as jade; translucent silicates he says act as light-shifting, piezoelectric "dopants" in teeth. |
| Neural crest | Embryonic cells that give rise to both the great vessels' nerves and the pigment cells that put melanin in them. |
| Neuromelanin | Iron-bound pigment in the brain, which he calls a magnetic battery. |
| Neuropsin (OPN5) | A UV-A light sensor in eye and skin that he treats as the master clock input. |
| NHANES | The US national nutrition survey he cites for flat calorie intake alongside rising obesity. |
| NIRDS (Near-Infrared Deficiency Syndrome) | Too little red and infrared light, or being unable to use it even in full sun when unpinned. |
| Nitric oxide | A gas made in the sinuses that he says keeps blood vessels healthy and shields incoming oxygen. |
| NMN / NR | Supplements sold to raise NAD⁺, which he says push the system rather than repair it. |
| nnEMF (non-native EMF) | His term for man-made electromagnetic fields from wireless devices, wiring and electronics. |
| nnEMF multiplier | His term for the newborn vitamin K shot amplifying the harm of a hospital's wireless fields. |
| Nocturnal Hall current inversion (polarity flip) | The nightly reversal of current in melanin and the brain that he says purges deuterium. |
| Nocturnal mouse fallacy | His objection to modeling day-active humans on night-active rodents. |
| Nodes of Ranvier | Gaps in a nerve's insulation where signals jump, where he says MS begins. |
| Non-ionizing radiation | Radiation too weak to break atoms apart, such as radio and wireless; he rejects the idea that this makes it harmless. |
| North-seeking pad | A static magnetic sleep pad with the north-seeking pole facing the body, which he says calms and organizes electron spins. |
| Obex | The narrow point where the fourth ventricle opens into the spinal canal; he says deuterium drains through it in ALS. |
| Occiput | The back of the skull where it meets the neck; his spot for the 40 Hz fork over the fourth ventricle. |
| Oculocardiac reflex | Pressure on the eye slowing the heart; the basis of his "piezo-vagal reset" by eye rubbing. |
| Ohmic heating | Heat released by electrical resistance; here, heat from a failing dynamo's currents. |
| Optically pumped magnetometer | A sensitive laser-based field sensor he names as the better ground instrument. |
| Optico-Magnetic Engineering | His name for the shared light-and-field physics he sees in the Sun and the mitochondrion. |
| Orch-OR | Hameroff and Penrose's theory placing consciousness in microtubules, which he rejects in favor of water. |
| Ortho- / para-water | Water whose hydrogen nuclei spin in step ("grease," his 78 state) / whose spins cancel ("superconductor," his 160 state). |
| Osteophyte / magnetic tafoni | A bone spur, which he reads as a calcified shadow of magnetic turbulence where deuterium stalled. |
| Oxidation field | The layer of hydroxyl radicals the skin makes at its surface, which lotions smother. |
| OXPHOS | A lab assay of mitochondrial energy enzymes, which he reads as a map of your location's field. |
| p53 | The genome's guardian protein, triggering repair, cell retirement or cell death. |
| PANS/PANDAS | Sudden childhood tics, rage or OCD often linked to strep, which he blames on a deuterium-loaded terrain. |
| Paramagnetic | Weakly drawn to a magnet because of unpaired electrons; oxygen, wet DNA, nitric oxide and basalt are examples. |
| Paramagnetic gate | Complex IV's field-backed checkpoint matching incoming electrons to oxygen's spin. |
| Paramagnetic lock | Membranes, cholesterol rafts and structured water ordered enough to use light and keep oxygen stable. |
| Parrots and eagles | His split between thinkers who repeat isolated mechanisms and those who see the whole system. |
| Pecten oculi | A melanin-packed comb of blood vessels inside a bird's eye that he calls a "photonic heat sink" shielding its magnetic compass. |
| PEMF | Pulsed electromagnetic field devices, which he calls the "loser option" next to grounding on basalt. |
| Peripheral artery disease (PAD) | Narrowing of arteries outside the heart, which he says starts with deuterated elastin. |
| Personal Quantum Solvency | Accepting that pills, food and exercise can't fix a field problem, and acting on location, light and grounding. |
| Phase separation | Proteins dropping out of solution into separate clumps, which he links to spinal scarring. |
| Phased-array radar | His image for hair and skin as an antenna system tuned to the planet's field. |
| Photonic grammar | His name for the coded pattern ozone and the sun's gaps carve into sunlight, which lets the vitamin D receptor act as a brake. |
| Photonic purge | Morning near-infrared knocking nitric oxide off Complex IV so oxygen can enter. |
| Piezoelectric | Producing a small electric current when squeezed or bent, as bone, collagen and tendon do. |
| Pin first | His order of repair: fix the magnetic and wireless environment first, add morning light second, change diet or exercise last. |
| Pinned / unpinned | Living grounded and sunlit under a stable, mostly horizontal local field that keeps electron spins aligned / lacking it. |
| Placental stator | His term for the placenta acting as the fetus's magnetic ground. |
| Polymorphous light eruption | A spring sun rash, which he calls sun allergy and reads as a deuterium flush. |
| POMC / alpha-MSH | A hormone precursor / the signal cut from it that tells skin and hair cells to make melanin. |
| ppm (parts per million) | The unit for deuterium in water; tap water is about 150 ppm in his figures. |
| Pre-glymphatic flush | Humming or drumming before bed to loosen heavy water so deep sleep can clear it. |
| Pregnenolone steal | Hormone building blocks diverted to cortisol instead of sex hormones in a weak field. |
| Primary water | Deep "new" water from volcanic fissures that never evaporated, low in deuterium, which he says helps rebuild structured water. |
| Prolotherapy | Injections meant to provoke tissue repair; he expects them to fail in heavily deuterated skin. |
| Proteasome | The cell's protein shredder. |
| Protomyxzoa rheumatica | A blood parasite he treats as a marker of a collapsed water table rather than a root cause. |
| Proton battery (myelin) | His model of white matter (myelin) storing protons during sleep to power the next day. |
| Protonic capacitor | His image of NAD⁺ as the cell's store of charge, like albumin holding fluid in blood. |
| PSA (prostate-specific antigen) | A protein-cutting enzyme also made by women's Skene's glands; he suggests it clears protein sludge in the pelvis. |
| Pseudohypoxia | A cell behaving as if starved of oxygen even though oxygen is present. |
| Psilocin | The active form of psilocybin; he proposes it works partly by deuterium depletion. |
| Quantum blockchain | His name for the four genes clustered around the chromosome 2 fusion site. |
| Quantum blockers | Hospital monitors and lights that, he says, keep a stalled body from restarting. |
| Quantum bypass | His description of methylene blue skipping steps in the energy chain. |
| Quantum centrifuge | His image for GLP-1 drugs forcing a crude deuterium purge. |
| Quantum de-magnetizers | His name for statins, which he says strip the membrane's charge-holding machinery. |
| Quantum seed | His term for an egg kept largely offline (Complex I suppressed) so it stays quiet and clean for decades. |
| Quantum synchrotron | His image of the mitochondrion, and the Sun, as a low-energy particle accelerator. |
| Quantum vice | Cold squeezing tissue so deuterium cannot get in. |
| Quinolinic acid | Broken-down melanin feeding a pathway that silences BDNF. |
| Racemic epinephrine | A form of adrenaline; he says high-dose dopamine drips act much like it. |
| Radical pair | Two molecules each holding one unpaired electron, whose spin balance a weak magnetic field can tip. |
| Radiotrophic fungi | Melanised black fungi (as at Chernobyl) that turn ionising radiation into metabolic energy. |
| Reality subtraction | Screens and algorithms replacing perception of the real world. |
| Recurrent laryngeal nerve | The vagus branch that runs the voice box, looping under the aorta; he places a "stuck" voice there. |
| Redlined | Cell voltage pushed to its limit after the fat buffer is stripped. |
| Relaxation time (T2) | How long protons take to settle after being disturbed; he says reversed local rock stretches it past a "7.2-second quiet buffer". |
| Renormalization operator | The vagus nerve as the body's exhaust for deuterium and disorder. |
| Respiratory (electron) chain | The series of protein complexes (I–IV) that pass electrons from food to oxygen. |
| Rev-Erbα | A clock protein that needs heme to switch off night-time sugar production and inflammation. |
| Reverse electron flow | Electrons pushed backward through Complex I when saturated fat is burned, which he says tells the cell to ignore sugar. |
| Reverse volcano | His name for a pyramid built to copy a volcano's magnetic and water-ordering effect away from volcanic ground. |
| Rhino skin | His term for thick, heavily deuterated skin. |
| Ring of Fire | The chain of volcanoes around the Pacific, whose dense magnetic pinning he says shaped East Asian lineages. |
| Safe mode | A lower-energy protective state, such as obesity, cancer or atavistic retreat, that he says buys the body time. |
| Salivary D/H | The ratio of deuterium to ordinary hydrogen in saliva; his target is below 130 ppm. |
| SANS | Spaceflight-associated neuro-ocular syndrome: swollen optic-nerve sheaths, flattened eyeballs and enlarged brain fluid spaces. |
| Satellite mode | His term for a developing egg keeping Complex I switched down to keep deuterium out, like a satellite powering down. |
| Schumann resonance | The natural 7.83 Hz electromagnetic hum between the ground and the upper atmosphere. |
| Sebacic acid | A gut compound he says rises with circadian disruption and shifts the microbiome. |
| Second pump (Lorentz force) | The push blood gets from moving through Earth's magnetic field, which he says eases the heart's work. |
| Secular acceleration | The speeding-up of the field's change over time; he says to read these charts for your own zip code. |
| Semiconductive transplant | His term for swapping a deuterium-loaded protein for a fresh one, washed by a burst of light protons from stored sugar. |
| Senescence | A cell's permanent retirement from dividing. |
| Siberian jerk | The fast run of the north magnetic pole toward Siberia, which he says strains the southern weak spot. |
| SIBO | Bacterial overgrowth in the small intestine. |
| Singlet flashover | Overheating of a northern ("leaky") engine under strong UV or radiation, which he links to melanoma and rapid aging. |
| Singlet oxygen | Oxygen with paired spins: reactive, membrane-damaging and diamagnetic, formed when the field fails to keep spins aligned. |
| SIRT1 | A repair enzyme that runs on NAD⁺. |
| Skene's glands | Glands around the urethra (the female counterpart of the prostate) that produce female ejaculate. |
| Smoke alarm (adenosine) | Adenosine left over as the cell strips ATP, which he says signals that the membrane grid is tiring and triggers sleep. |
| Solar callus | Skin and tissue adapted step by step to strong sun, so they tan rather than burn. |
| South Atlantic Anomaly (SAA) | A huge, expanding weak patch in Earth's field over South America and the South Atlantic, below about 22,000 nT. |
| Spaceflight sickness without the rocket | Chronic illness from indoor, ungrounded, wireless-soaked living that mimics orbit. |
| Spatial shear deflector | El Salvador's east–west volcanic front, which he says keeps a weakening field from tilting vertical. |
| Speed bump | His word for a single deuterium strike that interrupts the body's coherent signal. |
| Sphenoid / sphenoid Venturi | The bone at the center of the skull, his brain switchboard / the drain point behind the nose where heavy water should be filtered. |
| Spike protein | The coronavirus protein, which he describes as a charged molecule that short-circuits membranes. |
| Spin barrier | Triplet oxygen's inability to react directly with ordinary molecules, which he says makes aerobic life possible. |
| Spin-phase wrecking ball | Deuterium's nuclear spin drifting out of step with spin-filtering proteins. |
| Spinal lattice lock | Tethered cord and adhesive arachnoiditis treated as one deuterium-driven lock of the spinal sheath. |
| Stator | The fixed part of a motor that sets up its field; his role for Earth's dynamo. |
| Structural time permanence | A slowed, protected cell state that preserves genetic information. |
| Subatomic monkey wrench | Lithium, whose two mismatched isotopes he says jam the thalamus's timing. |
| Sulfated cholesterol (summer antenna) | Sulfur-rich summer cholesterol carried in HDL that catches UV-B; winter LDL lacks it and, he says, signals light stress. |
| Superior mesenteric artery | The main artery to the gut and part of the pancreas's supply; he says it "bleaches" (loses melanin) in diabetes. |
| Superoxide pulses | Brief oxygen-radical signals he calls healthy Morse code, which he says diabetics have exhausted. |
| Swarm (ESA) | The European Space Agency's trio of field-measuring satellites, his main data source. |
| Symmetry of the stone | His phrase for a deuterium-loaded aorta "freezing" into calcified scar to cut its energy costs. |
| Tailpipe trap | Widening an airway or drain while losing the spin of the fluid moving through it. |
| Takotsubo cardiomyopathy | Sudden "broken-heart" ballooning of the heart, which he ties to acute loss of melanin and its hormones. |
| Tanning the interior | His goal for deuterium-depleted water plus 3% saline plus UV: thinning inner fluid so light signals reach deep glands. |
| Tau stage | The point, at 30–50% of a Laschamp-scale collapse, when he expects Earth's core and outer shell to decouple. |
| taVNS | Stimulation of the vagus nerve through the skin of the ear, which he restricts to the left side. |
| Telluric currents | Natural electric currents flowing through Earth's crust, which he says the moon drives. |
| Telomeres | Protective caps on chromosome ends, which he says record entropy rather than age. |
| TENS | A small device sending pulsed current through skin pads; he uses gentle settings, left side only, for the vagus. |
| Term life insurance | UV-B protecting unpinned people by braking the energy chain. |
| Tethered cord (fetal matrix freeze) | A spinal cord anchored too low; to him the inherited, fetal form of spinal lattice lock from parents' deuterium-loaded germline. |
| Thalamic GPS | The thalamus as a three-axis position panel (sphenoid, vagus, heart) locked to the field and the Schumann resonance. |
| Thermostat (vitamin D) | The self-limiting cutoff in skin that he says makes sun-made vitamin D impossible to overdose. |
| Third ventricle | The fluid chamber beside the hypothalamus; heavy with deuterium it slows thyroid repair. |
| Thixotropic | Thinning as it moves; his description of blood and spinal fluid. |
| Three turns sense, seven turns resonate | His rule that a seven-turn spiral pushes nearby water toward the structured 160 state. |
| Time compression | Days blurring together, which he reads as a stalled thalamus. |
| Time thieves | His term for health influencers who sell dependency on products and regimens. |
| Time-stamping | The daily clock deciding when proteins receive their chemical tags. |
| TMAO | A gut-bacteria by-product he reads, with homocysteine, as a "silt" marker of a failing deuterium flush. |
| TMS | Transcranial magnetic stimulation; he calls Maya jade inlays an ancient version. |
| Topological insulator | A material that blocks current through its bulk but conducts along its surface; his model of the inner membrane. |
| Topological insurance | Marcus inversion acting as a built-in circuit breaker against thermal meltdown. |
| Total intensity | The overall strength of the field at a place; he wants above about 45,000 nT. |
| Transsulfuration | The sulfur pathway that turns homocysteine into cysteine, the building block of glutathione. |
| TRCS | The Telomere/rDNA Co-Regulation model: shortening of telomeres and DNA repeats drives aging through p53. |
| Trigeminal neuralgia | Severe facial nerve pain; he links right-sided cases to slow, deuterium-heavy right-side drainage. |
| Triplet oxygen | Ordinary oxygen with parallel spins, paramagnetic and safe; a good field and grounding keep it this way. |
| Tritium | Radioactive hydrogen, which he says cosmic rays make under the SAA and which damages DNA from inside. |
| Truncal vagotomy | An old ulcer operation cutting the main trunks of the vagus nerve; he says it lowered Parkinson's risk. |
| TSH | Thyroid-stimulating hormone. |
| Tunnel diode | An electronic part in which raising voltage lowers current, his analogy for Marcus inversion. |
| Tunneling | A particle slipping through an energy barrier it could not climb over; deuterium pays a heavy "tax" to do it. |
| Ubiquitin | A small protein "delete" label that marks other proteins for shredding. |
| UCP-2 | A gate in the inner membrane that he says leaks deuterium-rich water inward when cell voltage falls. |
| Umbilical castor capacitor | Castor oil in the navel at night, which he says sends a small current up the vagus. |
| Uncoupled halide storm | Isolated or high-dose iodine turning into damaging radicals in an unpinned body. |
| Uncoupling | Letting some mitochondrial energy leak away as heat instead of storing it, as he says northern lineages do. |
| Unfolded-protein response | The alarm raised when proteins fold badly; his cell "error log.". |
| Universal Stator | His term for a magnetic field that he says organizes living systems from the cosmos down to the cell. |
| UPE (ultra-weak photon emission) | The faint light cells give off, which he says turns chaotic under stress and quiet and coherent in health. |
| UV lighthouse | His image for a jade-inlaid tooth whose steadied faint light kills microbes. |
| UV-A / UV-B | The gentler ultraviolet of dawn, which he treats as the master clock signal / the stronger midday ultraviolet that makes vitamin D in skin. |
| Vagal brake | Calming the stress response through the vagus, for example with a 128 Hz fork on the sternum in the evening. |
| Vagal vortex | The neck region where the carotid arteries, vagus nerve and structured water run together. |
| Vagotomy | Surgical cutting of the vagus nerve, once used for ulcers; he reads its after-effects as deuterium backflow. |
| Vagus nerve | The long nerve from brainstem to heart, lungs and gut, which he treats as the body's deuterium exhaust cable. |
| Van Allen radiation belts | Rings of trapped radiation around Earth, which he says are sagging lower as the field weakens. |
| Ventricles (brain) | The brain's central fluid chambers, where he places a deuterium-separating vortex. |
| Vestibular migraine | Migraine with dizziness; he suggests a jade test to show when it is "solid-state.". |
| Viscosity | How thick and slow-flowing a fluid is. |
| Volcano Protocol | His routine of carbonated water with DDW, sauna, a spinal water jet with a 40 Hz fork, and sunrise grounding on pinned volcanic ground. |
| Volcano Rx | Living on, and grounding barefoot on, magnetic volcanic rock. |
| Vortex jammer | His term for digital speakers, whose pulsed fields and hum disturb the spinal-fluid spiral. |
| Wall shear stress | The frictional drag of flowing blood on the vessel lining, which he says a centred vortex keeps to a minimum. |
| Warburg shift | Cells falling back on fermentation instead of mitochondrial burning, which he treats as an ancient survival mode. |
| Water battery | The cell's charge-holding structured water. |
| Welded exhaust pipe | His mechanic's image for a blocked bicarbonate loop and vagal drain that lets deuterium back up. |
| World Magnetic Model | The official global field map used for navigation, whose numbers he uses to choose places to live. |
| Worse antenna | Nerve, pigment and skin tissue shrinking so the body picks up less electromagnetic noise. |
| Zeeman effect | The way a magnetic field splits electron energy levels; he says grounding restores it in heme. |
| Zeeman handshake | Earth's field keeping the CISS spin filter and oxygen's spins aligned. |
| Zeeman outsourcing | Northern peoples losing skin melanin and relying on strong polar field lines for spin control instead. |
| Zero-point energy | The minimum vibration energy of a chemical bond, lower for deuterium so its bonds are harder to break. |
| Zone 2 | Steady, moderate-intensity endurance exercise, which he says not to start until a person is pinned. |
